We also give a general criterion for constructing such examples and record a separate pendant-arm transfer construction.

\paragraph{Status and scope.}
These are ordinary mathematical proofs with reproducible exact-integer checks, not formal Lean verification or external peer review. The result resolves the one-shore-activity question in the inspected ProveIt Hall-bottleneck report negatively. It does not produce a unit-weight counterexample, identify the smallest possible rank, or assert exhaustive literature priority.
\mnote{The question is Research question~85 of \prp{} (\path{hb:q:oneshore}), with exactly these hypotheses: all left activities one, right activities arbitrary positive. The weighting here, activity $w$ on the right core $B$ and one elsewhere, is the ``one common right-core activity'' of Section~\ref{mrn:sec:notions}. The unit-weight counterexample is source~71's (Section~\ref{mrn:src:71}).}

\subsection{The polynomial and two explicit counterexamples}
For a bipartite graph $G=(X,Y;E)$ with positive vertex activities $u_x,v_y$, define
\[
 P_G(t)=\sum_{(I,J)\text{ feasible}}u_Iv_Jt^{|I|}=\sum_{k=0}^{d}p_kt^k.
\]
Here $I\subseteq X$, $J\subseteq Y$, $|I|=|J|$, and feasibility means that $G[I,J]$ admits a perfect matching. Each pair of endpoint sets is counted once, regardless of the number of realizing matchings. The degree $d$ is the maximum matching size. This is the vertex-weighted perfectly matchable set polynomial, with $t$ recording half the support size.

The property $\ULC_d$ requires log-concavity of $p_k/\binom dk$ and no internal zeros. For these positive-activity support polynomials there are no internal zeros, so the inequalities are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:gap}
 \gap_k^{(d)}=k(d-k)p_k^2-(k+1)(d-k+1)p_{k-1}p_{k+1}\ge0.
\end{equation}
In particular, the last inequality is $(d-1)p_{d-1}^2\ge2dp_{d-2}p_d$.

Write $H_{a,b;n,m}$ for the graph with shores $X=A\sqcup L$, $Y=B\sqcup R$, block sizes $a,n,b,m$, and exactly the edges
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:edges}
 (A\times B)\cup(L\times B)\cup(A\times R).
\end{equation}
All exterior-left vertices are adjacent to all of $B$, all exterior-right vertices to all of $A$, and there are no exterior-to-exterior edges. Give every vertex activity one except the $b$ vertices of $B$, each of which has activity $w>0$. Thus the weighting is confined to one shore. If $n\ge b$ and $m\ge a$, the matching rank is $d=a+b$: the core is a cover of size $d$, attained by disjoint matchings $A\to R$ and $L\to B$.

\begin{theorem}[A counterexample on 125 vertices]\label{mrn:u:os:thm:smallgraph}
Let $G=H_{8,42;44,31}$, with activity $w$ on $B$ and one elsewhere. Its matching rank is $50$, and
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:cleanwitness}
 \gap_{49}^{(50)}
 =98\left[\binom{44}{42}\binom{31}{7}\right]^2
       w^{82}\bigl(-w^2+5958w+421173\bigr).
\end{equation}
At $w=10000$ the parenthesized integer is $-39998827<0$. The first positive integer $w$ for which this particular margin fails is $6028$, where the integer is $-787$.
\end{theorem}
This graph has 125 vertices and 2432 edges. Only 42 activities differ from one. The theorem is an exact certificate against universal one-shore $\ULC_d$.

\begin{theorem}[A counterexample of matching rank 38]\label{mrn:u:os:thm:smallrank}
Let $G=H_{8,30;32,1000}$ with the same one-shore weighting. Put
\[
 Q(w)=-2404w^2+4878807600w+10888224221475.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:rank38}
 \gap_{37}^{(38)}
 =\frac{3}{2272}\left[\binom{32}{30}\binom{1000}{7}\right]^2w^{58}Q(w).
\end{equation}
At $w=3000000$, one has $Q(w)=-6988688975778525<0$. The first positive integer giving a negative value of this particular margin is $2031684$.
\end{theorem}
No minimal-rank claim is made. The rank-38 example has 1070 vertices; the rank-50 example is a substantially smaller graph.
\mnote{Within full Hall graphs with one common right-core activity, source~55 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:55}) proves that $38$ is the least matching number at which the last gap can fail, with $864$ vertices the fewest at that rank, and source~47 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:47}) extends this to every gap.}

\subsection{Endpoint counting gives the complete certificate}
A size-$k$ support selecting $i$ vertices of $A$ and $j$ vertices of $B$ is feasible exactly when $i+j\ge k$. Necessity follows because the $k-i$ selected exterior-left vertices must match into the $j$ selected vertices of $B$. For sufficiency, first match the exterior-left vertices into $B$, then the exterior-right vertices into $A$, and finally the remaining core vertices to each other. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:fullsum}
 p_k=\sum_{\substack{0\le i\le a,\ 0\le j\le b\\i+j\ge k}}
      \binom ai\binom n{k-i}\binom bj\binom m{k-j}w^j,
\end{equation}
with out-of-range binomial coefficients set to zero.

\begin{lemma}[The top three coefficients]\label{mrn:u:os:lem:top}
Let $a,b\ge2$, $d=a+b$, $n\ge b$, $m\ge a$, and set
\[
 X_i=\binom n{b-i},\qquad Y_i=\binom m{a-i}\qquad(i=0,1,2).
\]
Then
\begin{align}
 p_d&=X_0Y_0w^b,\label{mrn:u:os:eq:pd}\\
 p_{d-1}&=(X_1+aX_0)Y_1w^b+bX_1Y_0w^{b-1},\label{mrn:u:os:eq:pd1}\\
 p_{d-2}&=\left(X_2+aX_1+\binom a2X_0\right)Y_2w^b\notag\\
 &\qquad+b(X_2+aX_1)Y_1w^{b-1}+\binom b2X_2Y_0w^{b-2}.\label{mrn:u:os:eq:pd2}
\end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At $k=d$, the condition $i+j\ge d$ forces $(i,j)=(a,b)$. At $k=d-1$, the possible deficits $(a-i,b-j)$ are $(0,0),(1,0),(0,1)$. At $k=d-2$, these and $(2,0),(1,1),(0,2)$ are possible. Substitution in~\eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:fullsum} gives the formulas.
\end{proof}

For a compact parametrization write
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:parameters}
 h=n-b+1,\quad t=\frac bh,\quad z=\frac{b(b-1)}{h(h+1)},\quad
 R=\frac{m-a+1}{a},\quad \rho=\frac{a-1}{m-a+2},\quad
 J=\binom a2+at+z.
\end{equation}
Thus $t=X_1/X_0$, $z=X_2/X_0$, $R=Y_0/Y_1$ and $\rho=Y_2/Y_1$. Put $D=X_0Y_1>0$.

\begin{proposition}[Quadratic endpoint criterion]\label{mrn:u:os:prop:quadratic}
For $a,b\ge2$ and the above parameters,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:quadratic}
 \gap_{d-1}^{(d)}=D^2w^{2b-2}(\alpha w^2+\beta w+\gamma),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
 \alpha&=(d-1)(a+t)^2-2dR\rho J,\label{mrn:u:os:eq:alpha}\\
 \beta&=2bR\bigl((d-1)t(a+t)-d(at+z)\bigr),\label{mrn:u:os:eq:beta}\\
 \gamma&=bR^2\bigl(b(d-1)t^2-d(b-1)z\bigr).\label{mrn:u:os:eq:gamma}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Divide the three identities in Lemma~\ref{mrn:u:os:lem:top} by $D$ and substitute them into $(d-1)p_{d-1}^2-2dp_{d-2}p_d$. Grouping the three powers $w^{2b},w^{2b-1},w^{2b-2}$ yields the displayed coefficients.
\end{proof}
For Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:os:thm:smallgraph}, formulas~\eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:alpha}--\eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:gamma} give
\[
 (\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=98(-1,5958,421173).
\]
For Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:os:thm:smallrank}, they give
\[
 (\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=\frac{3}{2272}
 (-2404,4878807600,10888224221475).
\]
This proves both exact factorizations. Each primitive quadratic has a negative leading coefficient and a positive constant term, so it has precisely one positive zero. Direct substitution at the stated first integer and the preceding integer proves the threshold claims; the preceding values are respectively $5310$ and $1792188719$.

\subsection{A general construction criterion}
For fixed $a,b,h$ the leading coefficient~\eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:alpha} tends, as $m\to\infty$, to
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:limit}
 \alpha_\infty=(d-1)(a+t)^2-
       \frac{2d(a-1)}a\left(\binom a2+at+z\right).
\end{equation}
This is only a device for finding examples; the preceding theorems use finite exact identities.

\begin{proposition}[From a negative limiting coefficient to a finite graph]\label{mrn:u:os:prop:finite}
If $\alpha_\infty<0$, set
\[
 C=\frac{2d(a-1)}a\left(\binom a2+at+z\right),\qquad
 m=a-1+\left\lfloor\frac{C}{-\alpha_\infty}\right\rfloor.
\]
Then $m\ge a$ and $\alpha<0$. With $\beta,\gamma$ defined by the finite parameters, the positive integer
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:weightbound}
 w=1+\left\lfloor\frac{|\beta|+|\gamma|}{-\alpha}\right\rfloor
\end{equation}
necessarily makes the last margin negative.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
One has $\alpha=\alpha_\infty+C/(m-a+2)$. The chosen denominator is $1+\lfloor C/(-\alpha_\infty)\rfloor>C/(-\alpha_\infty)$, so $\alpha<0$. Also $C> -\alpha_\infty$, since their difference is $(d-1)(a+t)^2>0$, proving $m\ge a$. For~\eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:weightbound}, $(-\alpha)w>|\beta|+|\gamma|$ and $w\ge1$, hence
\[
 \alpha w^2+\beta w+\gamma
 \le -(-\alpha)w^2+|\beta|w+|\gamma|<0.
\]
\end{proof}
For $a=8$, $b=30$, $h=3$ (thus $n=32$),
\[
 \alpha_\infty=-\frac{61}{4},\qquad
 \alpha=\frac{48379-61m}{4(m-6)}.
\]
The leading coefficient is negative exactly when $m\ge794$. Choosing $m=1000$ gives Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:os:thm:smallrank}. The alternative $a=7$, $b=31$, $h=3$ also has rank 38 and gives $\alpha_\infty=-764/63$. These are construction examples, not a proof of any global optimum.

\subsection{What this settles and what remains open}
The inspected ProveIt Hall-bottleneck report~\cite{b:HallMS} distinguishes the arbitrary-two-shore weighted failure from its unit-weight and one-shore variants. Theorems~\ref{mrn:u:os:thm:smallgraph} and~\ref{mrn:u:os:thm:smallrank} supply a negative answer to the one-shore variant, even inside the full two-step Ferrers family~\eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:edges}. Since a degree-$d$ polynomial with positive coefficients and only real zeros satisfies Newton's inequalities with normalization $d$, these examples also fail positive-field real-rootedness.

There is no conflict with the ambient-shore normalization in the bimatroid theorem of R\"ohrle and Ulirsch~\cite{b:RU}. Nor does the current non-nesting-rook result of Alexandersson and Jal settle the stronger normalization used here: Corollary~4.1 of version~3 explicitly normalizes by the board's row count~\cite{b:AJ}. Our two examples have smaller shore sizes 52 and 40, respectively, while their matching ranks are 50 and 38. The older version's statement should not be substituted for that current row-normalized theorem.

The full-Hall constructions here have nonunit activities. Further concrete questions include the smallest rank admitting one-shore failure, the smallest graph admitting such failure, and which fixed full-Hall core shapes remain universally rank-ULC under one-shore activities.
\mnote{Re-scoped by the batch: for one common right-core activity on full Hall graphs the smallest failing rank is exactly $38$ (sources~55 and~47); among all one-shore graphs it lies in $[6,38]$, since Part~I gives $\ULC_\nu$ for $\nu\le5$.} The present calculation also suggests studying the sign of~\eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:limit} systematically; its negativity is sufficient for a counterexample, not asserted necessary for every one-shore failure.

\paragraph{Reproducibility.}
The accompanying script \texttt{verify\_full\_hall.py} \mnote{shipped as \path{code/73-one-shore-verify_full_hall.py}, output \path{data/73-one-shore-full_hall_verification.json}} evaluates the complete feasibility sum~\eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:fullsum}, checks it independently against Boolean matching on six small graphs, and compares the top-three formula on 100 deterministic parameter samples. It then verifies both factorizations at four stated integer activities using exact integers and rational numbers. For all four instances, the only negative order-$d$ Newton margin is the last one. These finite checks supplement the proof; they are not its logical basis.

%% (source appendix follows)

%% --- inlined from source 73 file pendant_appendix.tex ---
\subsection{Appendix: A pendant gadget that transfers fields between shores}
\label{mrn:u:os:sec:pendant-transfer}
The direct Hall counterexamples above give much smaller matching rank.
The following independent construction gives a one-shore counterexample
whose only nonunit activity is the modest integer $21$. It also explains
how a weighted obstruction can survive a transfer of fields between shores.

\mnote{Lemma~\ref{mrn:u:os:lem:pendant-identity} contains source~71's Lemma~\ref{mrn:u:uw:lem:pendant} as the case $T=1$ (shores exchanged); it is printed here once for both sources.}
\begin{lemma}[Exact pendant-arm identity]\label{mrn:u:os:lem:pendant-identity}
Let $x$ be a left vertex of activity one in a bipartite graph, and write
$p_G(t)=f(t)+g(t)$, where $f$ counts supports omitting $x$ and $g$ counts
supports containing $x$. Attach $k\ge1$ disjoint length-two arms
$x-z_i-\ell_i$. Give the new right vertices $z_i$ activity $T>0$ and the
new left leaves $\ell_i$ activity one. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:pendant-identity}
 p_{G'}(t)=(1+Tt)^{k-1}
 \bigl[(1+(k+1)Tt)f(t)+(1+Tt)g(t)\bigr].
\end{equation}
Moreover, $\nu(G')=\nu(G)+k$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix a support counted by $g$, so $x$ must match within the original graph.
Each selected new center then forces its own leaf, contributing
$(1+Tt)^k g$.
For a support counted by $f$, choose $j$ new centers. Either all their
leaves are selected and $x$ is absent, or $x$ replaces exactly one of those
$j$ leaves. The latter support forces $x$ to that leaf's center, so it
cannot also use $x$ in the original matching. These $j+1$ endpoint sets
are distinct. Their total contribution is
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^k\binom kj(j+1)(Tt)^j f
 =(1+Tt)^{k-1}(1+(k+1)Tt)f.
\]
This counts supports, not allocations or perfect matchings.
An original maximum matching together with all $k$ center--leaf edges
has size $\nu(G)+k$. An original minimum vertex cover together with the
$k$ centers covers the augmented graph, giving the reverse inequality.
\end{proof}

The top power of $T$ in \eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:pendant-identity} is
\[
 T^kt^k\bigl((k+1)f+g\bigr).
\]
Thus the forced-center limit simulates activity $1/(k+1)$ at $x$, up to
a positive scalar and the degree shift $k$. For gadgets at several left
vertices, these factors multiply. The rank shift must be accounted for:
a polynomial $t^Kp(t)$ of degree $r+K$ has last normalized inequality
\[
 (r+K-1)p_{r-1}^2\ge2(r+K)p_{r-2}p_r.
\]
In particular, a top-three ratio strictly below $2$ survives every such
shift, and continuity then gives a finite positive center activity.
This observation is a sufficient criterion; the finite example below is
checked by its exact endpoint polynomial.

\subsubsection{A rank 310 example with activity 21}
\mnote{This example is one-shore weighted, not unit weighted: its ten right-core vertices have activity $21$. Its rank is superseded by the rank-$50$ and rank-$38$ examples above; it is kept for the transfer construction.}
Start with a full $20\times10$ Hall core, with exterior populations
$N=14$ on the left and $M=30$ on the right. Attach $20$ pendant arms to
each exterior-left vertex. Give all left vertices activity one. Give the
ten original right-core vertices activity $21$, and every other right
vertex activity one, including every pendant center.
There are $K=280$ arms, so the graph has
\[
 634\text{ vertices},\qquad1500\text{ edges},\qquad\nu(G)=30+280=310.
\]
For completeness, the original $30$-edge matching pairs the twenty
left-core vertices to distinct exterior-right vertices and the ten
right-core vertices to distinct exterior-left vertices. Add all $280$
center--leaf edges. The thirty original core vertices together with the
$280$ centers form a cover of the same size.

Here is an exact count of the endpoint coefficients. More generally let
$a,b,N,M$ be Hall parameters, put $W=k+1$, $K=N(W-1)$, and let $P_\ell(t)$
count original supports using exactly $\ell$ exterior-left vertices when
only the right core has activity $W$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:marked-hall}
 P_\ell(t)=\binom N\ell
 \sum_{\substack{0\le i\le a,\ 0\le j\le b\\j\ge\ell}}
 \binom ai\binom bj\binom M{i+\ell-j}W^j t^{i+\ell},
\end{equation}
where out-of-range binomial coefficients are zero. The condition
$j\ge\ell$ is exactly the Hall-support feasibility condition after
balancing the shores. Repeated application of
Lemma~\ref{mrn:u:os:lem:pendant-identity} gives the full polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:os:eq:full-gadget-polynomial}
 P(t;T)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\min(N,b)}P_\ell(t)
 (1+Tt)^{K-N+\ell}(1+WTt)^{N-\ell}.
\end{equation}
Every factor and coefficient in this expression is explicit.

For $(a,b,N,M,W,T)=(20,10,14,30,21,1)$, coefficient extraction gives
\begin{align*}
 p_{308}&=8131749153344342901404784768563550,\\
 p_{309}&=39894459936702682423206366171900,\\
 p_{310}&=97561086654439123859418490215.
\end{align*}
Consequently the endpoint margin is
\begin{align*}
 309p_{309}^2-620p_{308}p_{310}
 &=-726235\cdot4485169759863727935000\cdot21^{26}<0.
\end{align*}
This is an exact one-shore-weighted counterexample. Its full negative
integer margin, full coefficient vector, edge list, matching, and cover
are included in the verification data. \mnote{Shipped as \path{data/73-one-shore-gadget_verification-one_shore_rank310_witness.json}, beside \path{code/73-one-shore-gadget_verification-*.py}; the gadget identity record is byte-identical to source~71's \path{data/71-unit-weight-gadget_identity_verification.json}.}

Keeping the same graph and allowing each center activity to be $T$, the
stronger exact identity is
\begin{align*}
 309p_{309}(T)^2-620p_{308}(T)p_{310}(T)
 ={}&4485169759863727935000\cdot21^{26}T^{558}\\
 &\cdot\bigl(-91777T^2-1459920T+825462\bigr).
\end{align*}
At $T=1$ the bracket is $-726235$. This identity makes the finite activity
explicit; no limiting value is used in the counterexample.

\paragraph{Verification.}
The gadget identity was checked against independent weighted endpoint-set
enumeration on $150$ small bipartite graphs, with a fixed seed. A separate
standard-library script expands \eqref{mrn:u:os:eq:full-gadget-polynomial} in full,
checks the three displayed coefficients and exact margin, and verifies
the explicit rank certificates. An independent audit recomputed the
three endpoint coefficients directly from Hall subset feasibility and
the top coefficients of each gadget factor. The finite checks supplement
the counting proofs above.

%% ======================== source 55 ========================
\section{A sharp rank-38 boundary for the last Hall inequality, one common right-core activity (source~55)}\label{mrn:src:55}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~55, as delivered.} For full Hall graphs with one common activity on the right core and unit activities elsewhere, the first possible failure of the last matching-rank-normalized Newton inequality occurs at rank 38. We prove this for all finite exterior populations and every positive core activity, rather than only in a limiting regime. An explicit positive identity rules out a negative finite-field window whenever the leading quadratic coefficient is nonnegative. A cubic then gives an exact parameter criterion, and a finite integer certificate proves the rank threshold. Precisely two core shapes occur at rank 38. The smaller of their minimal populations gives an 864-vertex example with an exact integer certificate.

\paragraph{Scope.}
The activity is common across one specified core block. The theorem concerns the \emph{last} Newton inequality. It does not determine the first failure among arbitrary individual one-shore activities or among all gaps. General finite-activity rank-six one-shore ultra-log-concavity remains open. \mnote{At rank six, the first gap holds for every positively weighted graph (\prp, Theorem~180.2), and the second and last gaps for every one-shore graph (sources~05 and~13, Part~III); the third and fourth stay open in general.} These are ordinary mathematical proofs with exact certificates, not formal kernel verification or external peer review; no global priority claim is made.

\subsection{The family and the sharp threshold}
Let $H_{a,b;n,m}$ have shores $A\sqcup L$ and $B\sqcup R$, with respective block sizes $a,n,b,m$, and edges
\[
 (A\times B)\cup(L\times B)\cup(A\times R).
\]
Here $a,b\ge1$ and $n,m\ge0$ are integers. Every vertex has activity one except the vertices of $B$, whose common activity is $w>0$. Write
\[
 P(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{d}p_kt^k,
\]
where $p_k$ is the total activity of pairs of $k$-element endpoint sets admitting a perfect matching. Each pair is counted once, regardless of its number of matchings. The degree $d$ is the matching rank. The last rank-normalized Newton inequality is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:last}
 \Delta_{d-1}:=(d-1)p_{d-1}^{\,2}-2d p_{d-2}p_d\ge0
 \qquad(d\ge2).
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp common-core-field endpoint boundary]\label{mrn:u:ep:thm:main}
Within this family, the smallest matching rank for which \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:last} fails at some $w>0$ is exactly $38$. At rank $38$, the only triples
\[
 (a,b,h),\qquad h=n-b+1,
\]
permitting failure for some finite $m,w$ are
\[
 (7,31,3)\quad\text{and}\quad(8,30,3).
\]
Their least possible $m$ are $923$ and $794$, respectively. Thus the fewest vertices at rank $38$ in this family with a failing last gap is $864$, attained by $H_{8,30;32,794}$. For that graph the first failing positive integer activity is $w=292602646$.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{Source~47 (Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:ag:thm:main}) shows that in this family no gap at all fails below matching number $38$, so the least failure rank of full $\ULC$ is also $38$. This is the one-shore analogue of Research question~88 of \prp; that question itself, with activity on both cores, is not answered here.}

The qualifier ``at rank $38$'' in the vertex statement matters: a previously constructed rank-$50$ example has only $125$ vertices \cite{b:m73}. The new conclusion is a sharp rank threshold for the stated family and gap.

\subsubsection{Deficient exterior populations}
The actual matching rank is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:rank}
 d=\min\{a+b,a+n,b+m\}.
\end{equation}
For a direct construction, first match $\min(a,m)$ pairs from $A$ to $R$ and $\min(b,n)$ pairs from $L$ to $B$, then match the remaining core vertices to each other. This attains \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:rank}; the three displayed quantities are upper bounds from the core cover and the two shores.

If $n<b$ or $m<a$, this rank is the smaller shore size, and the established weighted smaller-shore theorem gives the entire actual-rank ULC sequence. \mnote{That theorem is \prp's Theorem~70.1 (Part~V), Lemma~\ref{mrn:w:rf:lem:small} here; the specialization recalled next is its proof.} Here is the weighted specialization explicitly. For any bipartite graph $G=(X,Y)$, introduce a private element $\bar x$ for each $x\in X$. Match these private elements and $Y$ into $X$, with $\bar x$ adjacent only to $x$. The resulting transversal matroid has bases
\[
 \{\bar x:x\notin I\}\sqcup J
\]
for exactly the feasible support pairs $(I,J)$. Its Lorentzian basis polynomial $\mathcal B$, specialized by
\[
 \left(\prod_{x\in X}u_x\right)
 \mathcal B\big((s/u_x)_{x\in X},(tv_y)_{y\in Y}\big)
 =\sum_k p_k s^{|X|-k}t^k,
\]
gives $\ULC_{|X|}$ by the bivariate characterization and closure under nonnegative linear substitutions \cite{b:BH}. Transpose to obtain the smaller order. This is the weighted form of the relation/bimatroid argument in \cite{b:RU}. Consequently deficient populations cannot fail \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:last}. We henceforth assume $n\ge b$ and $m\ge a$, and write $r=a+b=d$.

\subsection{The exact endpoint quadratic}
A size-$k$ support selecting $i$ core-left and $j$ core-right vertices is feasible exactly when $i+j\ge k$. Indeed, the selected exterior-left vertices must fit into the selected $B$ vertices; after matching the exterior vertices, the remaining core vertices can be matched because the core is complete. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:count}
 p_k=\sum_{\substack{0\le i\le a,\ 0\le j\le b\\i+j\ge k}}
 \binom ai\binom n{k-i}\binom bj\binom m{k-j}w^j,
\end{equation}
where out-of-range binomial coefficients are zero.

Set
\begin{gather*}
 h=n-b+1,\quad x=\frac bh,\quad y=\frac{b(b-1)}{h(h+1)},\\
 R=\frac{m-a+1}{a},\quad \rho=\frac{a-1}{m-a+2},\quad
 J=\binom a2+ax+y,\quad D=\binom nb\binom m{a-1}>0.
\end{gather*}
Enumerating total core deficits zero, one and two in \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:count} gives
\begin{align*}
 p_r&=DRw^b,\\
 p_{r-1}&=D\big((a+x)w^b+bxRw^{b-1}\big),\\
 p_{r-2}&=D\left(J\rho w^b+b(ax+y)w^{b-1}
              +\binom b2 yRw^{b-2}\right).
\end{align*}
When $b=1$, the last term is zero. Substitution in \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:last} yields
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:quad}
 \Delta_{r-1}=D^2w^{2b-2}\big(\alpha_m w^2+2bRBw+R^2C\big),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
 \alpha_m&=(r-1)(a+x)^2-2rR\rho J,\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:alpha}\\
 B&=(r-1)x(a+x)-r(ax+y)
   =\frac{b[b(r-1)-ah^2]}{h^2(h+1)},\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:B}\\
 C&=b\big(b(r-1)x^2-r(b-1)y\big)\notag\\
  &=\frac{b^2\{b^2(r-1)+h[r(2b-1)-b^2]\}}{h^2(h+1)}>0.\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:C}
\end{align}
The last positivity uses $r(2b-1)-b^2=a(2b-1)+b(b-1)>0$.
Define
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:limit}
 K=\frac{2r(a-1)}aJ,\qquad
 \alpha_\infty=(r-1)(a+x)^2-K.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:monotone}
 \alpha_m=\alpha_\infty+\frac{K}{m-a+2}\ge\alpha_\infty.
\end{equation}

\subsection{A finite field cannot create a hidden negative window}
\begin{lemma}[Positive discriminant margin]\label{mrn:u:ep:lem:identity}
Put $H=ah^2-b(r-1)$ and define
\begin{align*}
 Q_0&=a^2(b^2+2b-1)+a(b-1)(2b^2+3b-1)+b(b-1)^2,\\
 Q_1&=(r-1)(2ab-a-b),\\
 R_0&=a(b^2+2b-1)+(b-1)(3b-1),\\
 R_1&=a(3b-1)+(b-1).
\end{align*}
For all positive integers $a,b$,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:positiveidentity}
 \frac{ah^3(h+1)^2}{b^2r}\big(\alpha_\infty C-b^2B^2\big)
 =H(Q_0+Q_1h)+br(r-1)(R_0+R_1h).
\end{equation}
If $H\ge0$, the right side is strictly positive.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Substitution of \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:B}, \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:C}, and \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:limit}, followed by collecting powers of $h$, proves the identity. Each displayed term in $Q_0,R_0,R_1$ is nonnegative and their first terms are positive. Also $2ab-a-b=a(b-1)+b(a-1)\ge0$, so $Q_1\ge0$. Finally $br(r-1)>0$ and $R_0+R_1h>0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact finite-field and population criterion]\label{mrn:u:ep:thm:criterion}
Fix $a,b,n,m$ with $n\ge b$, $m\ge a$. The last inequality fails for some $w>0$ if and only if $\alpha_m<0$. If $\alpha_m\ge0$, it is strictly satisfied for every $w>0$. If $\alpha_m<0$, failure occurs precisely above the unique positive root of the quadratic in \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:quad}.

For fixed $a,b,h$, there exist finite $m$ and $w>0$ giving failure if and only if $\alpha_\infty<0$. In that case the least eligible integer population is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:mmin}
 m_{\min}=a-1+\left\lfloor\frac{K}{-\alpha_\infty}\right\rfloor.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $B\ge0$ and $\alpha_m\ge0$, every term in the quadratic is nonnegative and its constant term is positive. If $B<0$, then $H>0$ by \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:B}. Lemma~\ref{mrn:u:ep:lem:identity} and \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:monotone} imply
\[
 \alpha_m C-b^2B^2\ge\alpha_\infty C-b^2B^2>0.
\]
In particular $\alpha_m>0$, and the quadratic has negative discriminant and is positive on the entire real line. This rules out every putative negative finite-$w$ window with nonnegative leading coefficient.

If $\alpha_m<0$, the constant term is positive, so the two real roots have opposite signs and failure is exactly above the positive root. Now \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:monotone} shows that some finite $m$ has $\alpha_m<0$ exactly when $\alpha_\infty<0$. In that case the necessary and sufficient condition is
\[
 m-a+2>\frac{K}{-\alpha_\infty},
\]
whose least integer solution is \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:mmin}. Since $K+\alpha_\infty=(r-1)(a+x)^2>0$, the quotient in \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:mmin} exceeds one, so $m_{\min}\ge a$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A cubic classification and the finite rank certificate}
Clearing the positive denominator in \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:limit} gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:cubic}
 ah^2(h+1)\alpha_\infty=F_{a,b}(h)
 =A_3h^3+A_2h^2+A_1h+A_0,
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align*}
 A_0&=ab^2(r-1),\\
 A_1&=-b(r-1)(ab-2a-2b),\\
 A_2&=a(a^2+2ab-a+2b^2-b),\\
 A_3&=a(a^2+2ab-a-b).
\end{align*}
The coefficients $A_0,A_2,A_3$ are strictly positive. Only $A_1$ can be negative. In particular $a\le2$ or $b\le2$ forces $F_{a,b}(h)>0$ for all $h\ge1$.

This gives a complete test for any fixed core shape, at arbitrary rank. If $A_1\ge0$, failure is impossible. Otherwise the unique positive critical point is
\[
 h_* =\frac{-A_2+\sqrt{A_2^2-3A_3A_1}}{3A_3}.
\]
Since $F''(h)=6A_3h+2A_2>0$ on $h\ge0$, the integer minimum for $h\ge1$ is attained at
\[
 \max\{1,\lfloor h_*\rfloor\},\qquad
 \max\{1,\lceil h_*\rceil\}.
\]
Some exterior populations and a common core field fail the last gap exactly when at least one of the two corresponding cubic values is negative.

For the sharp finite-rank certificate we use an integer-only tail bound. Set
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:cutoff}
 H_0=\max\left\{1,\left\lceil\frac{-A_1}{A_2}\right\rceil\right\}.
\end{equation}
For $h\ge H_0$, the sum $A_2h^2+A_1h=h(A_2h+A_1)$ is nonnegative, so $F(h)>0$. It therefore suffices to evaluate $1\le h\le H_0$, with the boundary included redundantly.

The following complete integer procedure covers every rank from $4$ through $38$:
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 r=4,\ldots,38;\qquad a=2,\ldots,r-2;\qquad b=r-a;\\
 h=1,\ldots,H_0(a,b);\qquad\text{evaluate }F_{a,b}(h).
 \end{gathered}
\]
It contains $630$ core pairs and exactly $2237$ integer evaluations; the largest cutoff is $10$. Every value at rank at most $37$ is positive. At rank $38$, the only negative values are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:two}
 F_{7,31}(3)=-3056,\qquad F_{8,30}(3)=-4392.
\end{equation}
No zeros occur. All omitted $a=1$ or $b=1$ cases have $A_1\ge0$, and \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:cutoff} proves positivity for all omitted $h$. The integer verifier and complete evaluation list accompany this report. For ease of inspection, Table~\ref{mrn:u:ep:tab:minima} records the minimum evaluated value at each rank. The tail argument, not a numerical extrapolation, makes the finite calculation exhaustive.

\subsection{The two rank 38 shapes and the smaller witness}
For $(a,b,h)=(8,30,3)$ one has
\[
 \alpha_\infty=-\frac{61}{4},\qquad
 \alpha_m=\frac{48379-61m}{4(m-6)}.
\]
Thus $\alpha_m<0$ exactly when $m\ge794$. For $(7,31,3)$, the limiting coefficient is $-764/63$, and \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:mmin} gives $m_{\min}=923$. These are the complete possibilities at rank $38$ by \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:two}.

For the first graph at its minimal population, put
\[
 D=\binom{32}{30}\binom{794}{7},\qquad
 q(w)=-22w^2+6437219280w+11385894392295.
\]
The exact endpoint factorization is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ep:eq:witness}
 \Delta_{37}=\frac{5}{6304}D^2w^{58}q(w).
\end{equation}
Direct integer substitution gives
\[
 q(292602645)=725475345>0,\qquad
 q(292602646)=-5711821777<0.
\]
The unique positive root proves the stated first failing integer. This graph has $864$ vertices and $7552$ edges. The other rank-$38$ shape has at least $38+33+923=994$ vertices, establishing the rank-$38$ vertex minimum in Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:ep:thm:main}.

For completeness, at that second minimal population its primitive endpoint quadratic is
\[
 -2w^2+3479879022w+8332557477297,
\]
whose first failing positive integer is $1739941906$. The preceding and failing values are $1715636157$ and $-1764252443$.

\subsection{Verification and attribution}
The top-three formula and the earlier rank-$38$ construction appeared in the companion \cite{b:m73}. \mnote{Source~73: Lemma~\ref{mrn:u:os:lem:top}, Proposition~\ref{mrn:u:os:prop:quadratic} (whose $\alpha,\beta,\gamma$ are the $\alpha_m$, $2bRB$, $R^2C$ above) and Proposition~\ref{mrn:u:os:prop:finite}.} The additional conclusions here are the exact finite-field criterion, exhaustive rank-$38$ endpoint classification, and minimal populations within that rank. The smaller-shore input is established Lorentzian theory \cite{b:BH,b:RU}.

The standard-library script \texttt{verify\_boundary.py} \mnote{shipped as \path{code/55-rank38-endpoint-verify_boundary.py} and \path{code/55-rank38-endpoint-verify_symbolic.py}, outputs \path{data/55-rank38-endpoint-*.json}} regenerates all $2237$ exact cubic values, verifies both rank-$38$ population thresholds, and checks the endpoint formula against the complete support sum on $160$ deterministic rational-activity samples. It also checks the full support coefficients of both new minimal-population witnesses; in each, the last inequality is the only failing gap. The separate script \texttt{verify\_symbolic.py} expands \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:positiveidentity} and \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:cubic} exactly over $\mathbb Q(a,b,h)$. The complete certificate, scripts, and outputs are included. The general identities and the proven finite tail bound establish the result; sample regressions are additional checks.

The exact first-failure question for arbitrary individual one-shore activities is broader. Earlier Newton gaps in the common-core-field family are also outside this report's classification. \mnote{The earlier gaps are settled by source~47 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:47}).} Neither is settled by the last-gap criterion proved here.

%% ======================== source 47 ========================
\section{The sharp rank-38 ULC boundary for full Hall graphs, one common right-core activity (source~47)}\label{mrn:src:47}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~47, as delivered.} For full Hall graphs with a common positive activity on the right core and unit activities elsewhere, the first possible failure of matching-rank-normalized ultra-log-concavity occurs at rank 38. We close the earlier-gap question left open by the endpoint classification. Every intermediate Newton gap through rank 38 has positive coefficients in the common field, for every eligible pair of exterior populations. The proof expands each gap in two binomial population bases and checks a finite array of 22,452,529 positive integer coefficients. This is an exact all-population certificate, not a bounded population or activity scan. Together with the first-gap inequality and the previously established endpoint theorem, it gives the complete failure classification at rank 38.

\paragraph{Scope.}
The field is common across one specified core block. The result does not determine the first failure for arbitrary individual one-shore activities or for arbitrary bipartite graphs. General finite-activity rank-six one-shore ultra-log-concavity remains open. \mnote{See the note in the scope paragraph of source~55 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:55}).} This is a computer-assisted mathematical proof with reproducible integer certificates, not formal kernel verification or an external peer-review claim.

\subsection{The family and the conclusion}
Let $H_{a,b;n,m}$ have shores $A\sqcup L$ and $B\sqcup R$, with block sizes $a,n,b,m$, respectively, and edges
\[
 (A\times B)\cup(L\times B)\cup(A\times R).
\]
Here $a,b\ge1$ and $n,m\ge0$ are integers. Every activity is one except those of $B$, which all equal $w>0$. The support polynomial is
\[
 P(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{d}p_k(w)t^k.
\]
Each pair of equally sized endpoint sets admitting a perfect matching is counted once, regardless of its number of matchings. Its degree is
\[
 d=\min\{a+b,a+n,b+m\}.
\]
For $1\le k<d$, put
\[
 \Delta_k=k(d-k)p_k^2-(k+1)(d-k+1)p_{k-1}p_{k+1}.
\]
The sequence is $\ULC_d$ precisely when every $\Delta_k\ge0$, since all coefficients up to the matching rank are positive.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp boundary for all gaps]\label{mrn:u:ag:thm:main}
Every graph in this common-core-field family of matching rank at most $37$ is $\ULC_d$ for every $w>0$ and all exterior populations.

At matching rank $38$, every gap except possibly the last is strictly positive whenever $a,b\ge3$ and $n\ge b,m\ge a$. A failure at some finite $m,w$ is possible only for
\[
 (a,b,n-b+1)=(7,31,3)\quad\hbox{or}\quad(8,30,3).
\]
The least corresponding $m$ are $923$ and $794$. The smallest graph at rank $38$ in this family admitting any failed gap therefore has $864$ vertices, namely $H_{8,30;32,794}$. Its first failing positive integer core activity is $292602646$.
\end{theorem}

The new ingredient is the following stronger statement about intermediate gaps.
\begin{theorem}[Positive common-field coefficients]\label{mrn:u:ag:thm:intermediate}
Suppose $a,b\ge3$, $r=a+b\le38$, $n\ge b$, and $m\ge a$. For every $2\le k\le r-2$, all coefficients of
\[
 w^{-\max(0,\,2k-2a)}\Delta_k
\]
from degree zero through degree $\min(2b,2k)-\max(0,2k-2a)$ are strictly positive. In particular $\Delta_k>0$ for every $w>0$.
\end{theorem}

At larger rank the last gap does not control the earlier ones: the separate rank-$49$ example $H_{14,35;38,624}$ has its last gap positive for every $w>0$ but a failing penultimate gap at $w=40000000$ \cite{b:m51}. The rank restriction in Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:ag:thm:intermediate} is therefore essential to the present certificate.

\subsection{Counting and the finite population expansion}
Write $\binom xy=0$ when $y<0$ or $y>x$, for nonnegative integer $x$. For $j,q\ge0$, define
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ag:eq:T}
 T_{j,q}(n)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{j}\binom a{q+\ell}\binom n{j-\ell};
\end{equation}
set $T_{j,q}=0$ when either subscript is negative. A support selecting $j$ vertices of $B$ and $q$ of $R$ must select at least $q$ vertices of $A$. If it selects $q+\ell$ of $A$, it selects $j-\ell$ of $L$. The complete core makes this necessary condition sufficient. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ag:eq:pk}
 p_k(w)=\sum_{j=0}^{b}\binom bj\binom m{k-j}T_{j,k-j}(n)w^j.
\end{equation}
This is endpoint-support counting throughout.

Fix $2\le k\le r-2$. For a coefficient of $w^s$, write $L=2k-s$. Its possible range is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ag:eq:srange}
 \max(0,2k-2a)\le s\le\min(2b,2k).
\end{equation}
For $0\le p\le\lfloor L/2\rfloor$, define
\begin{align}
 G_{k,s,p}(n)
 &=\binom{L-p}{p}
   \sum_{j=\max(0,s-b)}^{\min(b,s)}
       \binom bj\binom b{s-j}\bigl(U_j-V_j\bigr),\label{mrn:u:ag:eq:G}\\
 U_j&=k(r-k)\binom{L-2p}{k-j-p}
       T_{j,k-j}(n)T_{s-j,k-s+j}(n),\notag\\
 V_j&=(k+1)(r-k+1)\binom{L-2p}{k-1-j-p}
       T_{j,k-1-j}(n)T_{s-j,k+1-s+j}(n).\notag
\end{align}

\begin{lemma}[Exact finite population transfer]\label{mrn:u:ag:lem:transfer}
For every nonnegative integer $m$,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ag:eq:transfer}
 [w^s]\Delta_k=\sum_{p=0}^{\lfloor L/2\rfloor}
                  \binom m{L-p}G_{k,s,p}(n).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Two subsets of an $m$-element set, of sizes $q_1,q_2$ and intersection size $p$, can be specified by their union, their intersection, and the first disjoint remainder. Thus
\[
 \binom m{q_1}\binom m{q_2}
 =\sum_p\binom m{q_1+q_2-p}
          \binom{q_1+q_2-p}{p}
          \binom{q_1+q_2-2p}{q_1-p}.
\]
Both products in the coefficient of $w^s$ have $q_1+q_2=L$. Applying this identity to \eqref{mrn:u:ag:eq:pk} gives exactly \eqref{mrn:u:ag:eq:G}--\eqref{mrn:u:ag:eq:transfer}.
\end{proof}

This transfer keeps all finite-population terms, including intersections. The diffuse limit alone would retain only $p=0$ and would not justify the finite-$m$ conclusion.

\subsection{An exhaustive finite integer certificate}
The polynomial $T_{j,q}(n)$ has degree at most $j$, so $G_{k,s,p}(n)$ has degree at most $s$. Its Newton finite-difference expansion about $n=b$ is
\begin{align}
 G_{k,s,p}(b+h)
 &=\sum_{e=0}^{s}c_{a,b,k,s,p,e}\binom he,\label{mrn:u:ag:eq:difference}\\
 c_{a,b,k,s,p,e}
 &=\sum_{i=0}^{e}(-1)^{e-i}\binom ei G_{k,s,p}(b+i).\label{mrn:u:ag:eq:c}
\end{align}
These are integer identities. Combining them with Lemma~\ref{mrn:u:ag:lem:transfer} gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:ag:eq:certificate}
 \Delta_k=
 \sum_s\sum_{p=0}^{\lfloor(2k-s)/2\rfloor}\sum_{e=0}^{s}
 c_{a,b,k,s,p,e}\,
 w^s\binom m{2k-s-p}\binom{n-b}{e}.
\end{equation}
Every basis factor is nonnegative at the populations and activities in question.

\begin{proposition}[Verified coefficient array]\label{mrn:u:ag:prop:array}
Every integer $c_{a,b,k,s,p,e}$ is strictly positive over the finite range
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 6\le r\le38,\qquad 3\le a\le r-3,\qquad b=r-a,\qquad
 2\le k\le r-2,\\
 \max(0,2k-2a)\le s\le\min(2b,2k),\qquad
 0\le p\le\lfloor(2k-s)/2\rfloor,\qquad 0\le e\le s.
 \end{gathered}
\]
There are $561$ core pairs, $13{,}651$ intermediate gaps, $1{,}583{,}703$ polynomials $G$, and $22{,}452{,}529$ checked coefficients.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Exact verification]
The accompanying standard-library verifier evaluates \eqref{mrn:u:ag:eq:G} at $n=b,\ldots,b+s$, forms successive difference rows, and checks their first entries against zero. Every arithmetic operation is an integer addition, multiplication, binomial evaluation, or comparison. It visits precisely the displayed finite range; there are no numerical tolerances, sampled fields, or omitted population tails. Per-gap coefficient counts and SHA-256 records are included, allowing all entries to be regenerated and compared. A separate implementation constructs the support counts by adding exterior vertices using Pascal recurrence and computes the coefficients by the explicit inverse Pascal matrix. It agrees with every per-gap hash and count. The smallest checked coefficient is $9$; there are no zero entries.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:ag:thm:intermediate}]
Equation~\eqref{mrn:u:ag:eq:certificate} and Proposition~\ref{mrn:u:ag:prop:array} give coefficientwise nonnegativity in $w$ for all $n\ge b$ and $m\ge a$. For each $s$ in \eqref{mrn:u:ag:eq:srange}, take $p=\lfloor L/2\rfloor$ and $e=0$. Then
\[
 L-p=\lceil L/2\rceil\le a\le m,
\]
so the corresponding population binomial is positive. Its coefficient is strictly positive by Proposition~\ref{mrn:u:ag:prop:array}. Thus every stated common-field coefficient is positive.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Closing the first and last gaps}
\subsubsection{The first gap}
\mnote{Source~47 proves here, by the Motzkin--Straus route, that $\Delta_1\ge0$ for every positively weighted bipartite graph; this is \prp's Theorem~180.2 (Part~XI, \path{hb:thm:firstidentity}) and the inequality printed with this route in Sections~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:firstgap} and~\ref{mrn:src:72}. The argument is not reprinted; in it $M_2\ge p_2$ is the weighted number of two-edge matchings and the Motzkin--Straus theorem gives $M_2\le\frac{d-1}{2d}p_1^2$. Source~47's strictness step is kept verbatim:} In the genuine full Hall cases with $a,b\ge3$, two vertices from each core form a $K_{2,2}$. Its support contributes two positive matchings to $M_2$ but only once to $p_2$. Hence $M_2>p_2$ and $\Delta_1\ge2d(M_2-p_2)>0$. \mnote{In \prp's identity this is the positive term $B_4$.}

\subsubsection{The endpoint classification}
We use the exact endpoint theorem of \cite{b:m55}. \mnote{That theorem is source~55, printed in Section~\ref{mrn:src:55}: the quadratic~\eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:quad}, Lemma~\ref{mrn:u:ep:lem:identity}, Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:ep:thm:criterion}, the cubic~\eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:cubic} and the two negative values~\eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:two}. Source~47 recalls its formulas without proof; the recall is kept below, in source~47's notation, which agrees with source~55's.} Its essential formulas are recalled to make the scope and resulting boundary explicit. Assume $n\ge b,m\ge a$, and put
\[
 h=n-b+1,\quad x=\frac bh,\quad y=\frac{b(b-1)}{h(h+1)},\quad
 R=\frac{m-a+1}{a},\quad \rho=\frac{a-1}{m-a+2},
\]
\[
 J=\binom a2+ax+y,\qquad D=\binom nb\binom m{a-1}.
\]
Then
\[
 \Delta_{r-1}=D^2w^{2b-2}
       \bigl(\alpha_m w^2+2bRBw+R^2C\bigr),
\]
where
\[
 \alpha_m=(r-1)(a+x)^2-2rR\rho J,\qquad
 B=\frac{b[b(r-1)-ah^2]}{h^2(h+1)},
\]
\[
 C=\frac{b^2\{b^2(r-1)+h[r(2b-1)-b^2]\}}{h^2(h+1)}>0.
\]
The endpoint theorem proves that this quadratic is positive for every $w>0$ if $\alpha_m\ge0$. If $\alpha_m<0$, it has one positive root and is negative precisely above it. Thus finite fields introduce no hidden negative window.

Writing
\[
 K=\frac{2r(a-1)}aJ,\qquad
 \alpha_\infty=(r-1)(a+x)^2-K
\]
gives $\alpha_m=\alpha_\infty+K/(m-a+2)$. Some finite $m,w$ fail exactly when $\alpha_\infty<0$, and then
\[
 m_{\min}=a-1+\left\lfloor\frac K{-\alpha_\infty}\right\rfloor.
\]
The sign of $\alpha_\infty$ is the sign of the cubic
\begin{align*}
 F_{a,b}(h)
 &=a(a^2+2ab-a-b)h^3
   +a(a^2+2ab-a+2b^2-b)h^2\\
 &\quad-b(r-1)(ab-2a-2b)h+ab^2(r-1).
\end{align*}
Only its linear coefficient can be negative. The endpoint certificate uses the finite tail cutoff $\max(1,\lceil-A_1/A_2\rceil)$ for these cubic coefficients. Its $2237$ exact evaluations show positivity through rank $37$, and exactly
\[
 F_{7,31}(3)=-3056,\qquad F_{8,30}(3)=-4392
\]
as the rank-$38$ negative cases. The tail bound makes this exhaustive in $h$.

\subsubsection{The remaining cover and population cases}
If $n<b$ or $m<a$, the actual rank is the smaller shore size. The standard weighted transversal-matroid specialization then gives actual-rank ULC \cite{b:BH,b:RU}. \mnote{\prp's Theorem~70.1; Lemma~\ref{mrn:w:rf:lem:small} here.} Explicitly, with a private element for each left vertex, the extended transversal basis polynomial specializes to
\[
 \sum_k p_k s^{|X|-k}t^k
\]
by assigning a private variable $s/u_x$ and a right variable $tv_y$, then multiplying by $\prod_xu_x$. Matroid basis polynomials are Lorentzian, so this is $\ULC_{|X|}$; transpose for the smaller shore.

When $n\ge b,m\ge a$ and $\min(a,b)\le2$, the previously proved minimum-cover theorem \cite{b:m74} gives actual-rank ULC for arbitrary positive activities. \mnote{Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main}; also source~75's Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:fh:thm:classification}.} All remaining cases are precisely those in Proposition~\ref{mrn:u:ag:prop:array}. The first gap and the endpoint classification therefore complete the proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:ag:thm:main}.

\subsection{The sharp witness and reproducibility}
For $H_{8,30;32,794}$, let $D=\binom{32}{30}\binom{794}{7}$. The endpoint factorization is
\[
 \Delta_{37}=\frac5{6304}D^2w^{58}
  \bigl(-22w^2+6437219280w+11385894392295\bigr).
\]
The quadratic takes the values $725475345$ and $-5711821777$ at $292602645$ and $292602646$, respectively. The graph has $864$ vertices and $7552$ edges. The other rank-$38$ shape requires at least $994$ vertices. \mnote{These are source~55's numbers, \eqref{mrn:u:ep:eq:witness}; restated by source~47.} The new intermediate-gap theorem shows that these endpoint possibilities also exhaust every-gap failure at rank $38$.

The new certificate and the endpoint certificate have different purposes. The former covers every intermediate gap and both unbounded exterior populations through binomial-basis identities. The latter covers the endpoint through a cubic tail bound and an exact quadratic criterion. The source package contains the generators, compact per-gap coefficient records, regression checks of the population identity, and instructions for replay. \mnote{Shipped as \path{code/47-rank38-ulc-*.py} and \path{data/47-rank38-ulc-*.json} (33 per-rank certificate files, ranks 6--38); the replay instructions were in the delivered README, which is not shipped (this report's README gives them).} Numerical scans used during exploration are not part of the proof.

The new conclusion is restricted to a common core field on full Hall graphs. The minimum rank for failure with arbitrary individual one-shore activities remains between $6$ and $38$. \mnote{The lower end six rests on source~74's Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive} (Part~I); the upper end on sources~73 and~55.} The rank-$49$ separation \cite{b:m51} prevents extrapolating endpoint control to all higher ranks.

%% ======================== source 51 ========================
\section{The last Hall gap does not control ultra-log-concavity, one common right-core activity (source~51)}\label{mrn:src:51}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~51, as delivered.} We give a full Hall graph of rank 49 with a common activity on one right-core block for which the last rank-normalized Newton inequality holds strictly at every positive activity, but the preceding inequality fails. The example has 711 vertices. Four explicit coefficient formulas give an exact quartic certificate. This separates the last-gap criterion from full ultra-log-concavity without changing the previously proved sharp rank-38 endpoint threshold.

Let $H_{a,b;n,m}$ have left blocks $A,L$ of sizes $a,n$, right blocks $B,R$ of sizes $b,m$, and edges
\[
 (A\times B)\cup(L\times B)\cup(A\times R).
\]
Give every vertex activity one except the vertices of $B$, each of which has activity $w>0$. The coefficient $p_k$ of its support polynomial counts each pair of $k$-element endpoint sets admitting a perfect matching once, with the product of its vertex activities. For matching rank $r$, write
\[
 \Delta_k=k(r-k)p_k^2-(k+1)(r-k+1)p_{k-1}p_{k+1}.
\]
Rank-normalized ultra-log-concavity requires $\Delta_k\ge0$ at every $1\le k<r$.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:u:sp:thm:separation}
The graph $H_{14,35;38,624}$ has matching rank $49$, $711$ vertices and $10556$ edges. Its last gap $\Delta_{48}$ is strictly positive for every $w>0$. At $w=40000000$, its preceding gap $\Delta_{47}$ is negative. The first positive integer $w$ for which $\Delta_{47}<0$ is $38189138$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The core is a cover of size $49$, attained by disjoint matchings $A\to R$ and $L\to B$. This proves the rank. The vertex and edge counts are
\[
 14+35+38+624=711,\qquad14\cdot35+38\cdot35+14\cdot624=10556.
\]
For general full Hall parameters, a size-$k$ support selecting $i$ vertices of $A$ and $j$ of $B$ is feasible exactly when $i+j\ge k$. Hence, with out-of-range binomial coefficients zero,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:u:sp:eq:sum}
 p_k=\sum_{\substack{0\le i\le a,\ 0\le j\le b\\i+j\ge k}}
 \binom ai\binom n{k-i}\binom bj\binom m{k-j}w^j.
\end{equation}
Set $D=\binom{38}{35}\binom{624}{12}>0$. The four coefficients needed here are
\begin{align}
 \frac{p_{46}}{Dw^{32}}
 &=\frac{8801172765}{2}+32499495w
   +\frac{136955}{2}w^2+\frac{27846}{613}w^3,\label{mrn:u:sp:eq:four}\\
 \frac{p_{47}}{Dw^{33}}&=72736965+299880w+273w^2,\notag\\
 \frac{p_{48}}{Dw^{34}}&=\frac{1258425}{2}+1071w,\notag\\
 \frac{p_{49}}{Dw^{35}}&=\frac{14382}{7}.\notag
\end{align}
These follow directly from \eqref{mrn:u:sp:eq:sum}: the core-deficit pairs $(14-i,35-j)$ have total at most $3,2,1,0$, respectively. Thus the formulas require only ten, six, three and one terms.

Substituting \eqref{mrn:u:sp:eq:four} gives
\begin{align}
 \Delta_{47}&=\frac{882}{613}D^2w^{66}q(w),\label{mrn:u:sp:eq:penultimate}\\
 \Delta_{48}&=89964D^2w^{68}\bigl(w^2+47940w+48443370\bigr),\label{mrn:u:sp:eq:last}
\end{align}
where
\begin{align*}
 q(w)={}&-13w^4+496441140w^3+673931488920w^2\\
        &+331783888454280w+68528424519495225.
\end{align*}
Every factor in \eqref{mrn:u:sp:eq:last} is positive for $w>0$. In contrast,
\[
 q(40000000)=-1506688736346303933404280504775<0,
\]
proving the finite counterexample. The quartic has one sign change in descending coefficient order; Descartes' rule, together with $q(0)>0$ and $q(w)\to-\infty$, gives exactly one positive root. Finally,
\begin{align*}
 q(38189137)&=358201675329747057329792>0,\\
 q(38189138)&=-365864633482267706996743<0.
\end{align*}
This proves the first-integer claim.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Scope and reproducibility.}
The sharp rank-38 theorem in \cite{b:m55} concerns the last gap in this common-core-activity family. It remains valid. The present example shows that its criterion does not characterize the full ULC sequence. It does not decide whether an earlier gap can fail below rank $38$, or the first failure for arbitrary individual one-shore activities. \mnote{The first is decided by source~47 (Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:ag:thm:main}): no gap fails below rank $38$ in this family. The second stays open; it lies in $[6,38]$.}

The accompanying standard-library script \mnote{shipped as \path{code/51-last-gap-verify_counterexample.py}, output \path{data/51-last-gap-verification.json}} independently sums \eqref{mrn:u:sp:eq:sum}, verifies all four coefficient identities and both polynomial factorizations exactly, and checks the stated integer signs. At $w=40000000$, it also finds that $k=47$ is the only negative gap. No numerical root approximation is used. These are ordinary proofs with reproducible exact arithmetic, not formal kernel verification; global priority is not claimed.

\clearpage
\part{Matching number six with one weighted shore at every finite activity}\label{mrn:part:three}

\paragraph{Contents of Part~III.}
Part~III prints seven sources on graphs of matching number six with
\emph{one weighted shore}: all left activities one, right activities
arbitrary positive (Research question~85 of \prp). By source~74 one may
assume a genuine $3+3$ minimum cover. Source~60 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:60})
lifts rank-two exterior column blocks of matrices and gives exact
conditioning reductions; source~10 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:10}) proves a
Lorentzian three-vertex marginal and all five inequalities for star cores;
source~23 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:23}) proves rooted sector theorems and
gives a 25-vertex graph whose all-right-core conditional cubic fails its
unshifted Newton inequalities; source~13 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:13}) proves
the \emph{last} inequality and source~05 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:05}) the
\emph{second}, for every graph and every activity; source~69
(Section~\ref{mrn:src:69}) proves all five for every core with complete
exterior neighbourhoods, and source~63 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:63}) for a
complete core with nested exterior neighbourhoods, with a barrier and a
conditional theorem. Sources~60 and~10 also allow weights on both shores
in their main theorems; their section titles say so.

\paragraph{Status at matching number six, one weighted shore.}
Gap one holds for every positively weighted graph (\prp, Theorem~180.2);
gap two holds by source~05 and gap five by source~13, for every graph and
every finite activity. Gaps three and four are open in general; they hold
for star cores (source~10), for every core with complete exterior
neighbourhoods (source~69) and for the nested complete-core family
(source~63). Several sources, written before others, call more gaps open;
their sentences are kept with notes.

\paragraph{Same results in several sources.}
Source~60's $9\times9$ rank-three example is \prp's Theorem~192.1 (Part~XII,
\path{mr:thm:matrix}); it is replaced by a pointer. Source~05's
Lemma~\ref{mrn:s:sn:lem:ranktwo} is source~60's Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:matrix}
at $q=0$ (a second route, printed), and source~05's one-vertex compression is
the case $h=1$ of source~60's slack reductions. Source~05's quintic example is
source~23's 25-vertex graph. Source~13's incidence lemma and sector identity
are source~21's (Part~IV), and the lemma is the case $b=3$ of
source~08s's two-plane lemma; it is printed here with source~13's proof. The
first-gap arguments of sources~69 and~63 are \prp's Theorem~180.2 and become
pointers. Gaps two and five of sources~69 and~63 also follow from sources~05
and~13, and \prp's unit $H_{3,n}$ result (Corollary~177.2) is a special case of
source~69's theorem.

\paragraph{Non-claims of Part~III.}
No source proves $\ULC_6$ at every finite one-shore activity; source~60's
conditioned and eventual conclusions do not assert $\ULC$ of the original
polynomial at every finite activity; source~23's obstruction does not refute
rank-six $\ULC$ or an eventual-scaling theorem; source~13's local lemma is
graph-generic only; source~05's quartic route is a research direction and
its quintic route is refuted, not scalar $\ULC_6$; source~63's barrier rules
out the full-marginal approach, not $\ULC_6$; source~69's barrier
coefficient obstructs a proof method, not $\ULC$.

%% ======================== source 60 ========================
\section{Rank-two matrix blocks and exterior conditioning, both shores weighted (source~60)}\label{mrn:src:60}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~60, as delivered.} An explicit matroid lift proves ultra-log-concavity of order $q+2$ for weighted regular-minor support polynomials of matrices whose columns consist of $q$ ordinary columns and a block of rank two. The normalization is the actual matrix rank when that rank is $q+2$. Exact conditioning reductions with Hall slack at most two then show that, in a genuine $a+q$ bipartite cover, forcing any matchable $a-2$ exterior-right vertices leaves an actual-degree-$(q+2)$ ULC polynomial after the forced factor is removed. Scaling those activities sufficiently far also makes the full original polynomial strictly rank-ULC, with an explicit field-dependent threshold. The compressed object may be a matrix rather than a graph; a positive rank-three matrix example shows why this distinction matters. The general finite-activity rank-six one-shore problem remains open.

\subsection{A rank two exterior column block}
Let $M$ be a matrix over a field, with finite row set $X$ and column set $Y$. For positive real activities $u_i,v_j$, define its regular-minor support polynomial by
\[
 P_M(t)=\sum_{\substack{I\subseteq X,\ J\subseteq Y\\|I|=|J|,\ \det M[I,J]\ne0}}
 u_Iv_Jt^{|I|},\qquad u_I=\prod_{i\in I}u_i,
 \quad v_J=\prod_{j\in J}v_j.
\]
The empty minor is one. Each row--column pair is counted once, and determinant magnitudes are not used. The degree is $r=\rank M$. For $p(t)=\sum p_kt^k$, write
\[
 \gap_k^{(D)}(p)=k(D-k)p_k^2-(k+1)(D-k+1)p_{k-1}p_{k+1}.
\]
The property $\ULC_D$ means these gaps are nonnegative and the coefficient sequence has no internal zeros. This is equivalently log-concavity after division by $\binom Dk$.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:s:rb:thm:matrix}
Partition the columns as $Y=B\sqcup R$, where $|B|=q$ and $\rank M[X,R]=2$. For every positive right activity vector, the polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:leftmarginal}
 F_M(s,z)=\sum_{\substack{I,J:\ |I|=|J|\\\det M[I,J]\ne0}}
 z_Iv_Js^{q+2-|I|}
\end{equation}
is Lorentzian. Consequently $P_M$ is $\ULC_{q+2}$ for all positive activities on both sides. This is actual-rank normalization if $\rank M=q+2$.
\end{theorem}
If the rank is smaller, the theorem asserts order $q+2$, not the stronger normalization by that smaller rank. The activity parameters are positive real numbers, independently of the field containing the matrix entries.

\begin{proof}
Factor the exterior block and write
\[
 M=[C,DE],\qquad D\text{ has two columns},\quad
 E\text{ has two rows and rank two}.
\]
For the column $E_j$ indexed by $j\in R$, put
\[
 Q=\sum_{\substack{i<j\text{ in }R\\\det(E_i,E_j)\ne0}}v_iv_j>0,
 \qquad J_0=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Construct a representable matroid in dimension $q+2$ with three kinds of elements:
\[
 a_i=(C_i^{\mathsf T},D_i^{\mathsf T})\ (i\in X),\qquad
 e_b\ (b\in B),\qquad
 p_j=(0,J_0E_j)\ (j\in R).
\]
The private elements $e_b$ are the first $q$ coordinate vectors. Together with any independent plane pair $p_i,p_j$, they form a basis, so the matroid has rank $q+2$.

In its basis polynomial substitute
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:substitution}
 a_i\mapsto z_i,\qquad e_b\mapsto s/v_b,\qquad
 p_j\mapsto sv_j/Q,
\end{equation}
and multiply by $Q\prod_{b\in B}v_b$. We claim the result is~\eqref{mrn:s:rb:eq:leftmarginal}.

Fix a selected original row set $I$ and write the private set as $B\setminus J_B$. The basis-size equation forces the number of plane elements to be
\[
 \ell=2+|J_B|-|I|.
\]
Only $\ell=0,1,2$ are possible. Eliminate the private coordinates and consider the remaining determinant.

If $\ell=2$, it factors as the ordinary minor $C[I,J_B]$ times the plane-pair determinant, up to sign. The total plane-pair activity is $Q/Q^2$, which cancels the outside factor $Q$. This records selection of no exterior column.

If $\ell=1$, the plane element $p_j$ yields the nonzero-minor criterion for $M[I,J_B\cup\{j\}]$. The elementary identity behind this is
\[
 \det(d,J_0e)=d^{\mathsf T}e.
\]
Its activity $v_j/Q$ cancels the outside factor $Q$, recording that one exterior column.

If $\ell=0$, the augmented minor uses the two columns of $D$. An original minor with exterior columns $i,j$ equals this augmented minor times $\det(E_i,E_j)$, up to sign. Thus either no exterior pair works or exactly all independent pairs work; their total activity is $Q$, as supplied by the outside factor. Larger exterior selections are dependent.

In all three cases the private activities supply $v_{J_B}$, and the degree in $s$ is $q+2-|I|$. This proves the claimed polynomial identity without a determinant multiplicity. Matroid basis polynomials are Lorentzian, and nonnegative linear substitutions preserve the property~\cite{b:BH}. Setting $z_i=u_it$ proves the ULC conclusion.
\end{proof}
\mnote{Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:matrix} answers in part Research question~101 of \prp{} (\path{mr:q:bimatroid}, ``Which representable bimatroids satisfy actual-rank ULC despite the dense rank-three obstruction?''): every regular-minor matrix whose columns are $q$ ordinary columns and a block of rank two, when its rank is $q+2$. At $q=0$ it is source~05's Lemma~\ref{mrn:s:sn:lem:ranktwo}, proved there by a different route.}

The construction generalizes the two-vertex-shore graph lift: there the exterior columns are supported on two rows. \mnote{Source~74's lift, Section~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:fulllift}.} Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:matrix} requires only a rank-two exterior column space, not a particular zero pattern. The normalization can be smaller than the ambient-shore normalization in the general bimatroid theorem~\cite{b:RU}.

\subsubsection{The rank two hypothesis cannot simply be increased}
\mnote{Source~60 considers here the positive $9\times9$ matrix $M_{ij}=1+ij+(ij)^2$ of rank three, all of whose minors of orders one to three are positive, so that at unit activities $P_M(t)=1+81t+1296t^2+7056t^3$ and $\gap_2^{(3)}(P_M)=-69984<0$. This is \prp's Theorem~192.1 (Part~XII, \path{mr:thm:matrix}) at $m=n=9$, with the same Cauchy--Binet and Vandermonde proof; source~60 does not cite it. Its value $-69984$ is twice Part~XII's defect $1296^2-3\cdot81\cdot7056=-34992$, because $\gap_2^{(3)}=2\cdot1296^2-6\cdot81\cdot7056$ carries the factor $k(D-k)=2$. The statement and proof are not reprinted; source~60's comments on the example follow.}
The rank-two case is covered by Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:matrix} with $q=0$, whereas rank one is trivial. Thus arbitrary regular-minor matrices can first fail at rank three. This elementary example is used as a scope check, not as a novelty claim. Its entry-support graph is complete bipartite and has matching rank nine; that graph's endpoint polynomial is a different polynomial.

\subsection{Exact conditioning when Hall slack is at most two}
For a weighted bipartite graph $G=(X,Y)$ and a right set $F$, let
\[
 P_G^{\langle F\rangle}(t)=
 \sum_{\substack{(I,J)\text{ feasible}\\F\subseteq J}}u_Iv_Jt^{|I|}.
\]
This is the coefficient of $\lambda^{|F|}$ when the activities in $F$ are multiplied by $\lambda$. Suppose $F$ is matchable, $|F|=h\ge1$, and let $N=N_G(F)$. The Hall slack here means $|N|-h$.

\begin{proposition}[Slack zero and one]\label{mrn:s:rb:prop:smallslack}
If $|N|=h$, then
\[
 P_G^{\langle F\rangle}=v_Fu_Nt^hP_{G-N-F}.
\]
If $|N|=h+1$, put
\[
 E_h=\sum_{\substack{I\subseteq N,\ |I|=h\\G[I,F]\text{ matchable}}}u_I>0.
\]
Delete $N,F$ and add one left vertex of activity $u_N/E_h$. Its neighborhood is the union of the unforced-right neighborhoods of those $i\in N$ for which $N\setminus\{i\}$ matches $F$. Call the resulting graph $H$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:slackone}
 P_G^{\langle F\rangle}=v_FE_ht^hP_H.
\end{equation}
The remaining graph has matching rank $\nu(G)-h$ in either case.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
In slack zero all of $N$ is consumed by $F$. In slack one, supports selecting exactly $h$ members of $N$ likewise consume them all; summing their weights gives $E_h$, with the virtual vertex absent. A support selecting all $h+1$ neighbors has the fixed product $u_N$. Its one available vertex can be matched outside $F$ exactly through the stated union neighborhood. This is a Boolean existence test, so different successful choices do not multiply a support's weight. The virtual activity makes $E_h(u_N/E_h)=u_N$.

A matchable right set extends to a maximum matchable right set. Thus $P_G^{\langle F\rangle}$ has degree $\nu(G)$; the exact identities prove the rank statements.
\end{proof}
For example, conditioning on a right vertex of degree two merges its neighbors $a,b$ into a union-neighborhood vertex of activity $u_au_b/(u_a+u_b)$, with outside factor $v_F(u_a+u_b)t$.

\begin{theorem}[Slack two kernel reduction]\label{mrn:s:rb:thm:kernel}
If $|N|=h+2$, the conditioned polynomial has an exact representation
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:kernelidentity}
 P_G^{\langle F\rangle}=v_FE_ht^hP_{M'},
\end{equation}
where $M'$ is an explicit regular-minor matrix with positive row activities and rank $\nu(G)-h$. In general $M'$ need not represent a graph endpoint polynomial.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Represent $G$ by its generic matrix $M$, putting an algebraically independent indeterminate on every edge and zero elsewhere. Work over the rational-function field. A square minor is nonzero exactly when its endpoint graph has a perfect matching, since distinct matchings give distinct determinant monomials.

Let $\Gamma=M[N,F]$, of rank $h$, and define $E_h$ by summing $u_I$ over its nonsingular $h$-row submatrices. The coefficient space
\[
 K=\ker(\Gamma^{\mathsf T})
\]
has dimension two. For every $i\in N$ such that $\Gamma[N\setminus\{i\},F]$ has rank $h$, choose a nonzero vector $k_i$ spanning the line
\[
 K\cap\{x_i=0\}.
\]
If this intersection has dimension two, omit the index $i$: the corresponding $h+1$ rows cannot cover all the forced columns. Define the virtual row and its activity by
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:virtual}
 r_i'=k_i^{\mathsf T}M[N,Y\setminus F],\qquad
 w_i=u_{N\setminus\{i\}}/E_h.
\end{equation}
Retain the original rows outside $N$, their activities, and the columns outside $F$. Together with the virtual rows these form $M'$. Nonzero rescaling of a virtual row does not change any minor's zero/nonzero status.

For retained $i,j$, the kernel lines $k_i,k_j$ are independent exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:dualminor}
 \det\Gamma[N\setminus\{i,j\},F]\ne0.
\end{equation}
Indeed, their intersection is the kernel supported on that $h$-row complement. It is zero exactly when the complement is nonsingular. A nonsingular $h$-row set exists, so the retained lines span $K$.

Partition supports by their selected neighbors in $N$. Exactly $h$ selected neighbors contribute $E_h$ times the unchanged outside support polynomial. Exactly $h+1$ neighbors, $N\setminus\{i\}$, reduce after eliminating the forced columns to the one virtual row $r_i'$. Their activity is recovered because $E_hw_i=u_{N\setminus\{i\}}$.

When all $h+2$ neighbors are selected, any independent kernel pair is a basis of $K$. Changing that basis multiplies a residual determinant by a nonzero scalar. All such pairs therefore have the same residual nonzero-minor profile, including the possibility that this profile is identically zero. Their total assigned activity is
\begin{align}
 \sum_{\substack{i<j\\k_i,k_j\text{ independent}}}w_iw_j
 &=\frac{u_N}{E_h^2}
   \sum_{\substack{i<j\\\det\Gamma[N\setminus\{i,j\},F]\ne0}}
        u_{N\setminus\{i,j\}}\notag\\
 &=\frac{u_N}{E_h}.\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:pairweights}
\end{align}
Thus the prefactor $E_h$ recovers $u_N$ exactly once. Dependent kernel pairs and all triples of virtual rows contribute zero. This proves~\eqref{mrn:s:rb:eq:kernelidentity}.

The independence in~\eqref{mrn:s:rb:eq:pairweights} is independence of the kernel vectors, not necessarily of their image rows. Independent kernel vectors can have dependent images in a rank-deficient residual matrix; then their common determinant profile is zero, and the identity remains valid.

Finally, projecting the original row space onto the $F$ coordinates has rank $h$. Its kernel is spanned by the outside rows and the combinations from $K$. The retained virtual lines span $K$, so deleting the zero $F$ coordinates leaves rank $\rank M-h=\nu(G)-h$.
\end{proof}

For a single degree-three forced vertex, with forced-column entries $\gamma_1,\gamma_2,\gamma_3$ at its neighbors, the virtual rows can be written as
\[
 \gamma_2r_1-\gamma_1r_2,\quad
 \gamma_3r_1-\gamma_1r_3,\quad
 \gamma_3r_2-\gamma_2r_3.
\]
Their respective activities are $u_1u_2/E_1,u_1u_3/E_1,u_2u_3/E_1$, with $E_1=u_1+u_2+u_3$. Their pair-activity sum is $u_1u_2u_3/E_1$, giving a concrete instance of~\eqref{mrn:s:rb:eq:pairweights}.

\subsection{Forcing exterior vertices in a genuine cover}
Write a bipartite graph as $X=A\sqcup L$, $Y=B\sqcup R$, with $A\cup B$ a minimum vertex cover, $|A|=a$ and $|B|=q$. Call the displayed cover genuine when $\nu(G)=a+q$. There are no $L$--$R$ edges. Any maximum matching then consists of an $a$-matching $A\to R$ and a disjoint $q$-matching $L\to B$: an edge inside the cover would consume two cover vertices and prevent attaining its size.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:s:rb:thm:cover}
Suppose $a\ge3$ and the displayed $a+q$ cover is genuine. Let $F\subseteq R$ be any matchable set of $h=a-2$ vertices. For arbitrary positive activities on both shores,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:conditionedULC}
 P_G^{\langle F\rangle}(t)=C_Ft^hQ_F(t),
\end{equation}
where $C_F>0$ and $Q_F$ has actual degree $q+2$ and is $\ULC_{q+2}$. Consequently the entire conditioned sector is $\ULC_{a+q}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The neighborhood $N(F)$ is contained in $A$ and has size $h,h+1$, or $h+2$. Slack zero deletes $h$ core-left vertices and leaves a genuine $2+q$ cover. Slack one leaves one unchanged core-left vertex and one virtual union vertex, again giving a genuine $2+q$ cover. A generic matrix of either graph has a rank-two exterior-column block and total rank $q+2$, so Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:matrix} applies.

In slack two, $N(F)=A$. The generic exterior block originally has rank $a$, and its $h$ forced columns are independent. Eliminating those columns reduces its rank to $a-h=2$. The total compressed rank is $a+q-h=q+2$. Thus Theorems~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:kernel} and~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:matrix} give~\eqref{mrn:s:rb:eq:conditionedULC}, with the compressed object interpreted as a regular-minor matrix.

Multiplication by $t^h$ weakens the required Newton ratios. At coefficient index $j$ of a degree-$d$ polynomial, $d=q+2$, the required ratio changes from
\[
 \frac{(j+1)(d-j+1)}{j(d-j)}
 \quad\hbox{to}\quad
 \frac{(j+h+1)(d-j+1)}{(j+h)(d-j)}.
\]
The latter is smaller. This proves ULC at order $d+h=a+q$ for the shifted sector.
\end{proof}
\mnote{Compare source~23's Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:rs:thm:rooted}, which forces all of $B$ and exterior-\emph{left} vertices instead.} Further forcing is a coefficientwise limit at the same fixed normalization. In particular, for a genuine $3+3$ cover, conditioning on any exterior-right vertex leaves $t$ times a degree-five ULC polynomial, for arbitrary fields on both shores. Every further conditioned sector containing such a vertex is safe. Among three-right forced sets, only the all-$B$ set escapes this reduction. This does not prove the original polynomial is ULC at arbitrary finite activities.

\subsection{Eventual strictness and an explicit threshold}
\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:s:rb:thm:eventual}
Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:cover}, multiply just the activities in $F$ by a common $\lambda>0$, keeping all other activities fixed. The full, unconditioned polynomial is strictly $\ULC_{a+q}$ for all sufficiently large $\lambda$. The threshold depends on the fixed graph and activities.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $h=|F|\ge1$, $d=q+2$, and $r=h+d$. Write $p_k(\lambda)$ for the full coefficient. For $0\le k\le h$,
\[
 p_k(\lambda)=b_k\lambda^k+O(\lambda^{k-1}),
\]
where $(b_k)$ is the coefficient sequence of the graph restricted to right shore $F$. It has rank $h$ and is $\ULC_h$. One can see this directly from the usual basis matroid on its original row vectors and private coordinate elements in dimension $h$; positive specialization gives its degree-$h$ homogenization. For $0\le j\le d$,
\[
 p_{h+j}(\lambda)=c_j\lambda^h+O(\lambda^{h-1}),
\]
where $(c_j)$ is a positive scalar multiple of the $\ULC_d$ sequence in Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:rb:thm:cover}. All $b_k,c_j$ are positive, and $b_h=c_0$.

For $k<h$, the coefficient of $\lambda^{2k}$ in the order-$r$ gap is at least
\[
 \frac{(k+1)d}{h-k}\,b_{k-1}b_{k+1}>0,
\]
by the stronger $\ULC_h$ inequality. At $k=h$, the square has order $\lambda^{2h}$ while the adjacent product has at most order $\lambda^{2h-1}$; the leading coefficient is $hdc_0^2>0$. Above it, at $k=h+j$ with $1\le j<d$, $\ULC_d$ gives a leading coefficient at least
\[
 \frac{h(d-j+1)}{j}\,c_{j-1}c_{j+1}>0
\]
at order $\lambda^{2h}$. Every gap polynomial therefore has a positive leading coefficient. There are finitely many gaps, proving eventual strictness.
\end{proof}
For an explicit coefficient bound, write each gap as
\[
 G_k(\lambda)=L_k\lambda^{m_k}+\sum_{j<m_k}g_{kj}\lambda^j,
 \qquad L_k>0,
\]
and set
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:rb:eq:threshold}
 \Lambda=1+\max_k\frac{\sum_{j<m_k}\max\{-g_{kj},0\}}{L_k}.
\end{equation}
For every $\lambda\ge\Lambda$, the leading term dominates all negative terms, since $\lambda\ge1$ and
\[
 G_k(\lambda)\ge\lambda^{m_k-1}
 \left(L_k\lambda-\sum_{j<m_k}\max\{-g_{kj},0\}\right)>0.
\]
This is a field-dependent bound, not a uniform numerical threshold. With exact numerical activities, it is directly computable from the finitely many coefficient polynomials.

\subsection{Verification and scope}
Run \texttt{python3 checks/run\_all.py}. \mnote{Shipped as \path{code/60-rank-two-blocks-run_all.py} and \path{code/60-rank-two-blocks-check_*.py}, outputs \path{data/60-rank-two-blocks-*}; rerun on a copy of the delivered layout (README).} The package independently checks 32 rank-two-block basis identities, 32 degree-three contractions, 50 general kernel compressions with $h=1,2,3,4$, and 200 graph conditioning identities. The kernel cases include omitted and parallel kernel lines. Further exact checks verify all 8,433 positive minors in the rank-three scope example and the leading coefficients and explicit threshold on 36 genuine covers with positive integer activities. The universal proofs are the determinant-sector, kernel, and leading-coefficient arguments above; finite examples are regression checks.

The regular-minor matrix after slack-two compression must not be replaced by its entry-support graph. Its linear dependencies are part of the construction. Likewise, order $q+2$ is actual-rank ULC only when the total matrix rank is $q+2$. The conditioned and eventual conclusions do not assert ULC at every finite choice of positive activities on the original graph. In particular, the general rank-six one-shore question, including its all-core-right cubic limit, remains unresolved here. \mnote{Within the batch, at matching number six with one weighted shore, gap one holds by \prp's Theorem~180.2, gap two by source~05 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:05}) and gap five by source~13 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:13}), at every activity; gaps three and four are open in general. The all-core-right cubic limit is source~21's theorem (Section~\ref{mrn:src:21}).}

The note uses the matroid Lorentzian theorem and the regular-minor viewpoint of bimatroids~\cite{b:BH,b:RU}; it makes no exhaustive literature-priority claim. The arguments and exact verifiers received independent mathematical review. They are not formal theorem-prover verification or external peer review.

%% ======================== source 10 ========================
\section{Lorentzian marginals on three vertices and star cores, both shores weighted (source~10)}\label{mrn:src:10}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~10, as delivered.} For a bipartite graph with three vertices on one shore, fix arbitrary positive
activities on the other shore. The degree-three homogenization of its selected-vertex
marginal is Lorentzian. The proof combines rank-three matroid Rayleigh inequalities
with an exact positive-coefficient certificate for one Hessian determinant.
An endpoint-set vertex-sum identity then proves weighted rank-normalized ultra
log-concavity for every genuine $3+q$ cover whose core is a star centered on the
three-vertex shore. In particular, both star orientations of a $3+3$ cover satisfy
all five rank-six inequalities for arbitrary positive activities on both shores.
No general rank-three-block recombination theorem is asserted.

\subsection{The selected-vertex marginal}
Let $G=(X,Y)$ be bipartite, with $X=\{1,2,3\}$, and give each $y\in Y$ an
activity $v_y>0$. For $I\subseteq X$, define
\[
 c_I=\sum_{\substack{J\subseteq Y,\ |J|=|I|\\
                    G[I,J]\text{ perfectly matchable}}}\prod_{y\in J}v_y,
 \qquad c_\varnothing=1.
\]
Each pair of endpoint sets is counted once, regardless of the number of
perfect matchings realizing it. Write $c_i,c_{ij},c_{123}$ for the corresponding
coefficients.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:s:tv:thm:marginal}
The homogeneous cubic
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:tv:eq:H}
 H(s,z_1,z_2,z_3)=s^3+\sum_i c_i z_i s^2+
       \sum_{i<j}c_{ij}z_i z_j s+c_{123}z_1z_2z_3
\end{equation}
is Lorentzian.
\end{theorem}

For a homogeneous polynomial with nonnegative coefficients and $M$-convex
support, the Lorentzian criterion is that each quadratic coordinate derivative
has a Hessian with at most one positive eigenvalue \cite[Theorem 2.25]{b:BH}.
For a cubic these are its first coordinate derivatives. We verify them directly.
Positive left activities can subsequently be introduced by nonnegative
linear substitution.

\subsection{Three Rayleigh inequalities}
First suppose that every $c_I$ is positive. Represent the right vertices by
columns whose entries at graph edges are algebraically independent, and adjoin
the three private coordinate columns $e_1,e_2,e_3$. This gives a rank-three
transversal matroid. A generic minor is nonzero exactly when its endpoints
are matchable, since distinct matchings give distinct determinant monomials.

Every rank-three matroid is Rayleigh \cite[Theorem 1.1]{b:Wagner}. Set the private
variable at $i$ to zero, retain the other two private variables, and specialize
all right variables to their activities. For $\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}$, its basis
polynomial is
\[
 c_{123}+x_j c_{ik}+x_k c_{ij}+x_jx_k c_i.
\]
The Rayleigh inequality, extended to zero specializations by continuity, gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:tv:eq:rayleigh}
 c_{ij}c_{ik}\ge c_i c_{123}.
\end{equation}
The nonzero Hessian block of $\partial_{z_i}H$ is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 2c_i&c_{ij}&c_{ik}\\ c_{ij}&0&c_{123}\\c_{ik}&c_{123}&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its determinant is $2c_{123}(c_{ij}c_{ik}-c_i c_{123})\ge0$.
Its lower principal block has one positive and one negative eigenvalue.
Interlacing and the determinant therefore imply that the full block has at
most one positive eigenvalue.

\subsection{The remaining Hessian}
The Hessian of $\partial_sH$ is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 6&2c_1&2c_2&2c_3\\
 2c_1&0&c_{12}&c_{13}\\
 2c_2&c_{12}&0&c_{23}\\
 2c_3&c_{13}&c_{23}&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Eliminating its positive first pivot reduces the assertion to $Q\succeq0$,
where
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:tv:eq:Q}
 Q_{ii}=\frac23c_i^2,\qquad
 Q_{ij}=\frac23c_ic_j-c_{ij}\quad(i\ne j).
\end{equation}
The one- and two-dimensional principal minors are nonnegative:
$0\le c_{ij}\le c_ic_j$ gives
$-\frac13c_ic_j\le Q_{ij}\le\frac23c_ic_j$.
We prove the remaining determinant inequality by an exact finite identity.

Group right vertices by their nonempty neighborhoods
$S\subseteq\{1,2,3\}$. Let
\[
 m_S=\sum_{y:N(y)=S}v_y,\qquad
 h_S=\sum_{y:N(y)=S}v_y^2.
\]
For distinct $i,j$, put
\[
 s_{ij}=\sum_{S\supseteq\{i,j\}}m_S,\qquad
