Put $a=\#\{i:v_i=2\}$ and $b=\#\{i:v_i=1\}$. Distribute the singly occurring exterior variables by a vector $e$ with $0\le e_i\le o_i$, and the $b$ core singleton variables by an integer $h\in[0,b]$. The two factors have exterior counts $d+e,d+o-e$ and core counts $a+h,a+b-h$, with multiplicity
\[
 \binom bh\prod_{i=1}^3\binom{o_i}{e_i}.
\]
Use~\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:Tnested} for the two support coefficients, and retain the distributions of first-factor degree $k$ with multiplier $k(6-k)$ and those of degree $k-1$ with multiplier $-(k+1)(7-k)$. Subtract the corresponding $T^0$ product. This gives the coefficient in Lemma~\ref{mrn:s:ne:lem:cert} with no activity specialization.

The file \path{data/exact_certificate.json} contains all 3,391 coefficient vectors, scaled by 36 and listed in ascending powers of $z$. They are integer vectors because each $T$ is a sum of $\binom{z+3}{h}$ with $h\le3$. Every entry is nonnegative; the numbers of nonzero vectors at gaps $2,3,4,5$ are respectively $132,495,532,152$. The standard-library verifier reconstructs all vectors from~\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:hall}--\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:Tnested}, compares them to the stored certificate, and checks their signs. An independent literal matching enumeration checks 80 small weighted graphs.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:ne:thm:nested}]
Lemma~\ref{mrn:s:ne:lem:cert} and $\ULC_6$ of $Q$ prove gaps two through five. The first gap holds for every positive weighted bipartite support polynomial; here it gives $5p_1^2\ge12p_2$. \mnote{This is \prp's Theorem~180.2; source~63's one-sentence Motzkin--Straus justification (edge-disjointness graph, clique number the matching rank, two-matchings at least size-two supports) is the route printed in Section~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:firstgap}.}

The cover $A\cup B$ has size six. The assumed three-matching $A\to R$ and a disjoint three-matching $L\to B$ attain that size. The degree is therefore six, as required.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An obstruction to the full marginal approach}
With unit activities on $X$, define the homogeneous right marginal of degree six by
\[
 F_G(s,z)=\sum_{\substack{J\subseteq Y\\|J|\le6}}
 \#\{I\subseteq X:(I,J)\text{ feasible}\}\;z_Js^{6-|J|}.
\]
If $F_G$ were Lorentzian, all positive one-shore specializations would be $\ULC_6$. The converse is not asserted, and the stronger property already fails in the irreducible $3+3$ class.

\begin{proposition}\label{mrn:s:ne:prop:barrier}
There is a 20-vertex graph of matching rank six with a unique minimum vertex cover, of split $3+3$, for which $F_G$ is not Lorentzian.
\end{proposition}
\mnote{Added 1 October 2026, batch~73: three-tail obstructions of another kind,
on a thirteen-vertex graph, are in source~80 (Part~V,
Sections~\ref{mrn:v:tt:sec:graph13} and~\ref{mrn:v:tt:sec:identify}).}
\begin{proof}
Use three-bit masks $1,2,4$ for the first, second and third core vertices. The left-core neighborhoods in $B$ are $(2,0,5)$. The seven exterior-left neighborhoods in $B$ are
\[
 (3,6,5,4,3,1,4),
\]
and the seven exterior-right neighborhoods in $A$ are
\[
 (2,5,5,5,1,4,7).
\]
There are no other edges. Each exterior block satisfies $|N(S)|>|S|$ for every nonempty subset of its three core vertices, so it has a three-matching. Also, omitting any nonempty set of core vertices from a cover requires more exterior vertices than were omitted. Thus $A\cup B$ is the unique minimum cover, and the matching rank is six.

Differentiate $F_G$ in all three $B$ variables and the variable of the first exterior-right vertex, whose neighborhood is $\{A_2\}$. This forces $A_2$ into the support. Of the six remaining right variables, four are then common neighbors of $A_1,A_3$, and two are private neighbors, one at each. Their support coefficients are exactly
\[
 \text{constant }64,\quad
 \text{each common singleton }74,\quad
 \text{each private singleton }42,\quad
 \text{each pair }27.
\]
These integers can be obtained by enumerating left subsets of sizes four, five and six and applying Hall's criterion. The accompanying verifier independently obtains them by dynamic enumeration of matchable left endpoint sets; it also enumerates all size-six covers.

Set the four common variables equal to $x$ and both private variables equal to $y$. The resulting quadratic is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:ne:eq:badquad}
 64s^2+(296x+84y)s+27(6x^2+8xy+y^2).
\end{equation}
At $x=t,y=19t$, this becomes
\[
 64s^2+1892st+14013t^2,
 \qquad 1892^2-4\cdot64\cdot14013=-7664<0.
\]
A nonnegative bivariate quadratic is Lorentzian exactly when its middle coefficient squared is at least four times the product of its endpoint coefficients. Derivatives and nonnegative linear substitutions preserve Lorentzian polynomials~\cite{b:BH}. Hence $F_G$ cannot be Lorentzian.
\end{proof}
This is not a scalar $\ULC_6$ counterexample. A quadratic sector forced to degrees $4,5,6$ needs the weaker ratio $12/5$, whereas the displayed sector's ratio is close to four. The result specifically rules out universal full-marginal Lorentzianity as a proof route.

%% --- inlined from source 63 file conditioning.tex ---
\subsection{Every four right vertex sector is safe}
\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:s:ne:thm:conditional}
Let $G$ have matching rank six and a displayed minimum cover $A\cup B$ with $|A|=|B|=3$. Give all left vertices activity one and all right vertices arbitrary positive activities. Force four designated right vertices by scaling their activities together and taking the leading coefficient of degree four in the scaling parameter. Suppose the resulting sector is
\[
 t^4(c_0+c_1t+c_2t^2),\qquad c_0c_2>0.
\]
Divide out the fixed positive product of the four forced right activities. Then $c_1^2\ge3c_0c_2$. Thus its last rank-six Newton gap is strictly positive, since the required constant is $12/5$.
\end{theorem}
The theorem concerns these conditioned sectors. It does not assert ULC at arbitrary finite activities, and it does not address cubic sectors obtained by forcing only three right vertices.

\subsubsection{Hall tight subfamilies}
If the forced set contains $S$ with $T=N(S)$, $|T|=|S|=h\ge1$, and a perfect matching between $T$ and $S$, every support containing $S$ contains all of $T$, matched to $S$. Deleting both endpoint sets gives a coefficient-preserving correspondence apart from the fixed factor $u_Tv_St^h$.

The remaining graph has rank $6-h$. Indeed, replacing all edges incident to $T$ in a maximum matching by a matching from $T$ to $S$ cannot decrease size. The weighted rank-at-most-five theorem applies to the remaining graph~\cite{b:m74}. \mnote{Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive}.} Force the other $4-h$ right vertices and take a coefficientwise limit at this fixed normalization. Its last gap gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:ne:eq:tightbound}
 c_1^2\ge\frac{2(6-h)}{5-h}c_0c_2\ge\frac52c_0c_2,
 \qquad 1\le h\le4.
\end{equation}
This includes a forced exterior vertex with a singleton neighborhood, two forced exterior vertices with a two-vertex combined neighborhood, and any three matchable exterior-right vertices.

\subsubsection{Two core vertices and two exterior vertices}
This case permits arbitrary positive activities on both shores. Let $J\subseteq B$ be the forced pair, and let $z$ be the missing-$B$ activity. Let $E$ be the sum of the activity products of the $A$-pairs that match the two forced exterior-right vertices, and put $a=\prod_{i=1}^3u_{A_i}$. Let $S$ be the union of the core neighborhoods of the possible unmatched $A$ rows, namely those whose complementary $A$-pair matches the two forced exterior vertices. Define weighted exterior-left counts
\begin{align*}
 b&=\#\{L\text{-pairs matching }J\},&
 c&=\#\{L\text{-triples matching }B\},\\
 V&=\#\{L\text{-singletons completing }J\text{ with row }S\},&
 U&=\#\{L\text{-pairs completing }B\text{ with row }S\}.
\end{align*}
Here each count includes the product of the selected left activities. Let $W$ be the total activity of remaining exterior-right vertices that complete the forced exterior pair to a matching of all of $A$. Partitioning supports by whether they select two or three vertices of $A$ gives
\[
 c_0=Eb+aV,\quad
 c_1=(Ec+aU)z+abW,\quad
 c_2=ac zW.
\]
In the three-$A$ case the common product $a$ makes the virtual union row exact: an endpoint support is counted once even if several unmatched rows work. Eligibility in $W$ depends only on the exterior-right triple.

Take the rank-three transversal matroid on the exterior-left elements, the virtual row $S$, and a private element at the missing-$B$ slot. After the exterior-left activity substitution, its basis polynomial in the last two variables is $c+Ux+by+Vxy$. Every rank-three matroid is Rayleigh~\cite[Theorem 1.1]{b:Wagner}, so $bU\ge cV$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:ne:eq:rayleighsector}
 c_1^2-4c_0c_2=[(Ec+aU)z-abW]^2+4a^2zW(bU-cV)\ge0.
\end{equation}
This stronger bound settles the two-core/two-exterior case.

\subsubsection{A useful quadratic inequality}
Suppose remaining right elements have parallel-class activity totals $x_1,\ldots,x_m$ and a total $g$ of other elements, each forming an independent pair with every other distinct element. Put $w=g+\sum x_i$. Suppose their linear costs are $q-\delta_i$ on the parallel classes and $q$ on the other elements, where $\delta_i\ge0$ and $\sum\delta_i\le q$. If a pair of independent right elements has coefficient $T>0$, then
\[
 c_1=qw-\sum_i\delta_ix_i,\qquad
 c_2\le\frac{T}{2}\left(w^2-\sum_ix_i^2\right).
\]
The missing term in the second formula is the nonnegative sum of squared individual activities in the generic group. Set $D=q^2-\sum_i\delta_i^2$. Ordinary Cauchy--Schwarz, or the reverse inequality for the quadratic form $z_0^2-\sum z_i^2$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:s:ne:eq:cauchy}
 c_1^2\ge D\left(w^2-\sum_ix_i^2\right)\ge\frac{2D}{T}c_2.
\end{equation}
For a direct check, replace $\sum\delta_ix_i$ by $\|\delta\|\|x\|$. Subtracting $D(w^2-\|x\|^2)$ from $(qw-\|\delta\|\|x\|)^2$ leaves $(q\|x\|-\|\delta\|w)^2$.

\subsubsection{All three core vertices and a degree three exterior vertex}
All left activities are now one. Let the forced exterior vertex have neighborhood $A$. Remove isolated exterior-left vertices and let $N$ be the remaining number. Write $T$ for the number of exterior-left triples matching $B$; $T\ge1$. For each core row $A_i$, let $\mathcal P_i$ be the family of exterior-left pairs completed to $B$ by $A_i$. Put
\[
 W=|\mathcal P_1\cup\mathcal P_2\cup\mathcal P_3|,
 \quad a_i=|\mathcal P_i\setminus(\mathcal P_j\cup\mathcal P_k)|,
 \quad A_0=\sum_i a_i\le W.
\]
Let $V$ count exterior-left singletons completed to $B$ by some two core rows. Exact endpoint counting gives
\[
 C:=c_0=3T+3W-A_0+V.
\]
The three singleton-neighborhood classes of remaining right vertices have costs $2T+W-a_i$. Every right vertex with neighborhood size at least two has cost $q=3T+W$. Any pair completes the forced exterior vertex to a matching of $A$, except two vertices in the same singleton class. Thus~\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:cauchy} applies with $\delta_i=T+a_i$, since $\sum\delta_i\le q$.

We show $D\ge(3/2)CT$. The exterior-left rank-three matroid has $N$ nonloops. A fixed basis and one distinct replacement basis for every other element show $T\ge N-2$. Since $V\le N$, we have $V\le T+2$. Expanding and using $A_0\le W$ yields
\begin{align*}
 D-\tfrac32CT
 &=\tfrac32T^2+\tfrac32TW-\tfrac12TA_0
   +W^2-\sum_i a_i^2-\tfrac32TV\\
 &\ge T(W-3)+W^2-\sum_i a_i^2\ge0
 \qquad(W\ge3).
\end{align*}

For the small-$W$ boundary, represent the transversal matroid generically and let $F$ be the span of the three core-row vectors. If $\dim F\le1$, then $V=0$, and
\[
 D-2CT=W^2-\sum_i a_i^2-2TV\ge0.
\]
If $\dim F\ge2$, choose a generic vector $e\in F$, avoiding all $N$ exterior-left directions. Contraction by $e$ gives a rank-two matroid on these $N$ nonloops, with exactly $W$ bases: a pair extends $e$ exactly when at least one core row extends it. Hence $W\ge N-1$. If $W\le2$, necessarily $N=3,T=1,W=2$. The sole exterior-left pair not extending $e$ spans $F$, which is two-dimensional. Exactly one exterior-left vector is outside $F$, so $V=1$. Consequently
\[
 D-\tfrac32CT=7-A_0/2-\sum_i a_i^2\ge7-1-4>0.
\]
Together with~\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:cauchy}, this proves $c_1^2\ge3c_0c_2$.

\subsubsection{All three core vertices and a lower degree exterior vertex}
Relabel its neighborhood as $\{A_1,A_2\}$. Let $U$ count exterior-left pairs completed by a virtual row with neighborhood $N(A_1)\cup N(A_2)$, let $P$ count those completed by $A_3$, and let $W$ count those completed by at least one of these two rows. Put
\[
 O=U+P-W,\qquad u=U-O,\qquad p=P-O.
\]
Let $V$ count exterior-left singletons completed by those two rows. With $T$ as before,
\[
 C=c_0=2T+U+2P+V=2T+3W-2u-p+V.
\]
The remaining right vertices have two parallel classes: neighborhoods contained in $\{A_1,A_2\}$, of cost $T+P$, and neighborhood $\{A_3\}$, of cost $2T+U$. All other nonempty neighborhoods have cost $q=3T+W$. Apply~\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:cauchy} with $\delta_1=2T+u$, $\delta_2=T+p$. Expansion gives
\[
 D=2CT+(2UP-O^2)-2TV.
\]
The previously proved sharp rank-three two-row inequality is
\[
 4UP-2O^2\ge3TV;
\]
apply it to the virtual union row and $A_3$~\cite{b:m74}. \mnote{Source~74's inequality~\eqref{mrn:w:rf:eq:leftkey} (Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:three}).} This inequality allows arbitrary positive exterior-left activities. It gives
\[
 D\ge2CT-TV/2\ge\tfrac32CT,
\]
because $C\ge V$. Again $c_1^2\ge3c_0c_2$ follows.

If the forced exterior vertex has degree one, remove it and its forced left neighbor. Two unit core-left rows remain. With the same two-row counts $T,U,P,V,O$, put $W=U+P-O$, $u=U-O$, and $p=P-O$. This time
\[
 C=T+U+P+V,\quad q=2T+W,\quad
 \delta_1=T+u,\quad\delta_2=T+p.
\]
The exact axis costs are $T+P$ and $T+U$, and the generic cost is $q$. The same expansion again gives
\[
 D=2CT+(2UP-O^2)-2TV\ge\tfrac32CT.
\]
Thus the constant three holds in this case as well.

\begin{proof}[Completion of Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:ne:thm:conditional}]
A size-six matching uses all of $A\cup B$ and no core edge. Four feasible forced right vertices contain one, two or three exterior-right vertices. Three are Hall-tight with $h=3$, giving constant three in~\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:tightbound}; two are handled by~\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:rayleighsector}; one has degree one, two or three and is covered by the corresponding argument above. These cases exhaust the claim.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Verification.}
Run \texttt{python3 checks/run\_all.py}. \mnote{Shipped as \path{code/63-nested-exterior-run_all.py}; \path{data/exact_certificate.json} above is \path{data/63-nested-exterior-exact_certificate.json}.} The package uses only Python's standard library and contains the complete nested certificate, independent endpoint checks, and an exact verification of Proposition~\ref{mrn:s:ne:prop:barrier}, and weighted checks of~\eqref{mrn:s:ne:eq:rayleighsector}. The proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:s:ne:thm:conditional} is analytic; the finite experiments are not its justification. It contains no numerical searches or internal review reports. These are computer-assisted mathematical proofs, not formal theorem-prover verification or claims of exhaustive priority.

\clearpage
\part{Eventual one-shore scaling through matching number eight}\label{mrn:part:four}

\paragraph{Contents of Part~IV.}
All sources of Part~IV keep every left activity equal to one, fix positive
right activities, fix a minimum cover $A\sqcup B$, and multiply the
activities of its \emph{right} part $B$ by a common factor $\lambda$; their
conclusions hold for all sufficiently large $\lambda$, with a threshold
depending on the graph, the cover and the field. Forcing $B$ gives a
conditional polynomial $C(t)$ whose Newton gaps, shifted to the actual
matching number, decide the leading coefficients. Source~08s
(Section~\ref{mrn:src:08s}), delivered only inside archive~08, is the base:
a top conditional gap for every $a+b$ cover, a two-plane incidence lemma,
eventual $\ULC$ for $3+3$, $3+4$ and $3+5$ covers, and a reduction of rank
seven to one middle gap. Source~21 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:21}) is its
matching-number-six case with further detail; sources~43, 38, 35, 32 and~29
(Sections~\ref{mrn:src:43}--\ref{mrn:src:29}) prove the same rank-six
theorem for special cores and are printed as second routes. Source~08
(Section~\ref{mrn:src:08}) settles the rank-seven middle gap, source~03
(Section~\ref{mrn:src:03}) the $5+3$ covers, source~02
(Section~\ref{mrn:src:02}) the $4+4$ top gap and source~01
(Section~\ref{mrn:src:01}) the $4+4$ middle gap, so that every graph with
$\nu\le8$ is eventually $\ULC_\nu$ under this scaling.

\paragraph{The two scalings and the two brackets.}
Part~I's failures at every matching number $\ge6$
(Section~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:negative}) scale the whole minimum cover, on both
shores; Part~IV scales its right part only, and there no failure occurs
through matching number eight. The least matching number of a
\emph{persistent} failure under right-cover scaling lies in $[9,38]$
(source~01; the upper end is source~55's $H_{8,30;32,794}$); sources~21 and~08s
give $[7,38]$ and source~08 $[8,38]$, kept as history. The least matching
number of a one-shore failure at some \emph{finite} activity lies in
$[6,38]$; its lower end rests on Part~I.

\paragraph{Same results in several sources.}
Source~21's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main} is the case $a=b=3$ of source~08s's
Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top} (with source~08s's constant $75/28$ equal to
source~21's refinement), its incidence lemma is the case $b=3$ of source~08s's
Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:tg:lem:plane} (printed with proof in source~13's section,
Part~III), and its Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:ec:cor:scaling} is the case $b=3$ of
source~08s's Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}. The eventual theorems of
sources~43, 38, 35, 32 and~29 are cases of source~21's; their proofs are
printed as second routes, with what each adds. Source~08s's weighted local
equality lemma is the necessity half of source~72's contraction criterion
(Part~I); its appendix proof is a pointer. The every-field branches of
sources~38, 35, 32 and~29 (a coloop, or a single exterior-left neighbour)
are corollaries of sources~74 and~72.

\paragraph{Non-claims of Part~IV.}
No source of Part~IV proves $\ULC$ at every finite activity at matching
numbers six to eight, or a uniform threshold over graphs or fields;
source~08s proves no middle-gap bound and no counterexample (source~08 later
proves the bound); source~03 and source~02 do not claim a complete
rank-eight theorem; source~02's unrestricted upper incidence bound is false
(its Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:ff:lem:counter}); source~08s's numerical search is
discovery history, not evidence for a uniform inequality.

%% ======================== source 08s ========================
\section{Top conditional gaps and the rank-seven reduction, one weighted shore, right-cover scaling (source~08s)}\label{mrn:src:08s}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~08s, as delivered.} We prove a uniform top conditional Newton inequality for a bipartite graph with an $a+b$ minimum cover, unit left activities and arbitrary positive right activities. Its constant is $a(b+2)^2/((a-1)(b^2+b+2))$. A two-plane incidence inequality in rank $b$ supplies the geometric input; elementary extension counts are the only use of unit left activities. The result proves eventual strict ultra-log-concavity for all $3+4$ and $3+5$ covers. At rank seven it also excludes the top gap of a $4+3$ cover. A separate weighted equality argument handles the six-exterior-left boundary. Any eventual rank-seven failure must consequently occur at the middle conditional quartic gap, with at least nineteen nonisolated vertices. No such counterexample or uniform middle-gap bound is proved here.
\mnote{Source~08s was delivered only inside archive~08 (\path{Rank7_Eventual_Report/companion/structural_companion_source.zip}); it is a manuscript of its own and the base of Part~IV. Its uniform middle bound, which the abstract says is not proved here, is source~08's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r7:thm:middle}.}

\subsection{Coefficients and conclusions}
For a bipartite graph with shores $X,Y$ and positive vertex activities, define
\[
 P_G(t)=\sum_{\substack{I\subseteq X,\ J\subseteq Y\\|I|=|J|,\ G[I,J]\text{ perfectly matchable}}}u_Iv_Jt^{|I|}.
\]
A feasible endpoint pair is counted once, irrespective of its number of perfect matchings. For matching rank $d$, write $P_G=\sum p_jt^j$ and
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:gaps}
 G_j=j(d-j)p_j^2-(j+1)(d-j+1)p_{j-1}p_{j+1}.
\end{equation}
The condition $G_j\ge0$ at every $1\le j<d$, together with no internal zeros, is denoted $\ULC_d$.

Assume
\[
 X=A\sqcup L,\qquad Y=B\sqcup R,\qquad |A|=a,\quad |B|=b,
 \qquad \nu(G)=a+b,
\]
where $A\sqcup B$ is a vertex cover and $a,b\ge2$. There are no $L$--$R$ edges. Every maximum matching avoids core edges and gives exterior matchings of sizes $b$ and $a$. Remove isolated vertices and put $n=|L|\ge b$.

Give every left vertex activity one and every right vertex an arbitrary positive activity. Force all vertices of $B$ into the selected right endpoint set and divide out $v_B=\prod_{y\in B}v_y$. The conditional polynomial is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:conditional}
 t^bC(t),\qquad C(t)=\sum_{j=0}^a C_jt^j,
\end{equation}
with all $C_j>0$. In \emph{common cover scaling}, all original $B$ activities are multiplied by the same parameter $\lambda>0$.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:tg:thm:top}
Under these hypotheses,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:top}
 C_{a-1}^2\ge
 \frac{a(b+2)^2}{(a-1)(b^2+b+2)}\,C_{a-2}C_a.
\end{equation}
No assumption on the core matrix's rank or support is needed.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{At $a=b=3$ the constant is $75/28$, which is source~21's refinement (Remark after Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}); source~21's stated constant there is $21/8$.}

\begin{corollary}\label{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}
For $a=3$ and $b\in\{3,4,5\}$, $P_G$ is strictly $\ULC_{b+3}$ for all sufficiently large common $B$ scalings. The threshold may depend on the graph and the fixed right field. In particular this holds for every genuine $3+4$ and $3+5$ cover.
\end{corollary}
\mnote{At $b=3$ this is source~21's Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:ec:cor:scaling}, and so it contains the eventual theorems of sources~43, 38, 35, 32 and~29 for special cores (Sections~\ref{mrn:src:43}--\ref{mrn:src:29}). The scaling multiplies the activities of the \emph{right} part $B$ of the cover only; see the entry ``Scalings'' of Section~\ref{mrn:sec:notions}.}

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:tg:thm:rankseven}
For a rank-seven graph and a specified minimum cover, an eventual failure under common right-cover scaling can occur only for a $4+3$ cover. In that case all leading gaps except the middle conditional one are strictly positive. The unresolved middle expression is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:middle}
 5C_2^2-9C_1C_3.
\end{equation}
A strictly negative value is sufficient for eventual failure; if it is zero, lower powers of $\lambda$ must still be examined.

Every eventual rank-seven counterexample has $|L|\ge7$, $|R|\ge5$, and an $R$ vertex with at least two $A$ neighbors. Hence it has at least nineteen nonisolated vertices. The six-exterior-left equality boundary cannot give a counterexample.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{The middle expression is settled by source~08 (Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r7:thm:middle}): $C_2^2\ge\frac{15}{8}C_1C_3>\frac95C_1C_3$ for every genuine $4+3$ cover. Hence there is no eventual rank-seven failure (source~08's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r7:thm:global}).}

The cover splits with one shore of size at most two are covered by the previously established small-cover theorem \cite[Theorem 1.1 and Section 2]{b:m74} \mnote{source~74's Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main} and Section~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:lorentz}}; the present argument addresses the new $3+4$ and $4+3$ cases. We use standard Lorentzian facts: matroid basis polynomials are Lorentzian, and nonnegative substitutions, directional derivatives and coefficientwise limits preserve this property. Their quadratic Hessians have at most one positive eigenvalue \cite{b:BH}.

\subsection{A two-plane incidence inequality}
\begin{lemma}\label{mrn:e:tg:lem:plane}
Let $E$ be a rank-$b$ represented configuration over an infinite field, with positive element weights, and let $H$ be a two-dimensional subspace of its ambient row space. Let $q$ be the weighted basis count and $\beta$ the weighted count of $(b-2)$-subsets complementary to $H$. Let $h$ count independent $(b-1)$-subsets $P$ whose hyperplanes do not contain $H$. Write $h_\delta$ for their weights grouped by the line $\delta=\spn(P)\cap H$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:plane}
 h^2-\sum_\delta h_\delta^2\ge\frac{b}{b-1}q\beta.
\end{equation}
Repeated and parallel elements are permitted and remain separately labeled.
\end{lemma}
\mnote{At $b=3$ this is source~21's Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:ec:lem:incidence}, printed with source~13's proof as~\eqref{mrn:s:lg:eq:incidence}, and the lower bound of source~08's Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:r7:lem:bounds}; at $b=4$ it is the lower incidence bound that sources~02 and~01 use.}
\begin{proof}
The case $\beta=0$ is immediate. Otherwise adjoin one vector on every occurring line $\delta$, with variable $x_\delta$, and many generic vectors in $H$ of total activity $y$, each of weight $y/N$. Mark original elements by $t$. Passing to the coefficientwise limit $N\to\infty$ gives the Lorentzian basis polynomial
\begin{align}
 F={}&qt^b+hyt^{b-1}+\frac\beta2 y^2t^{b-2}\notag\\
 &+\sum_\delta x_\delta\bigl((h-h_\delta)t^{b-1}+\beta yt^{b-2}\bigr)
 +\beta t^{b-2}\sum_{\delta<\eta}x_\delta x_\eta.\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:diffuse}
\end{align}
Three added vectors are dependent because all lie in $H$. Two on distinct lines form a basis with exactly the original $(b-2)$-sets complementary to $H$. A fixed added line completes precisely those original hyperplanes whose intersection line is different. These facts give every coefficient in \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:diffuse}. Generic additions may be made over a transcendental extension.

Apply $\partial_t^{b-2}/(b-2)!$. The resulting Hessian has entries
\[
 tt=b(b-1)q,\quad ty=(b-1)h,\quad t\delta=(b-1)(h-h_\delta),
\]
\[
 yy=\beta,\quad y\delta=\beta,\quad\delta\delta=0,
 \quad\delta\eta=\beta\quad(\delta\ne\eta).
\]
Eliminating the positive $yy$ pivot leaves line block $-\beta I$, cross entries $-(b-1)h_\delta$, and $tt$ entry $b(b-1)q-(b-1)^2h^2/\beta$. Its remaining Schur complement is nonpositive, so
\[
 b(b-1)q-\frac{(b-1)^2}{\beta}h^2
 +\frac{(b-1)^2}{\beta}\sum_\delta h_\delta^2\le0.
\]
This is \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:plane}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The exact top sectors}
Represent each graph edge by an algebraically independent nonzero entry. A square minor is nonzero exactly when its endpoints are matchable, since distinct matchings yield distinct determinant monomials. Write $K$ for the $a\times b$ core matrix, $\ell_i$ for the exterior-left rows and $r_j$ for the exterior-right columns. They have exterior ranks $b$ and $a$ respectively.

Let $q$ be the unweighted number of independent $b$-subsets of $L$. For every independent $(b-1)$-subset $P$, choose a nonzero annihilating column $v_P$ and introduce the virtual right vector
\[
 w_P=Kv_P.
\]
These virtual elements remain separately labeled, including loops and parallel vectors. Form the rank-$a$ matroid on $R$, the virtual elements, and the private coordinate basis $E$ in the $A$ space. Mark these three classes by $t,z,s$ respectively, keeping the original positive $R$ activities. Define the following weighted basis counts:
\begin{align*}
 T &: a\text{ elements of }R, &
 A_1 &: (a-1)R+1E,\\
 U &: (a-2)R+2E, &
 P_B &: 1\text{ virtual}+(a-1)R,\\
 P_V &:1\text{ virtual}+(a-2)R+1E, &
 D &:2\text{ virtual}+(a-2)R.
\end{align*}
Pairs of virtual elements in $D$ are unordered.

For an independent labeled $(a-2)$-subset $J\subseteq R$, let $\beta_J$ count $(b-2)$-subsets $I\subseteq L$ for which the square block
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:block}
 \begin{pmatrix}K&R_J\\L_I&0\end{pmatrix}
\end{equation}
is nonsingular. Put $N=\sum_J v_J\beta_J$. Dependent $J$ contribute zero and can be omitted.

Partitioning endpoint sets by their selected $L$ cardinality gives exactly
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:sectors}
 C_{a-2}=qU+P_V+N,\qquad C_{a-1}=qA_1+P_B,
 \qquad C_a=qT.
\end{equation}
At the first of these degrees, the possible $L$ sizes are $b,b-1,b-2$; at the next they are $b,b-1$; at the last only $b$ occurs. Eliminating the chosen $L$ rows gives the stated virtual column. Complementary private coordinates record the selected $A$ rows. Thus the partition counts endpoint sets once, with no allocation multiplicity.

Differentiate the right basis polynomial $a-2$ times in $t$ and divide by $(a-2)!$. Its Hessian in $(t,s,z)$ is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:rightH}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 a(a-1)T&(a-1)A_1&(a-1)P_B\\
 (a-1)A_1&2U&P_V\\
 (a-1)P_B&P_V&2D
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
The positive $tt$ pivot and the Schur inequality on $(s,z)=(q,1)$ imply
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:rightbound}
 C_{a-1}^2\ge\frac{2a}{a-1}T\bigl(q(C_{a-2}-N)+D\bigr).
\end{equation}

\subsection{Quotient geometry and the unit counts}
Fix an independent $(a-2)$-set $J\subseteq R$ and quotient the right space by $\spn(R_J)$. The induced core map is a $2\times b$ matrix $K_J$. If its rank is below two, both $\beta_J$ and the corresponding contribution $D_J$ to $D$ vanish.

Otherwise $H=\operatorname{row}(K_J)$ is a two-plane in the $b$-dimensional left row space. Row reduction along $R_J$ identifies \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:block} with the condition that $L_I$ be complementary to $H$. Moreover $K_Jv_P=0$ exactly when $H\subseteq\spn(P)$. For a surviving $P$, restriction of its annihilator to $H$ has kernel $H\cap\spn(P)$. Consequently two virtual images are independent in the quotient exactly when their intersection lines differ. Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:tg:lem:plane} gives
\[
 2D_J=h^2-\sum_\delta h_\delta^2\ge\frac{b}{b-1}q\beta_J.
\]
The factor two is essential: the left side counts ordered virtual pairs, while $D_J$ counts unordered pairs. Summing over $J$ with its product activity proves
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:D}
 D\ge\frac{b}{2(b-1)}qN.
\end{equation}
This treats every core rank, including singular cores.

Now use unit left activities. Every complementary $(b-2)$-set extends to an original basis, which contains at most $\binom b2$ such subsets. It also has at least two distinct one-element extensions to independent $(b-1)$-sets. Every such extension belongs to $h$: a hyperplane containing both the set and $H$ would contain their full $b$-dimensional sum. Each hyperplane subset contains at most $b-1$ of the original complementary sets. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:unit}
 q\ge\frac{2\beta_J}{b(b-1)},\qquad
 h\ge\frac{2\beta_J}{b-1}.
\end{equation}
For fixed $J$, its coefficient in $U$ is at least one because private coordinates complete it to a basis. Every surviving virtual vector is completed with $J$ by at least one private coordinate, so its coefficient in $P_V$ is at least $h$. From \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:sectors} and \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:unit},
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:N}
 C_{a-2}\ge\left(1+\frac{2(b+1)}{b(b-1)}\right)N
 =\frac{b^2+b+2}{b(b-1)}N.
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:rightbound}, \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:D} and \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:N} gives
\begin{align*}
 C_{a-1}^2
 &\ge\frac{2a}{a-1}qT\left(C_{a-2}-\frac{b-2}{2(b-1)}N\right)\\
 &\ge\frac{a(b+2)^2}{(a-1)(b^2+b+2)}C_{a-2}C_a,
\end{align*}
proving Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top}. The incidence inequality itself allows weighted left elements; \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:unit} is where the unit-field hypothesis enters.

\subsection{Scaling and the cubic consequences}
We record two standard matroid specializations to fix the normalization. For a graph with exactly $b$ vertices on the right, take the transversal matroid matching left vertices and one private element per right vertex into those $b$ slots. Give original left elements activity $tu_i$ and private element $j$ activity $s/v_j$, then multiply by $\prod_jv_j$. The rank-$b$ basis polynomial becomes
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^b b_jt^js^{b-j},
\]
where $b_j$ are exactly its endpoint-support coefficients. Thus it is $\ULC_b$; if the graph has rank $b$, every coefficient is positive.

For the conditional polynomial \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:conditional}, instead use the extended transversal matroid in the $n+a$ left coordinates and differentiate in all $B$ elements. Specialization gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:ambient}
 s^{n-b}\sum_{j=0}^a C_js^{a-j}t^j,
\end{equation}
Lorentzian of degree $d_0=n+a-b$. In particular
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:first}
 C_1^2\ge\frac{2d_0}{d_0-1}C_0C_2>2C_0C_2.
\end{equation}
This ambient estimate permits arbitrary positive left activities as well. \mnote{At $a=b=3$ it is source~21's ambient lower gap~\eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:ambient}.}

Under common $B$ scaling, the leading coefficient of $p_j(\lambda)$ for $j\le b$ is $b_j\lambda^j$, where $b_j$ comes from the graph restricted to right shore $B$. Its rank is $b$ by the exterior matching. For $j\ge b$, the leading term is $v_BC_{j-b}\lambda^b$. At $j<b$, the required actual-rank factor
\[
 \frac{(j+1)(a+b-j+1)}{j(a+b-j)}
\]
is strictly smaller than the pure-shore factor $(j+1)(b-j+1)/(j(b-j))$. The leading gaps are therefore strictly positive. At $j=b$, the square has degree $2b$ and the neighboring product degree at most $2b-1$.

For $a=3$, the two remaining required factors are
\[
 \frac{3(b+2)}{2(b+1)},\qquad \frac{2(b+3)}{b+2}.
\]
The first is below two for $b\ge3$, and the top theorem gives constants
\[
 \frac{75}{28}\ (b=3),\qquad \frac{27}{11}\ (b=4),
 \qquad \frac{147}{64}\ (b=5).
\]
At $b=4,5$ their margins above the required factors are respectively $4/33$ and $5/448$. More generally the sign of the top margin is the sign of $b(-b^2+2b+16)$, positive for $2\le b\le5$.
Every gap polynomial has positive leading coefficient. Hence all are positive for sufficiently large $\lambda$, proving Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}. This is a field-dependent eventual statement, not a uniform numerical cutoff.

\subsection{The rank seven boundary}
For $a=4,b=3$, \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:first} exceeds the required first conditional factor $5/3$. The top theorem gives
\[
 C_3^2\ge\frac{50}{21}C_2C_4>\frac73C_2C_4,
\]
with margin $1/21$. Only \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:middle} remains. The degree $d_0=n+1$ ambient bound at index two is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:ambientmiddle}
 C_2^2\ge\frac{3n}{2(n-1)}C_1C_3.
\end{equation}
It is strictly stronger than $9/5$ for $n\le5$, and equal to that constant at $n=6$. A zero leading gap alone would not settle the eventual sign, so this boundary requires its own argument.

We use the following weighted equality implication. Its unweighted form is the local characterization of Chan and Pak \cite[Theorem 1.3]{b:CP}; a proof of the weighted form needed here is included in the appendix.
\begin{lemma}\label{mrn:e:tg:lem:strict}
Let a positively weighted matroid have rank $d$, with red set $R$ and blue complement. Suppose its grouped normalized basis counts attain equality at index $j$, with positive neighboring counts. Then there is a common $\lambda>0$ such that every independent $(d-2)$-set $I$ containing $j-1$ red elements has the following property: in every nonloop parallel class of $M/I$, red weight is $\lambda$ times blue weight. In particular, a monochromatic nonloop parallel class in one such contraction forces strictness for every positive field.
\end{lemma}
\mnote{Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:tg:lem:strict} is the necessity half of source~72's weighted contraction criterion, Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:weighted} (Part~I), with the same proof; see the note in the appendix below.}

Suppose $n=6$. Contract the three $B$ columns in the extended transversal matroid on $R$ and the private left coordinates. This has rank seven; color $R$ red and all private elements blue. Its grouped polynomial is
\[
 C_0s^7+C_1s^6t+C_2s^5t^2+C_3s^4t^3+C_4s^3t^4.
\]
Choose three $L$ rows forming an exterior basis. All four private $A$ coordinates and the three complementary private $L$ coordinates form a blue basis after contracting $B$.

If a red vertex $u$ has at least two $A$ neighbors, extend it to an $R$ basis and choose a perfect matching of that basis to $A$. Name its assigned coordinate $i$, and choose another neighbor $j$. Choose one of the other two red basis elements assigned outside $i,j$, say $u_h$. Contract $u_h$, the private $A$ coordinate at the remaining fourth position, and the three chosen private $L$ coordinates. This independent five-set has one red and four blue elements. The images of the uncontracted coordinates $A_i,A_j$ remain independent: the contracted red element has a nonzero generic entry at its assigned coordinate $h$.

The contraction and every blue vector are independent of all generic entries of $u$. Its two entries at $i,j$ are independent parameters multiplying independent quotient coordinates. For any nonloop blue vector $w$, the determinant of $u,w$ is a nonzero polynomial in those parameters, because its two coefficients cannot both vanish. Thus $u$ belongs to a red-only nonloop parallel class. Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:tg:lem:strict} proves strictness in \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:ambientmiddle} when $n=6$.

If every $R$ vertex instead has a singleton neighborhood, compress each nonempty $A$-leaf class to one right vertex of summed activity. This preserves every endpoint coefficient, since at most one vertex of such a class can be selected. Exterior rank four guarantees all four classes are nonempty. The compressed graph has exactly seven right vertices and matching rank seven. The pure-shore theorem proves $\ULC_7$ at every field and every common scaling. \mnote{The compression is \prp's Lemma~206.1 and the pure-shore theorem its Theorem~70.1 (Lemma~\ref{mrn:w:rf:lem:small} here).}

It follows that a rank-seven eventual failure requires a $4+3$ cover, $n\ge7$ and a multi-neighbor $R$ vertex. Also $|R|\ge5$: otherwise the full right shore has at most seven vertices and the same pure-shore theorem applies. There must therefore be at least $(4+7)+(3+5)=19$ nonisolated vertices. Together with the previously proved small-cover case, this proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:rankseven}.

%% (source appendix follows)

\subsection{Appendix: The weighted local equality implication}
\mnote{Source~08s's appendix proves Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:tg:lem:strict} by exactly the argument of source~72's necessity proof for Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:weighted} (Section~\ref{mrn:src:72}): equality gives $Hz=0$ for $z=f-\lambda g$, the decomposition below propagates $H_iz=0$ to every allowed contraction, and at rank two the kernel equation is the class-ratio condition. The argument is printed once, in source~72's section, and source~08s's text of it is not reprinted. In source~08s's notation, $F$ is the unweighted basis polynomial, $f,g$ carry the red and blue weights, $H=\operatorname{Hess}(D_f^{j-1}D_g^{d-j-1}F)$ and, for $d\ge3$, $j\ge2$ and each red nonloop $i$, $H_i=\operatorname{Hess}(D_f^{j-2}D_g^{d-j-1}\partial_iF)$; its key decomposition is}
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tg:eq:kernel}
 H=\sum_{i\in R}w_iH_i,\qquad (Hz)_i=z^TH_if,
\end{equation}
\mnote{source~72's~\eqref{mrn:w:se:eq:inherit}. Source~08s closes by noting that this proves Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:tg:lem:strict} without a continuity claim about strict inequalities or an assertion that equality propagates to every index of the original matroid.}

\subsection{Appendix: Exact checks and supplementary searches}
The included standard-library checker \mnote{shipped as \path{code/08-rank-seven-top-gap-*.py} with outputs \path{data/08-rank-seven-top-gap-*}} verifies the two-plane and unit-count inequalities on $320$ represented configurations of ranks three through six. A separate endpoint-set computation agrees with the compressed profile algorithm in $400$ exact coefficient comparisons and checks $100$ instances of the general top-gap bound. The displayed test graphs have explicit exterior bases, so their actual ranks equal their cover sizes. These finite regressions are not used in place of the proof.

\mnote{The search below is discovery history: source~08 later proved the middle bound. Its NumPy/SciPy scripts and logs are shipped as \path{code/08-rank-seven-top-gap-supplement-search*.py}, \path{data/08-rank-seven-top-gap-supplement-run_*.log} and \path{data/08-rank-seven-top-gap-search_summary.json}; they were not rerun at placement and are not part of any proof.} The search was narrowed to \eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:middle}. It tested $2000$ ordinary configurations, $1000$ highly uneven unit-left clone configurations, $120$ configurations with a heavy and diffuse right population in each nonsingleton class, and $600$ embeddings based on a corrected rank-four transversal Rayleigh example. None produced a negative candidate. The smallest numerically optimized ratio $C_2^2/((9/5)C_1C_3)$ was approximately $1.2456$. This is neither a certified global minimum nor evidence sufficient for a uniform inequality.

For reproducibility, the printed presentation in Choe--Wagner \cite[Proposition 5.9]{b:CW} was independently counted by dynamic matching and Hall's criterion on all $495$ four-subsets. It has $309$ bases, $69$ containing $e$, $147$ containing $f$ and $33$ containing both, giving Rayleigh difference $-54$. These correct the printed counts for that presentation and still give a negative Rayleigh example. They do not give a shifted Newton counterexample. \mnote{This correction of Choe--Wagner's printed counts is source~08s's own statement; it was not checked against the publication in this merge. The shipped \path{data/08-rank-seven-top-gap-rayleigh_seed_checks.json} records that the printed counts were not reproduced.}

The search logs and exact regression data are supplementary. The persistent-failure rank bracket remains $[7,38]$. \mnote{Superseded within the batch: $[8,38]$ by source~08 and $[9,38]$ by source~01 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:01}).} The unresolved mathematical target is the $4+3$ middle conditional gap and, at its equality points, the lower-order scaling coefficients. No exact counterexample, universal middle bound, or exhaustive priority assertion is made.

%% ======================== source 21 ========================
\section{Rank-six conditioning for every core, one weighted shore, right-cover scaling (source~21)}\label{mrn:src:21}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~21, as delivered.} For a rank-six bipartite graph with a displayed $3+3$ minimum cover, unit activities on the left shore and arbitrary positive activities on the right shore, we prove both shifted Newton inequalities for the cubic obtained by forcing the three right-cover vertices. A rank-three plane-incidence inequality, proved through a matroid basis polynomial, handles every core matrix, including singular cores. This removes the core-edge restrictions in the earlier conditional results. Consequently every such fixed graph and field is strictly ultra-log-concave after sufficiently large common scaling of the right-cover activities. Arbitrary finite-activity rank-six ultra-log-concavity remains open.
\mnote{The ``earlier conditional results'' whose core-edge restrictions this source removes are sources~43, 38, 35, 32 and~29, printed after it (Sections~\ref{mrn:src:43}--\ref{mrn:src:29}); source~21 does not name them.}

\subsection{The conditional theorem}
For a bipartite graph $G=(X,Y)$ with positive vertex activities, write
\[
 P_G(t)=\sum_{\substack{I\subseteq X,\ J\subseteq Y\\
 |I|=|J|,\ G[I,J]\text{ perfectly matchable}}}u_Iv_Jt^{|I|}.
\]
Each feasible pair of endpoint sets is counted once, regardless of the number of perfect matchings. If the matching rank is $d$, the degree-normalized Newton gaps are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:gap}
 G_k=k(d-k)p_k^2-(k+1)(d-k+1)p_{k-1}p_{k+1}.
\end{equation}
We call $P_G$ $\ULC_d$ when these gaps are nonnegative; its positive-activity coefficients have no internal zeros.

Assume
\[
 X=A\sqcup L,\quad Y=B\sqcup R,\quad
 |A|=|B|=3,\quad \nu(G)=6,
\]
and $A\sqcup B$ is a vertex cover. There are no $L$--$R$ edges. A maximum matching must avoid core edges and consists of exterior matchings of sizes three on both sides. In particular $n=|L|\ge3$ after isolated vertices are removed.

Force the inclusion of all $B$ in the selected right endpoint set, and divide by $v_B=\prod_{b\in B}v_b$. The resulting polynomial is
\[
 t^3C(t),\qquad C(t)=C_0+C_1t+C_2t^2+C_3t^3.
\]
All four coefficients are positive: an exterior-left basis and an exterior-right independent set of each size give the required supports.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}
If every left activity is one, then, for arbitrary positive right activities,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:main}
 C_1^2\ge\frac{2n}{n-1}C_0C_2,\qquad
 C_2^2\ge\frac{21}{8}C_1C_3.
\end{equation}
In particular the two shifted degree-six inequalities are strict:
\[
 8C_1^2>15C_0C_2,\qquad 5C_2^2>12C_1C_3.
\]
The first inequality in \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:main} also holds for arbitrary positive activities on both shores.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{This is the case $a=b=3$ of source~08s's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top} (constant $75/28\ge21/8$, see the Remark below) together with its ambient bound~\eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:first}; source~21's proof is printed as the rank-six case. Source~21 states more than source~08s at $a=b=3$: the first bound with the explicit constant $2n/(n-1)$, also for arbitrary left activities.}

The cubic need not be $\ULC_3$: a connected, unique-cover example fails both degree-three inequalities \cite{b:m23}. \mnote{Source~23's 25-vertex graph, Section~\ref{mrn:s:rs:sec:obstruction}. It fails the unshifted degree-three inequalities only; the shifted inequalities of Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main} hold for it.} The normalization in Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main} is therefore substantive. No hypothesis on the core's edge set, rank or connectedness is imposed.

Our structural input is established Lorentzian theory: matroid basis polynomials are Lorentzian; nonnegative linear substitutions, derivatives and coefficientwise limits preserve this property; and a quadratic Lorentzian polynomial has at most one positive Hessian eigenvalue \cite[Theorems 2.10, 2.25 and 3.10]{b:BH}. The bivariate form is the normalized Newton inequality \cite[Example 2.26]{b:BH}.

\subsection{The ambient lower gap}
Introduce a private element $\bar x$ for each $x\in X$ and use the transversal matroid of rank $n+3$ whose elements are $Y\sqcup\bar X$, matched into $X$. Its bases are exactly
\[
 J\sqcup\{\bar x:x\notin I\}
\]
for the feasible endpoint pairs $(I,J)$. Differentiate its basis polynomial in the three $B$ variables. Set the private variable for $x$ to $s/u_x$, and the variable for $r\in R$ to $tv_r$. Multiplication by $\prod_{x\in X}u_x$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:ambient}
 s^{n-3}(C_0s^3+C_1s^2t+C_2st^2+C_3t^3).
\end{equation}
This is Lorentzian of degree $n$, even for arbitrary positive left activities. The index-one Newton inequality gives the first bound in \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:main}. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:lowerstrict}
 8C_1^2-15C_0C_2\ge\frac{n+15}{n-1}C_0C_2>0.
\end{equation}
The degree here is the ambient left count $n$, not the degree three of $C$. Nevertheless its resulting factor is stronger than the shifted factor $15/8$. \mnote{Source~08s's~\eqref{mrn:e:tg:eq:first} in general.}

\subsection{A plane incidence inequality}
\begin{lemma}\label{mrn:e:ec:lem:incidence}
Let $E$ be a loopless rank-three vector configuration over an infinite field, with positive element activities, and let $H$ be a plane. Let $q$ be the weighted sum of independent triples, $\beta$ the total activity outside $H$, and $h$ the weighted sum of independent pairs not contained in $H$. For each such pair $P$, let
$\delta(P)=\spn(P)\cap H$, a line, and let $h_\delta$ be the total pair activity in that line class. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:incidence}
 h^2-\sum_\delta h_\delta^2\ge\frac32q\beta.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof (pointer)]
This is the case $b=3$ of source~08s's Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:tg:lem:plane}. \mnote{Source~13 recalls this lemma from source~21 with its proof (Section~\ref{mrn:src:13}, display~\eqref{mrn:s:lg:eq:incidence}); that is where it is printed. Source~21's proof is the same: with $p$ the weighted sum of all independent pairs and $g$ those in $H$, so that $h=p-g$, adjoin one vector on each line $\delta$ and $N$ generic vectors in $H$ of activity $y/N$; the limiting basis polynomial is} Taking $N\to\infty$ gives
\begin{align}
 F={}&qt^3+hyt^2+\frac{\beta}{2}y^2t \notag\\
 &+\sum_\delta x_\delta\bigl((h-h_\delta)t^2+\beta yt\bigr)
   +\beta t\sum_{\delta<\eta}x_\delta x_\eta. \label{mrn:e:ec:eq:plane}
\end{align}
\mnote{and the Schur complement of the $\partial_tF$ Hessian at its $yy$ pivot gives~\eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:incidence}, as in source~13's printed proof; not reprinted.}
\end{proof}

Generic vectors may be taken over a transcendental field extension. Only finitely many line classes occur, and the coefficientwise limit is in a fixed polynomial space. Parallel original vectors are allowed. The lemma's weights are unrestricted; unit weights will enter later.

\subsection{Virtual vectors and exact support counts}
Choose a matrix with the graph's zero pattern and algebraically independent nonzero entries over a rational-function field. A square minor is nonzero exactly when its endpoint sets are matchable: distinct matchings produce distinct determinant monomials. Denote the core matrix by $K$, the exterior-left rows in the $B$ coordinates by $\ell_i$, and the exterior-right columns in the $A$ coordinates by $r_j$. Both exterior configurations have rank three. Let $p,q$ count independent pairs and triples of the $\ell_i$.

For every independent pair $P$ of exterior-left rows, choose a nonzero column $v_P$ annihilated by those rows and introduce the virtual right vector
\[
 w_P=Kv_P.
\]
It may be zero; different pairs remain separately labeled even when their virtual vectors are parallel.

Consider the rank-three matroid on the original $r_j$, the three private coordinate vectors $e_1,e_2,e_3$, and the virtual $w_P$. Assign the private vectors activity $s$, the original $r_j$ activity $tv_j$, and each virtual element activity $z$. Let $F_R(s,t,z)$ be its Lorentzian basis polynomial. Define:
\[
\begin{aligned}
 U&=[s^2t]F_R,& a&=[st^2]F_R,& T&=[t^3]F_R,\\
 P_V&=[stz]F_R,& P_B&=[t^2z]F_R,& Q/2&=[tz^2]F_R.
\end{aligned}
\]
For each $j$ let $\beta_j$ count rows $\ell_i$ for which
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:block}
 \begin{pmatrix}K&r_j\\ \ell_i&0\end{pmatrix}
\end{equation}
is nonsingular, and put $N=\sum_jv_j\beta_j$.

Partitioning supports by their number of selected exterior-left vertices gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:sectors}
 C_1=qU+P_V+N,\qquad C_2=qa+P_B,\qquad C_3=qT.
\end{equation}
Indeed, three selected independent exterior-left vertices contribute $q$ times the original right-side basis profiles. Two selected exterior-left vertices can be eliminated; their remaining column is $w_P$. Complementary private coordinate vectors record the selected $A$-row set once. One selected exterior-left vertex contributes to $C_1$ exactly through \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:block}; it cannot contribute to $C_2$ or $C_3$. Zero selected exterior-left vertices contribute only to $C_0$. This is a partition of endpoint supports, with no allocation multiplicity.

The Hessian of $\partial_tF_R$, in coordinates $(t,s,z)$, is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 6T&2a&2P_B\\
 2a&2U&P_V\\
 2P_B&P_V&Q
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Since $T>0$, its Schur complement at $6T$ is negative semidefinite. Evaluate it on $(s,z)=(q,1)$ to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:rightschur}
 C_2^2\ge3qT(qU+P_V)+\frac32TQ
       =3qT(C_1-N)+\frac32TQ.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Every core rank}
Let $Q_j$ count ordered pairs of virtual elements whose vectors together with $r_j$ are independent. Then $Q=\sum_jv_jQ_j$. We prove
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:Qj}
 Q_j\ge\frac32q\beta_j
\end{equation}
by treating the possible ranks of $K$.

\paragraph{Invertible core.}
Put $a_0=K^{-1}r_j$ and $H=\{\ell:\ell a_0=0\}$. The block determinant \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:block} is nonzero exactly when $\ell_i\notin H$, so $\beta_j$ is the exterior-left count outside $H$.
A pair contained in $H$ has $v_P$ proportional to $a_0$, hence is zero after its virtual vector is quotiented by $r_j$. For other pairs, two virtual vectors are independent modulo $r_j$ exactly when their pair spans meet $H$ in distinct lines.

To see this last assertion directly, restriction to $H$ identifies the dual quotient by $\spn(a_0)$ with the dual of $H$. The restriction of $v_P$ has kernel $\spn(P)\cap H$. Nonzero restrictions are proportional exactly when these kernels coincide. Therefore, with the notation of Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:ec:lem:incidence},
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:Qpartition}
 Q_j=(p-g)^2-\sum_\delta h_\delta^2.
\end{equation}
The lemma proves \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:Qj}.

\paragraph{Core rank two.}
Write $\ker K=\spn(a_0)$ and $\operatorname{im}K=P_K$. If $r_j\in P_K$, then $\beta_j=Q_j=0$. If $r_j\notin P_K$, use the same plane $H=\{\ell:\ell a_0=0\}$. Now \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:block} is nonsingular exactly when $\ell_i a_0\ne0$. Moreover $Kv_P,Kv_{P'}$ are independent in $P_K$ exactly when $v_P,v_{P'},a_0$ are independent. Since $r_j\notin P_K$, the same line classification gives \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:Qpartition} and \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:Qj}.

\paragraph{Core rank at most one.}
Every block \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:block} has rank at most three, so $\beta_j=0$. All virtual vectors lie in a subspace of dimension at most one, so $Q_j=0$. This completes the proof of \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:Qj}, including zero and parallel virtual elements.

\subsection{The unit count and the higher gap}
Let $\kappa_j$ be the coefficient of $v_j$ in $C_1$. We claim
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:kappa}
 \kappa_j\ge2\beta_j.
\end{equation}
There is nothing to prove if $\beta_j=0$. Otherwise the plane $H$ in the preceding section exists. Every independent left pair not contained in $H$ gives a virtual vector independent of $r_j$. At least one private coordinate vector completes this pair to a basis. Thus $P_{V,j}\ge p-g$. Also $U_j\ge1$.

For a loopless rank-three configuration of $n$ unit elements,
\[
 q\ge n-2,\qquad p\ge2n-3.
\]
Fix a basis: every other element extends some pair of that basis to a new triple, and is independent with at least two basis elements. These give the displayed bounds without a simplicity or coloop assumption.

If $H$ contains $f\ge1$ original elements, all $f(n-f)$ cross pairs are independent, so
$p-g\ge f(n-f)\ge n-1$. If $f=0$, use $p-g=p\ge2n-3\ge n-1$. Consequently
\[
 \kappa_j=qU_j+P_{V,j}+\beta_j
 \ge(n-2)+(n-1)+\beta_j\ge2\beta_j,
\]
because $n\ge3$ and $\beta_j\le n$. This is the only step using the unit-left convention.

Summing \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:Qj} and \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:kappa} against the positive right activities gives
\[
 Q\ge\tfrac32qN,\qquad C_1\ge2N.
\]
Insert these in \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:rightschur}:
\[
 C_2^2\ge3qTC_1-\frac34qTN
       \ge\frac{21}{8}qTC_1=\frac{21}{8}C_1C_3.
\]
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:ec:eq:upperstrict}
 C_2^2-\frac{12}{5}C_1C_3\ge\frac9{40}C_1C_3>0.
\end{equation}
Together with \eqref{mrn:e:ec:eq:lowerstrict}, this proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}.

\begin{remark}
A small refinement gives $\kappa_j\ge7\beta_j/3$ and hence
$C_2^2\ge75C_1C_3/28$. If $f=0$, then
$q+(p-g)\ge3n-5\ge4n/3$. If $f\ge1$, write $\beta=n-f$ and use
$q+(p-g)\ge(f+1)\beta+f-2\ge4\beta/3$:
for $f=1$, rank three forces $\beta\ge2$; for $f\ge2$ the inequality is immediate.
Adding $\beta$ proves the refinement. No optimality is claimed.
\end{remark}
\mnote{$75/28$ is the constant of source~08s's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top} at $a=b=3$.}

\subsection{Eventual common scaling}
\begin{corollary}\label{mrn:e:ec:cor:scaling}
Fix the graph and positive right activities in Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}. Multiply the three $B$ activities by a common $\lambda$. The full polynomial is strictly $\ULC_6$ for every sufficiently large $\lambda$.
\end{corollary}
\mnote{The case $b=3$ of source~08s's Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}. Sources~43, 38, 35, 32 and~29 prove it for special cores (Sections~\ref{mrn:src:43}--\ref{mrn:src:29}); they are the ``earlier conditional results'' of source~21's abstract.}
\begin{proof}
Let $b_j$ be the weighted support coefficients of the graph induced by all left vertices and $B$, which has rank three. For $j\le3$, the coefficient of $\lambda^j$ in $p_j(\lambda)$ is $b_j>0$. For $k\ge3$, the coefficient of $\lambda^3$ in $p_k(\lambda)$ is $v_BC_{k-3}$, with $b_3=v_BC_0$.

The smaller-shore degree-three transversal basis specialization gives
$b_1^2\ge3b_2$ and $b_2^2\ge3b_1b_3$. Therefore the five leading limits are
\begin{align*}
 \lambda^{-2}G_1&\longrightarrow5b_1^2-12b_2>0,\\
 \lambda^{-4}G_2&\longrightarrow8b_2^2-15b_1b_3>0,\\
 \lambda^{-6}G_3&\longrightarrow9b_3^2>0,\\
 \lambda^{-6}G_4&\longrightarrow v_B^2(8C_1^2-15C_0C_2)>0,\\
 \lambda^{-6}G_5&\longrightarrow v_B^2(5C_2^2-12C_1C_3)>0.
\end{align*}
For $G_3$, the adjacent product has degree at most five. The last two limits follow from Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}.

There is also an explicit coefficient-dominance bound. For each exact gap polynomial
$G_k(\lambda)=a_d\lambda^d+\sum_{j<d}a_j\lambda^j$, with $a_d>0$, it suffices that
\[
 \lambda\ge1+\frac{\sum_{j<d}|a_j|}{a_d}.
\]
Taking the maximum of these five quantities gives a computable, field-dependent threshold.
\end{proof}

For any chosen minimum cover of an arbitrary rank-six graph, either the cover splits $3+3$, or one of its shores has size at most two. The previously established cover theorem handles the latter cases for all positive activities \cite{b:m74}. Thus common scaling of the right part of any chosen minimum cover is eventually safe for every rank-six graph with unit left activities. This global statement is nonstrict, since balanced unions of stars can have equality. \mnote{The cover theorem is Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main}; equality occurs only for balanced star forests (source~72, Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:main}).}

This improves the known bracket for the minimum rank of \emph{persistent failure under this common scaling} to $[7,38]$. \mnote{Superseded within the batch: $[8,38]$ (source~08) and $[9,38]$ (source~01).} The upper witness uses exactly the same convention: the full Hall graph $H_{8,30;32,794}$ has unit left activities, unit exterior-right activities, and common activity $w$ on the right part $B$ of its minimum cover. Its last gap is \cite{b:m55}
\[
 G_{37}=\frac5{6304}D^2w^{58}
 \bigl(-22w^2+6437219280w+11385894392295\bigr),
 \qquad D=\binom{32}{30}\binom{794}{7}.
\]
It is negative for every sufficiently large $w$. The minimum rank for failure at arbitrary finite one-shore fields remains bracketed by $[6,38]$; the present theorem does not decide the rank-six case at finite fields. \mnote{The lower end six rests on source~74 (Part~I). At matching number six and finite fields, gaps one, two and five hold for every one-shore graph (Part~III); gaps three and four are open.}

\subsection{Verification and scope}
The accompanying standard-library exact checker \mnote{shipped as \path{code/21-every-core-*.py}, outputs \path{data/21-every-core-*.json}} verifies 960 unit and 960 weighted plane-incidence instances, matrix instances for all 512 Boolean core masks, and 38 full endpoint-minor enumerations. An independently written checker verifies 600 further weighted plane configurations. The checks use exact arithmetic and no numerical tolerance. These finite regressions supplement the general proof; they are not an exhaustive verification of the theorem's infinitely many exterior configurations. The matrix assignments may contain additional algebraic dependencies, which the incidence proof also permits.

This report gives an ordinary mathematical proof using established Lorentzian theory, with independent internal mathematical review and exact checks. It is not a formal-kernel proof or an externally refereed publication. No exhaustive priority claim is made. The general finite-activity rank-six one-shore question remains open.

%% ======================== source 43 ========================
\section{Second route for the complete core: a rank-six cubic sector, one weighted shore (source~43)}\label{mrn:src:43}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~43, as delivered.} Consider a rank-six bipartite graph with a displayed $3+3$ minimum vertex cover and a complete core. Both exterior neighborhood systems are arbitrary. With unit activities on the left shore and positive activities on the right, we prove strict shifted Newton inequalities for the cubic sector obtained by forcing all three right-core vertices. A generic extension of a rank-three transversal matroid supplies three coupled Hessian inequalities; these control the two required gaps using ordinary log-concavity of the exterior-left independence counts. Consequently sufficiently large common scaling of the right-core activities gives strict rank-six ultra-log-concavity. The threshold may depend on the graph and fields.

\paragraph{Scope.}
This is a conditional-sector and eventual-scaling theorem. It does not prove ultra-log-concavity at arbitrary finite activities, and it does not yet cover a noncomplete $3\times3$ core. \mnote{Every core is covered by source~21 and, more generally, source~08s.} The general rank-six one-shore problem remains open. The argument is an ordinary proof supported by exact checks, not a formal proof-assistant development or an external priority claim.

\subsection{The conditional cubic}
Let $G$ have shores $C\sqcup L$ and $D\sqcup R$, where
\[
 |C|=|D|=3,\qquad \nu(G)=6.
\]
The displayed cover is $C\sqcup D$, so there are no $L$--$R$ edges. Assume every edge of $C\times D$ is present. The exterior graphs $L\to D$ and $C\to R$ are arbitrary. All left activities are one, and the right activities are arbitrary positive real numbers.

The matching-support polynomial counts each feasible pair of endpoint sets once, regardless of its number of perfect matchings. Condition on selecting every vertex of $D$, and divide out the fixed product of their activities. The resulting polynomial has the form
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:cubic}
 t^3(c_0+c_1t+c_2t^2+c_3t^3).
\end{equation}
\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:cc:thm:main}
Under these hypotheses all four $c_i$ are positive, and
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:targets}
 8c_1^2>15c_0c_2,\qquad 5c_2^2>12c_1c_3.
\end{equation}
Thus the conditional cubic satisfies the two nontrivial shifted $\ULC_6$ inequalities strictly.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{Same theorem as source~21 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:21}), restricted to the complete core $K_{3,3}$: its eventual statement is the case of that core in source~21's Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:ec:cor:scaling}, and its two conditional inequalities ($8C_1^2>15C_0C_2$, $5C_2^2>12C_1C_3$) follow from source~21's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}, whose constants $2n/(n-1)$ and $21/8$ are larger and which needs no hypothesis on the core; both are the case $a=b=3$ of source~08s's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top} and Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}. Source~43's proof is printed as a second route. What it adds: the closed support partition of Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:cc:lem:counts}, an elementary lower-gap proof by six quadratic forms that uses only $p^2\ge nq$, and an explicit higher margin.}

A matching of size six uses no core edge, so both exterior graphs have matching rank three. Delete isolated exterior vertices. Let
\[
 n=I_1,\qquad p=I_2,\qquad q=I_3
\]
be the counts of independent exterior-left subsets in their rank-three transversal matroid on slots $D$. In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:left}
 n\ge3,\quad p\ge n,\quad q\ge1,\quad p^2\ge nq.
\end{equation}
For the pair bound, fix a basis. Every other nonloop forms an independent pair with at least two basis elements. Hence $p\ge3+2(n-3)=2n-3\ge n$. The final inequality in \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:left} is ordinary log-concavity of matroid independence counts. The stronger established Mason inequality is
\[
 p^2\ge \frac{3n(n-1)}{2(n-2)}q.
\]
Its normalization uses the \emph{ground-set size} $n$, not the matroid rank three \cite[Theorem 4.14]{b:BH}. \mnote{Source~43's bibliography also lists \cite{b:ALOV} as an independent proof of that stronger independence-count theorem.}

For the exterior-right graph on the three slots $C$, use the following weighted counts:
\begin{itemize}
 \item $U,A,T$ are its matching-support coefficients of orders one, two, three;
 \item $W,B$ count its independent right subsets of sizes one and two;
 \item $V$ counts pairs consisting of a two-element subset of $C$ and one right vertex, with at least one incidence.
\end{itemize}
The weight of a right subset is the product of its activities. Thus $U$ counts incidences, whereas $W$ counts nonloop right vertices only once. A singleton-neighborhood right vertex contributes twice its activity to $V$; a vertex with at least two neighbors contributes three times. Genuine rank six ensures $W,T>0$.

\begin{lemma}[Exact support partition]\label{mrn:e:cc:lem:counts}
The coefficients in \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:cubic} are
\begin{align}
 c_0&=q+3p+3n+1,&
 c_1&=qU+pV+nW,\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:counts}\\
 c_2&=qA+pB,&
 c_3&=qT.\notag
\end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $j$ exterior-right vertices are selected, together with $i$ core-left vertices. The exterior-left set must have size $3+j-i$ and must be independent into $D$. The selected right set must match into the selected core-left set. These two conditions are sufficient: after matching those exterior vertices, the complete core matches the remaining $i-j$ selected core-left vertices into the remaining slots of $D$.

For $j=0$, the four core-left sizes give $q,3p,3n,1$. For $j=1$, core-left sizes one, two, three give $qU,pV,nW$. For $j=2$, sizes two and three give $qA,pB$. For $j=3$, only size three is possible, giving $qT$. Every support is counted once.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Three coupled inequalities from one matroid}
Take the rank-three transversal matroid on the right exterior vertices and three private coordinate elements. The private elements form a basis. Assign activity $s$ to each private element and $t$ times its fixed activity to each exterior-right element.

Represent this transversal matroid by a generic three-row matrix. Add $N$ generic elements in the full rank-three flat, each with activity $z/N$. One new element extends every independent original pair; two extend every original nonloop; three form a basis. Their basis polynomial specializes to
\begin{align*}
 F_N={}&s^3+Us^2t+Ast^2+Tt^3+z(3s^2+Vst+Bt^2)\\
 &+\frac{N-1}{2N}z^2(3s+Wt)
   +\frac{(N-1)(N-2)}{6N^2}z^3.
\end{align*}
The coefficient $V$ here is the private/right independent-pair count: a singleton-neighborhood element is independent with two private elements, and any element with at least two neighbors is independent with all three.

Matroid basis polynomials are Lorentzian, and this property is preserved by nonnegative linear substitutions and coefficientwise limits \cite{b:BH}. Therefore the cubic
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:F}
 \begin{split}
 F={}&s^3+Us^2t+Ast^2+Tt^3\\
 &+z(3s^2+Vst+Bt^2)+\frac{z^2}{2}(3s+Wt)+\frac{z^3}{6}
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
is Lorentzian. No real-stability or general Rayleigh assertion is needed.

\begin{lemma}[Coupled Hessian bounds]\label{mrn:e:cc:lem:hessian}
The following matrix is positive semidefinite:
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:PSD}
 \begin{pmatrix}
  A^2-3UT&AB-\frac32VT\\
  AB-\frac32VT&B^2-\frac32WT
 \end{pmatrix}\succeq0.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:upper}
 A\le A_*:=\frac{U^2-(V-2U)^2/2}{3},\qquad
 B\le B_*:=\frac{W^2-(V-3W)^2/3}{2}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Every first derivative of $F$ is a Lorentzian quadratic, so its Hessian has at most one positive eigenvalue. In variable order $(s,t,z)$, the three Hessians are
\[
 \nabla^2\partial_tF=
 \begin{pmatrix}2U&2A&V\\2A&6T&2B\\V&2B&W\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 \nabla^2\partial_sF=
 \begin{pmatrix}6&2U&6\\2U&2A&V\\6&V&3\end{pmatrix},
\]
\[
 \nabla^2\partial_zF=
 \begin{pmatrix}6&V&3\\V&2B&W\\3&W&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
For the first matrix, take the Schur complement of its positive $t,t$ entry $6T$. That complement is negative semidefinite; multiplication by $-3T/2$ gives \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:PSD}. For the second matrix, complement its $s,s$ entry $6$. The resulting determinant condition is
\[
 2(U^2-3A)-(V-2U)^2\ge0.
\]
For the third, complement the $z,z$ entry $1$, obtaining
\[
 3(W^2-2B)-(V-3W)^2\ge0.
\]
These are exactly \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:upper}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The two shifted Newton gaps}
\subsubsection{The higher gap}
Set $X=A^2-3UT$, $Y=B^2-\frac32WT$, and $Z=2AB-3VT$. By \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:PSD},
\[
 Q=q^2X+qpZ+p^2Y\ge0.
\]
Using \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:counts}, an exact rearrangement gives
\begin{align*}
 c_2^2-\frac{12}{5}c_1c_3
 =Q+\frac35q^2UT+\frac35qpVT
       +\frac32p^2WT-\frac{12}{5}nqWT.
\end{align*}
Each nonloop right vertex has at least one neighbor, and intersects at least two two-slot subsets. Hence $U\ge W$ and $V\ge2W$. Together with \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:left}, these give
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:secondstrict}
 c_2^2-\frac{12}{5}c_1c_3
 \ge\frac3{10}qWT(2q+4p-3n)>0.
\end{equation}
Indeed $p\ge n$ and all of $n,q,W,T$ are positive.

\subsubsection{The lower gap}
Let $x,y,z_0$ be the total activities of right vertices with exactly one, two, three neighbors. Then
\[
 U=x+2y+3z_0,\qquad V=2x+3y+3z_0,\qquad W=x+y+z_0.
\]
Put $S=q+3p+3n+1$ and $\ell=qU+pV+nW$. By \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:upper},
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:firstlower}
 8c_1^2-15c_0c_2\ge8\ell^2-15S(qA_*+pB_*).
\end{equation}
The following six coefficient forms give an explicit positive certificate:
\begin{align*}
 P_{xx}&=8n^2+17np+nq+17p^2+12pq-5p+3q^2-5q,\\
 P_{xy}&=16n^2+35np-12nq+51p^2+37pq-15p+12q^2-20q,\\
 P_{xz}&=16n^2+35np-26nq+51p^2+39pq-15p+18q^2-30q,\\
 P_{yy}&=16n^2+51np-41nq+99p^2+72pq-15p+29q^2-35q,\\
 P_{yz}&=16n^2+51np-55nq+99p^2+90pq-15p+51q^2-45q,\\
 P_{zz}&=16n^2+51np-39nq+99p^2+138pq-15p+99q^2-45q.
\end{align*}
Direct expansion yields
\begin{align}\label{mrn:e:cc:eq:six}
 2\bigl[8\ell^2-15S(qA_*+pB_*)\bigr]
 ={}&2P_{xx}x^2+2P_{xy}xy+2P_{xz}xz_0\notag\\
 &+P_{yy}y^2+2P_{yz}yz_0+P_{zz}z_0^2.
\end{align}
Every $P$ is strictly positive for $n,p,q\ge1$ and $p^2\ge nq$. In the last five forms, absorb the negative $nq$ term into $p^2$. The remaining $p^2$ coefficients are, respectively,
\[
 39,\quad25,\quad58,\quad44,\quad60.
\]
Group each negative linear $p$ term with $np$ and each negative linear $q$ term with $pq$; both groups remain positive for $n,p\ge1$. The same grouping works directly for $P_{xx}$, whose $nq$ term is already positive. Since $x+y+z_0=W>0$, at least one square term in \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:six} is positive. This proves the first inequality in \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:targets} and completes Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:cc:thm:main}.

\subsection{Eventual strictness and a computable threshold}
\begin{corollary}\label{mrn:e:cc:cor:scaling}
Fix a graph and positive right fields satisfying the theorem. Multiply all three right-core activities by a common factor $\lambda$. For every sufficiently large $\lambda$, the full matching-support polynomial is strictly $\ULC_6$. The threshold may depend on the graph and the fixed relative fields.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Let $a_1,a_2,a_3>0$ be the leading coefficients at orders one, two, three when the common factor is introduced. They are the support coefficients of the induced graph on the three right-core vertices and all left vertices. The weighted smaller-shore theorem gives
\[
 a_1^2\ge3a_2,\qquad a_2^2\ge3a_1a_3.
\]
Consequently the leading coefficients of the first two rank-six gaps are
\[
 5a_1^2-12a_2\ge3a_2>0,\qquad
 8a_2^2-15a_1a_3\ge9a_1a_3>0,
\]
at powers $\lambda^2$ and $\lambda^4$. At the third gap, the square has degree six in $\lambda$, whereas the adjacent product has degree at most five, so its leading coefficient is $9a_3^2>0$.

Let $\gamma$ be the product of the three fixed right-core activities. At orders three through six, the leading coefficients are $\gamma c_0,\ldots,\gamma c_3$. Thus the last two gap-leading coefficients are
\[
 \gamma^2(8c_1^2-15c_0c_2)>0,\qquad
 \gamma^2(5c_2^2-12c_1c_3)>0,
\]
both at degree six. Every gap is therefore eventually positive.
\end{proof}

There is an elementary field-dependent numerical prescription. Write each gap as
\[
 \Delta_k(\lambda)=a_{k,d_k}\lambda^{d_k}
                 +\sum_{j<d_k}a_{k,j}\lambda^j,
 \qquad a_{k,d_k}>0.
\]
Then all gaps are positive whenever
\[
 \lambda\ge1+\max_{1\le k\le5}
     \frac{\sum_{j<d_k}|a_{k,j}|}{a_{k,d_k}}.
\]
This bound is computable from the support coefficients; no uniform threshold is asserted.

\subsection{Verification and the remaining frontier}
The proof uses the established matroid basis theorem and ordinary independence-count log-concavity. Its new step is the simultaneous use of three Hessian consequences of the auxiliary cubic to control the forced support sector.

The source package \mnote{shipped as \path{code/43-complete-core-verify_complete_core.py} and \path{data/43-complete-core-*}} includes exact symbolic verification of \eqref{mrn:e:cc:eq:six} and the Schur-complement matrix, together with $120$ deterministic direct conditional-support enumerations using arbitrary exterior neighborhoods and positive integer right fields. These are supplementary checks of the identities; the population and activity quantifiers follow from the proof.

The complete-core hypothesis is used in Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:cc:lem:counts}. With a missing core edge, the left completion counts depend on which core vertices remain available, so the three numbers $n,p,q$ no longer suffice. A flag of minor or extension profiles is a possible next route, but no noncomplete-core theorem is claimed here. The full finite-activity rank-six one-shore question remains open. \mnote{See the notes at the scope paragraph and after Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:cc:thm:main}; the finite-activity status is in Part~III's introduction.}

%% ======================== source 38 ========================
\section{Second route for matching-hole cores, one weighted shore (source~38)}\label{mrn:src:38}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~38, as delivered.} We extend the complete-core conditional theorem to rank-six bipartite graphs whose displayed $3+3$ core is obtained from $K_{3,3}$ by deleting a matching of up to three edges. Exterior neighborhoods are arbitrary, left activities are one, and right activities are positive. The all-right-core sector satisfies strict shifted Newton inequalities in the remaining flag regime; the complementary cases have a $4+2$ minimum cover and are already ultra-log-concave at every positive field. The higher gap follows from a Lorentzian polynomial with generic coordinate-plane extensions. The lower gap has a direct certificate for the 25 independent pairs of right-neighborhood types. This proves eventual strict rank-six ultra-log-concavity under common right-core scaling.

\paragraph{Scope.}
This is an eventual-scaling and conditional-sector theorem. It does not prove ultra-log-concavity at arbitrary finite fields in the new cases, or cover cores with two missing edges in one row or column. \mnote{Those cores are treated by sources~35 and~32, and every core by source~21.} The general rank-six one-shore problem remains open. The result is an ordinary mathematical proof with exact rational certificates, not a formal proof-assistant development or a claim of external priority.

\subsection{The graph and the conclusion}
Let $G$ have shores $A\sqcup L$ and $B\sqcup R$, with
\[
 A=\{A_1,A_2,A_3\},\quad B=\{B_1,B_2,B_3\},\quad \nu(G)=6.
\]
There are no $L$--$R$ edges. After relabeling, the missing core edges form a subset of
\[
 \{A_1B_1,A_2B_2,A_3B_3\}.
\]
All left activities are one, and all right activities are arbitrary positive real numbers. The matching-support polynomial counts each feasible pair of endpoint sets once, irrespective of its number of matchings.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:mh:thm:main}
For every graph and field in this family, sufficiently large common scaling of the three right-core activities makes the full support polynomial strictly $\ULC_6$.

More precisely, suppose each $B_i$ has at least two exterior-left neighbors. Condition on selecting every vertex of $B$, divide out their fixed activity product, and write the result as
\[
 t^3(C_0+C_1t+C_2t^2+C_3t^3).
\]
Then all $C_i$ are positive and
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:targets}
 8C_1^2>15C_0C_2,\qquad 5C_2^2>12C_1C_3.
\end{equation}
If some $B_i$ has only one exterior-left neighbor, the graph already has strict $\ULC_6$ at every positive field.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{Same theorem as source~21 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:21}), restricted to cores obtained from $K_{3,3}$ by deleting a matching (source~21 also needs no restriction on the exterior-left neighbourhoods of the $B_i$): its eventual statement is the case of that core in source~21's Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:ec:cor:scaling}, and its two conditional inequalities ($8C_1^2>15C_0C_2$, $5C_2^2>12C_1C_3$) follow from source~21's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}, whose constants $2n/(n-1)$ and $21/8$ are larger and which needs no hypothesis on the core; both are the case $a=b=3$ of source~08s's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top} and Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}. Source~38's proof is printed as a second route. What it adds: the every-field branch when some $B_i$ has a unique exterior-left neighbour, which is a corollary of Part~I (source~74's theorem for the resulting $4+2$ cover and source~72's strictness), the exact subtraction formula, and a 25-pair certificate.}

Every six-matching avoids core edges, so both exterior graphs have rank three. In particular no $B_i$ has zero exterior-left neighbors. If one has a unique such neighbor $\ell$, replace $B_i$ by $\ell$ in the displayed cover. This produces a minimum cover with four left and two right vertices. The established small-cover theorem \cite{b:m74} gives ULC at every positive field. Its strictness companion \cite{b:m72} applies because the nontrivial graph is connected and contains the nonstar core. We may therefore assume each $B_i$ has at least two exterior-left neighbors.

\subsection{Unit left counts and exact plane defects}
Delete isolated exterior vertices. Let $n,p,q$ be the counts of independent exterior-left subsets of sizes one, two, three in their rank-three transversal matroid on slots $B$.

For an actual hole $A_iB_i$, let $d_i$ count independent exterior-left pairs whose neighborhoods both avoid $B_i$. For an absent hole set $d_i=0$. Put $D=d_1+d_2+d_3$. The following 18 polynomials are nonnegative:
\begin{gather}
 g_1=n-3,\qquad g_2=p-2n+3,\qquad g_3=q-n+2,\notag\\
 g_4=3q-p,\qquad g_5=p-D,\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:constraints}\\
 g_{5+i}=d_i,\qquad g_{8+i}=q-2d_i\qquad(1\le i\le3),\notag\\
 g_{11+i}=p-d_i-2n+4\qquad(1\le i\le3),\notag\\
 g_{15}=2p^2-3nq,\qquad g_{16}=n(n-1)-2p,\notag\\
 g_{17}=2p^2(n-2)-3n(n-1)q,\notag\\
 g_{18}=n(n-1)(n-2)-6q.\notag
\end{gather}
Here and below the formula order fixes the certificate's generator indices.

For $g_2,g_3$, fix one basis: each additional nonloop forms independent pairs with at least two basis elements and belongs to a new basis with two fixed basis elements. Every independent pair extends to a basis, whereas each basis contains three pairs, proving $p\le3q$. The upper bounds on $p$ and $q$ are immediate subset counts.
