An independent pair cannot lie in two different coordinate planes, since their intersection has rank one; hence $D\le p$. Every pair counted by $d_i$ extends with each of the at least two exterior vertices adjacent to $B_i$, giving $2d_i\le q$. For $p-d_i\ge2n-4$, let $n_0,n_1$ be the numbers of exterior-left vertices inside and outside that plane. If $d_i>0$, both $n_0,n_1\ge2$, and every cross pair is independent. Thus
\[
 p-d_i\ge n_0n_1\ge2(n_0+n_1)-4=2n-4.
\]
If $d_i=0$, use $p\ge2n-3$ instead.

The two Mason constraints are consequences of the established \emph{ground-set-normalized} independence inequality \cite[Theorem 4.14]{b:BH}:
\[
 p^2\ge\frac{3n(n-1)}{2(n-2)}q.
\]
The weaker consequence $p^2\ge3nq/2$ is also recorded in \eqref{mrn:e:mh:eq:constraints}. No matching-rank-three normalization of these independence counts is asserted.

\subsubsection{Right profiles}
For the exterior-right graph on the three slots $A$, let $U,A_2,T$ be its support coefficients of orders one, two, three; let $W,B_2$ count independent right singles and pairs; and let $V$ count private-coordinate/right independent pairs. A right vertex of degree one contributes twice its activity to $V$, and one of degree two or three contributes three times. In formulas below abbreviate $A_2$ by $a$ and $B_2$ by $b$.

Let $R_i$ be the right vertices avoiding $A_i$, with total activity $W_i$. Let $u_i,v_i$ be the two-slot support coefficients of their restricted graph on the other two slots.

\begin{lemma}[Exact conditional coefficients]\label{mrn:e:mh:lem:count}
The conditional cubic in Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:mh:thm:main} has
\begin{align}
 C_0&=q+3p+3n+1-D,&
 C_1&=qU+pV+nW-\sum_i d_i u_i,\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:coeff}\\
 C_2&=qa+pb-\sum_i d_i v_i,&
 C_3&=qT.\notag
\end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Partition supports by the number of selected exterior-left vertices. With three, their set is a basis and every selected core-left vertex is used on the right exterior; this contributes $q$ times the exterior-right support polynomial. With one, any remaining $2\times2$ core submatrix has a perfect matching, so the contribution is unchanged from the complete core. With none, the core itself has a perfect matching.

Only two selected exterior-left vertices can lose a support. If their pair can leave two different $B$ slots unused, every core-left vertex can match one of those slots, since each core row misses at most one edge. Otherwise the pair lies in one coordinate plane and leaves only $B_i$. An actual hole there removes the profile
\[
 d_i(1+u_i t+v_i t^2).
\]
At exterior-right degrees zero, one, two, the losses are respectively one forbidden leftover core vertex, one right vertex avoiding it, and an independent right pair avoiding it. The plane classes are disjoint, so these subtractions do not overlap. The complete-core count \cite{b:m43} then gives \eqref{mrn:e:mh:eq:coeff}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Lorentzian flag polynomial and the higher gap}
Start with the rank-three transversal matroid on the right exterior vertices and three private coordinate elements. Give the private elements activity $s$ and the right elements their fixed fields times $t$. Add diffuse generic elements in the whole rank-three flat with total activity $z$, and in each relevant coordinate plane with total activity $f_i$. Write $Z=z+\sum_i f_i$.

The coefficientwise limit of their basis polynomials is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:flag}
 F=F_{\rm base}(s,t,Z)
 -\sum_i\left[
 f_i(s^2+u_i st+v_i t^2)
 +\frac{f_i^2}{2}(2s+W_i t)+\frac{f_i^3}{6}
 \right],
\end{equation}
where
\[
 F_{\rm base}=s^3+Us^2t+ast^2+Tt^3
 +Z(3s^2+Vst+bt^2)+\frac{Z^2}{2}(3s+Wt)+\frac{Z^3}{6}.
\]
The subtractions have a direct basis interpretation. One plane point fails to extend exactly the original independent pairs spanning that plane. Two same-plane points fail to extend exactly the original elements inside it. Three same-plane points are dependent. Generic selections from distinct coordinate planes have the required independent extension profiles; their mixed terms need no correction.

This constructs $F$ as a Lorentzian polynomial in the original nonnegative variables $s,t,z,f_i$, using matroid basis Lorentzianity and its closure properties \cite{b:BH}. For the Hessian calculation, we merely change linear coordinates to $(s,t,Z,f_i)$. Inertia of a quadratic form is preserved by this invertible change; no Lorentzian-cone closure under negative substitutions is being used.

The Hessian of $\partial_tF$ has $t,t$ entry $6T>0$ and cross-column $2(a,b,-v_i)$. On the remaining variables its quadratic form is
\[
 2Us^2+2VsZ+WZ^2-2s\sum_i u_i f_i-\sum_iW_i f_i^2.
\]
Its Schur complement at $6T$ is negative semidefinite. Evaluate the resulting positive-semidefinite inequality at $(s,Z,f_i)=(q,p,d_i)$ to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:PSD}
 C_2^2\ge3qT(C_1-nW)+\frac32T\left(Wp^2-\sum_iW_i d_i^2\right).
\end{equation}

For a right vertex with nonempty neighborhood $S$, its contribution to $\sum_i d_i u_i$ is $|S|\sum_{i\notin S}d_i$ times its activity. There are at most two indices outside $S$, each $d_i\le q/2$. Hence $C_1\ge pV+nW\ge(2p+n)W$. Also
\[
 \sum_iW_i d_i^2\le\frac q2\sum_iW_i d_i\le\frac12Wqp.
\]
Equation~\eqref{mrn:e:mh:eq:PSD} now yields
\begin{align}
 C_2^2-\frac{12}{5}C_1C_3
 &\ge WT\left(\frac32p^2+\frac9{20}pq-\frac{12}{5}nq\right)\notag\\
 &\ge WT\left(-\frac1{10}p^2+\frac9{20}pq\right)
 \ge\frac1{20}WTp^2>0.\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:higher}
\end{align}
The last two steps use $p^2\ge3nq/2$ and $p\le3q$. Genuine exterior rank three ensures $W,T>0$.

\subsection{The lower gap by 25 neighborhood pairs}
For a right vertex with nonempty neighborhood $S\subseteq\{1,2,3\}$, its coefficient in $C_1$ is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:kappa}
 \kappa(S)=q|S|+p\,\epsilon(S)+n-|S|\sum_{i\notin S}d_i,
 \qquad
 \epsilon(S)=
 \begin{cases}2,&|S|=1,\\3,&|S|\ge2.\end{cases}
\end{equation}
The preceding profile argument gives $\kappa(S)\ge2p+n>0$.

For an independent pair of distinct right vertices with neighborhoods $S,T$, let $a(S,T)$ count the two-slot subsets into which they match. Their coefficient in $C_2$ is
\[
 \lambda(S,T)=q\,a(S,T)+p-\sum_{i\notin S\cup T}d_i.
\]
Thus their product coefficient in $8C_1^2-15C_0C_2$ is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:J}
 J(S,T)=16\kappa(S)\kappa(T)-15C_0\lambda(S,T).
\end{equation}
There are 25 unordered independent pairs of nonempty neighborhood types, allowing repeated types. The three excluded pairs are repeated singleton types; their $C_2$ coefficient is zero and their gap coefficient is positive. The square of an individual right activity has gap coefficient $8\kappa(S)^2>0$.

\begin{proposition}[Exact pair certificate]\label{mrn:e:mh:prop:certificate}
For all 25 independent types, the accompanying rational identities have the form
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:mh:eq:cert}
 (n-2)J(S,T)
 =1+\sum_{\alpha}r_\alpha\prod_{j=1}^{18}g_j^{\alpha_j},
 \qquad r_\alpha\ge0,\quad\alpha_j\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}.
\end{equation}
There are 1165 nonzero terms across the complete certificate.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Exact verification]
The supplied standard-library verifier reconstructs $\kappa$, $\lambda$, every target polynomial, and all 25 rational identities. It verifies nonnegative weights and exponents, exact sparse-polynomial equality, complete pair coverage, and the strict remainder one. No floating-point calculation enters this replay. The original linear-programming search served only to discover the certificate.
\end{proof}

All $g_j\ge0$ by Section 2 \mnote{Subsection~\ref{mrn:src:38}.2 here}, and $n-2>0$. Therefore every $J(S,T)>0$. Every quadratic coefficient of the first shifted gap is positive, proving its strict positivity for arbitrary positive real right activities. Together with \eqref{mrn:e:mh:eq:higher}, this proves the conditional assertion of Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:mh:thm:main}.

\subsection{Eventual strictness and reproducibility}
Scale the three right-core fields by a common factor $\lambda$, keeping their positive relative fields fixed. At support orders one, two, three, let the leading coefficients be $a_1,a_2,a_3>0$. They are the coefficients of the induced three-right-vertex support graph, so the weighted smaller-shore theorem gives
\[
 a_1^2\ge3a_2,\qquad a_2^2\ge3a_1a_3.
\]
The first two rank-six gap-leading coefficients are consequently positive:
\[
 5a_1^2-12a_2\ge3a_2>0,\qquad
 8a_2^2-15a_1a_3\ge9a_1a_3>0.
\]
At the third gap the square has degree six in $\lambda$, whereas the adjacent product has degree at most five. The final two leading coefficients are positive multiples of the two expressions in \eqref{mrn:e:mh:eq:targets}. All five full-polynomial gaps are therefore eventually strictly positive. This completes Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:mh:thm:main}, including its small-cover branch.

A field-dependent threshold can be obtained directly. If
\[
 \Delta_k(\lambda)=a_{k,d_k}\lambda^{d_k}
                   +\sum_{j<d_k}a_{k,j}\lambda^j,
 \quad a_{k,d_k}>0,
\]
then every gap is positive for
\[
 \lambda\ge1+\max_{1\le k\le5}
       \frac{\sum_{j<d_k}|a_{k,j}|}{a_{k,d_k}}.
\]
No uniform threshold is claimed.

The package \mnote{shipped as \path{code/38-matching-hole-*.py} and \path{data/38-matching-hole-*.json}} includes the exact 25-case coefficient data and its independent standard-library replay. A separate endpoint enumerator checks 160 graphs, 40 for each number of holes, with arbitrary exterior neighborhoods and positive integer fields. All support identities agree; all 104 cases in the flag regime also pass the stated constraints and strict inequalities. These are supplementary regressions. The real-field and unbounded-population claims follow from the Lorentzian argument and the exact constraint-cone identities.

The next structural case has two missing edges in the same row or column. Then some $2\times2$ core minors vanish, and exterior-left singleton profiles also change. The present proof does not cover those additional degeneracies or settle arbitrary finite-field ULC at rank six. \mnote{The next cases are sources~35 and~32; every core, source~21.}

%% ======================== source 35 ========================
\section{Second route for cores with two complete rows, one weighted shore (source~35)}\label{mrn:src:35}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~35, as delivered.} Consider a genuine rank-six bipartite graph with a displayed $3+3$ cover, two complete core rows, and an arbitrary third core row. Exterior neighborhoods are arbitrary, left activities are one, and right activities are positive. We prove eventual strict rank-six ultra-log-concavity under common right-core scaling. The new cases have an exceptional row of degree one or zero. For degree one, paired left and right Lorentzian flag polynomials control the higher conditional gap, while an exact 25-case rational certificate controls the lower gap. For degree zero, both inequalities have direct analytic proofs. The degree-two and degree-three cases follow from the preceding matching-hole and complete-core theorems.

\paragraph{Scope.}
The new general conclusion is eventual strictness, with a graph- and field-dependent threshold. ULC at rank six for arbitrary finite activities remains open. The transposed column-defect family is a separate question under the fixed unit-left convention. This is an ordinary proof with exact rational checks, not formal kernel verification or an external priority claim.

\subsection{The family and its conditional sector}
Let $G$ have shores $A\sqcup L$ and $B\sqcup R$, where
\[
 A=\{A_1,A_2,A_3\},\quad B=\{B_1,B_2,B_3\},\quad \nu(G)=6.
\]
There are no $L$--$R$ edges. The rows of $A_2,A_3$ are complete to $B$; the neighborhood of $A_1$ inside $B$ is arbitrary. All left activities are one and all right activities are positive real numbers.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:tr:thm:main}
Fix such a graph and its right activities. Multiplying all three right-core activities by a sufficiently large common factor makes the matching-support polynomial strictly $\ULC_6$.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{Same theorem as source~21 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:21}), restricted to cores with two complete rows: its eventual statement is the case of that core in source~21's Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:ec:cor:scaling}, and its two conditional inequalities ($8C_1^2>15C_0C_2$, $5C_2^2>12C_1C_3$) follow from source~21's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}, whose constants $2n/(n-1)$ and $21/8$ are larger and which needs no hypothesis on the core; both are the case $a=b=3$ of source~08s's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top} and Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}. Source~35's proof is printed as a second route. What it adds: the every-field branch when the exterior-left matroid has a coloop (a corollary of Part~I's sources~74 and~72), a direct proof for an exceptional row of degree zero, and a 25-case certificate for degree one.}

Matching supports count each feasible pair of endpoint sets once. Every size-six matching avoids core edges, so both exterior graphs have rank three. Condition on selecting all of $B$ and divide out their fixed activity product. The resulting polynomial is
\[
 t^3(C_0+C_1t+C_2t^2+C_3t^3).
\]
The two nontrivial shifted inequalities are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:target}
 8C_1^2>15C_0C_2,\qquad 5C_2^2>12C_1C_3.
\end{equation}
They supply the last two leading coefficients needed for Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tr:thm:main}.

The cases $\deg_B(A_1)=3,2$ were proved in the complete-core and matching-hole reports \cite{b:m43,b:m38}. We prove the remaining two degrees below. Isolated exterior vertices can be deleted throughout.

\subsubsection{Common notation}
Let $n,p,q$ count independent exterior-left subsets of sizes one, two, three in the rank-three transversal matroid on slots $B$. We use
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:basic}
 n\ge3,\quad p\ge2n-3\ge n,\quad q\ge n-2\ge1,\quad
 p^2\ge\frac32nq.
\end{equation}
The extension bounds follow by fixing a basis. The last inequality is a weaker consequence of the established ground-set-normalized Mason theorem \cite[Theorem 4.14]{b:BH}:
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:mason}
 p^2\ge\frac{3n(n-1)}{2(n-2)}q.
\end{equation}
The normalization here uses $n$, not the matching rank three.

For the exterior-right graph on slots $A$, let $U,a,T$ be its support coefficients of orders one, two, three; let $W,b$ count weighted independent right singles and pairs; and let $V$ count private-coordinate/right independent pairs. A right vertex of degree one contributes twice its activity to $V$, and one of degree two or three contributes three times.

Let $R_0$ consist of right vertices avoiding $A_1$. Write $W_0$ for its total activity and $u,v$ for its two-slot support coefficients. Put $W_1=W-W_0>0$. This positivity follows from exterior-right rank three.

\subsection{The exceptional row of degree one}
Relabel so that $A_1$ is adjacent only to $B_3$ inside the core. Let $e$ count exterior-left vertices adjacent only to $B_3$. Let $\beta$ count exterior-left pairs whose projections to slots $B_1,B_2$ are independent. Define
\[
 d=p-\beta,\qquad \mu=n-e.
\]
Thus $d$ counts independent pairs whose represented span contains the $B_3$ axis. Exterior rank three gives $\mu\ge2$ and $\beta\ge1$.

\begin{lemma}[Exact subtraction]\label{mrn:e:tr:lem:counts}
The conditioned coefficients are
\begin{align}
 C_0&=q+3p+3n+1-d-2e,\notag\\
 C_1&=qU+pV+nW-du-eW_0,\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:coeff}\\
 C_2&=qa+pb-dv,\qquad C_3=qT.\notag
\end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Relative to the complete core, an independent exterior-left pair fails to permit $A_1$ as its leftover core vertex exactly when it cannot match to $B_1,B_2$. This removes $d(1+ut+vt^2)$. A singleton causes an additional loss exactly when it is parallel to the $B_3$ axis. Then the remaining core pair must be $A_2,A_3$, losing two constant supports; with one exterior-right vertex, that vertex must see $A_1$, losing $W_0$. The additional subtraction is $e(2+W_0t)$. These are disjoint left-cardinality cases, so no support is counted twice.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{A left flag and the higher gap}
Add diffuse generic global points and one point on the $B_3$ axis to the left rank-three transversal matroid. Its basis-polynomial limit is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:leftflag}
 \mathcal L(t,z,f)=qt^3+pzt^2+\frac n2z^2t+\frac{z^3}{6}
       +f\left(\beta t^2+\mu zt+\frac{z^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation}
It is Lorentzian by the matroid basis theorem and its closure properties \cite{b:BH}. The Hessian of $\partial_t\mathcal L$ is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 6q&2p&2\beta\\
 2p&n&\mu\\
 2\beta&\mu&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
It has at most one positive eigenvalue, and its determinant is twice
\[
 H=4p\beta\mu-2n\beta^2-3q\mu^2\ge0.
\]
The exact identity
\[
 2\mu\left[\left(p^2-\frac32nq\right)-(d^2-2eq)\right]
   =H+e(2\beta^2+\mu q)
\]
therefore proves
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:leftbound}
 d^2-2eq\le p^2-\frac32nq.
\end{equation}

On the right, use the global/coordinate-plane extension from \cite{b:m38}. In coordinates $(s,t,Z,f)$ it is
\[
 \mathcal R=F_{\rm base}(s,t,Z)
       -f(s^2+ust+vt^2)-\frac{f^2}{2}(2s+W_0t)-\frac{f^3}{6},
\]
where
\[
 F_{\rm base}=s^3+Us^2t+ast^2+Tt^3
 +Z(3s^2+Vst+bt^2)+\frac{Z^2}{2}(3s+Wt)+\frac{Z^3}{6}.
\]
The original nonnegative variables are $(s,t,z,f)$, with $Z=z+f$; the displayed coordinate change is used only for Hessian inertia. The Schur complement of the positive $6T$ entry of $\nabla^2\partial_t\mathcal R$, evaluated at $(s,Z,f)=(q,p,d)$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:rightbound}
 C_2^2\ge3qT(C_1-nW+eW_0)+\frac32T(Wp^2-W_0d^2).
\end{equation}
Since $p^2-\frac32nq\ge0$ and $W_0\le W$, \eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:leftbound} implies
\[
 W_0(d^2-2eq)\le W\left(p^2-\frac32nq\right).
\]
Hence \eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:rightbound} yields
\[
 C_2^2\ge3qTC_1-\frac34nqWT.
\]
Also $U\ge W$, $V-u\ge W$, $d\le p$, and $\mu\ge2$ imply
\[
 C_1\ge(q+p+n-e)W\ge2nW
\]
by \eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:basic}. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:higher}
 C_2^2-\frac{12}{5}C_1C_3
   \ge\frac9{20}nqWT>0.
\end{equation}
This higher-gap proof does not require the following coloop reduction.

\subsubsection{Coloops and unit flag constraints}
If the exterior-left matroid has a coloop $\ell$, deleting it leaves an exterior graph of matching rank two. A two-vertex cover of that graph, together with $\ell$ and all three core-left vertices, is a minimum cover of $G$ meeting the right shore in at most two vertices. The small-cover theorem \cite{b:m74} and strictness classification \cite{b:m72} apply. \mnote{Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main} and Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:main}.} In the present positive-degree case the nontrivial graph is connected and nonstar.

We may therefore assume the exterior-left matroid is coloop-free. Then $n\ge4$ and $2p\le3q$, since every independent pair has at least two basis extensions. Its projected rank-two matroid is also coloop-free: if deleting one projected element left rank one, all remaining original vectors would lie in the preimage plane, making that element a coloop before projection. Thus $\mu\ge3$. Each projected nonloop has at least two independent partners, so double counting gives $\beta\ge\mu$.

The exact first-gap certificate uses the following 19 nonnegative polynomials, in the displayed order:
\begin{gather}
 g_1=n-4,\quad g_2=p-2n+3,\quad g_3=q-n+2,\quad g_4=3q-2p,\notag\\
 g_5=e,\quad g_6=\mu-3,\quad g_7=d,\quad g_8=\beta-\mu,\notag\\
 g_9=d-e\mu,\quad g_{10}=q-e\beta,\quad
 g_{11}=q+e\beta-2d,\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:constraints}\\
 g_{12}=2p^2-3nq,\quad
 g_{13}=n(n-1)-e(e-1)-2p,\notag\\
 g_{14}=\mu(\mu-1)-2\beta,\notag\\
 g_{15}=2p^2(n-2)-3n(n-1)q,\notag\\
 g_{16}=n(n-1)(n-2)-6q,\notag\\
 g_{17}=4p\beta\mu-2n\beta^2-3q\mu^2,\notag\\
 g_{18}=2p^2-3nq-2d^2+4eq,\notag\\
 g_{19}=\mu(\mu-1)(\mu-2)+6e\beta-6q.\notag
\end{gather}
The basic count bounds and Mason constraints have already been justified. Every axis/nonaxis pair is independent and counted by $d$, proving $d\ge e\mu$. Bases containing an axis element are exactly the $e\beta$ choices of that element and a projected independent pair. This proves $g_{10}\ge0$ and the bound $g_{19}\ge0$ on the remaining nonaxis triples.

Every $d$-pair has at least two extensions. A basis containing an axis element has exactly two $d$-pairs. A basis containing none has at most one: the spans of two pairs in a basis intersect in their common coordinate line. Thus
\[
 2d\le q+e\beta.
\]
The pair upper bound excludes the $\binom e2$ dependent axis pairs, and $g_{14}$ is the projected pair upper bound. Equations \eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:leftflag}--\eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:leftbound} give $g_{17},g_{18}\ge0$. All certificate hypotheses are therefore established.

\subsubsection{The first gap}
For a nonempty right neighborhood $S\subseteq\{1,2,3\}$, put
\[
 \epsilon(S)=
 \begin{cases}2,&|S|=1,\\3,&|S|\ge2.\end{cases}
\]
Its coefficient in $C_1$ is
\[
 \kappa(S)=q|S|+p\epsilon(S)+n
      -\mathbf1_{\{1\notin S\}}(d|S|+e)>0.
\]
For an independent pair of distinct right vertices of types $S,T$, its coefficient in $C_2$ is
\[
 \lambda(S,T)=q\,a(S,T)+p
              -\mathbf1_{\{1\notin S\cup T\}}d,
\]
where $a(S,T)$ counts its two-slot endpoint supports. Therefore the product coefficient in the first shifted gap is
\[
 J(S,T)=16\kappa(S)\kappa(T)-15C_0\lambda(S,T).
\]
For all 25 unordered independent neighborhood pairs, including repeated types, the supplied exact data give
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:certificate}
 (n-2)J(S,T)
   =1+\sum_\alpha r_\alpha\prod_{j=1}^{19}g_j^{\alpha_j},
 \qquad r_\alpha\ge0,\quad\alpha_j\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}.
\end{equation}
There are 1112 nonzero rational terms. A separately written standard-library verifier reconstructs every generator, pair profile, target polynomial and rational identity, and checks complete coverage and the strict remainder one. The numerical search that found the identities is not used by this replay.

Since all $g_j\ge0$ and $n\ge4$, every $J(S,T)>0$. Dependent repeated singleton types have zero $C_2$ coefficient, as do individual activity squares; their gap coefficients are positive. This proves the first inequality in \eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:target} for degree one, and \eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:higher} proves the second.

\subsection{The exceptional row of degree zero}
Now $A_1$ has no core neighbors. The direct conditioned count is
\begin{align}
 C_0&=q+2p+n,&
 C_1&=qU+p(V-u)+nW_1,\label{mrn:e:tr:eq:zero}\\
 C_2&=qa+p(b-v),&
 C_3&=qT.\notag
\end{align}
An exterior-left triple contributes $q$ times the full right support polynomial. A pair leaves one core-left vertex for $B$, which must be $A_2$ or $A_3$, giving the plane-point profile $2+(V-u)t+(b-v)t^2$. A singleton leaves both $A_2,A_3$ for $B$, giving $1+W_1t$. No support with no exterior-left vertex can match all three $B$ vertices, because the core has rank two.

The right plane Hessian evaluated at $(s,Z,f)=(q,p,p)$ gives
\[
 C_2^2\ge3qT(C_1-nW_1)+\frac32p^2W_1T.
\]
Using \eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:basic} yields
\[
 C_2^2-\frac{12}{5}C_1C_3
 \ge\frac35qTC_1-\frac34nqW_1T.
\]
Since $U\ge W_1$, $V-u\ge2W_1$, and $p\ge n$,
\[
 C_1\ge(q+2p+n)W_1\ge3nW_1.
\]
The higher gap is therefore at least $\frac{21}{20}nqW_1T>0$.

\subsubsection{A direct first-gap proof}
For a right neighborhood $S$ of size $k$, its $C_1$ coefficient is
\[
 \kappa(S)=
 \begin{cases}
 qk+p,&1\notin S,\\
 qk+p\epsilon(S)+n,&1\in S.
 \end{cases}
\]
For an independent pair $S,T$, its $C_2$ coefficient is $q$ if both avoid 1, and $q\,a(S,T)+p$ otherwise.

If both avoid 1, then $\kappa(S),\kappa(T)\ge q+p$. Their gap coefficient is at least
\[
 16(q+p)^2-15q(q+2p+n)
 =q^2+2pq+16p^2-15nq
 \ge q^2+2pq+6p^2>0.
\]
Otherwise take $1\in S$. It suffices to show
$\kappa(S)\kappa(T)\ge C_0\lambda(S,T)$.
\begin{enumerate}
 \item If $S=\{1\}$, then $\kappa(S)=C_0$. If $T$ avoids 1, $a(S,T)=|T|$ and $\kappa(T)=\lambda(S,T)$. If $T$ contains 1, $a(S,T)=|T|-1$ and $\kappa(T)\ge\lambda(S,T)$.
 \item If $|S|\ge2$ and $T$ avoids 1, use $\kappa(S)\ge2q+3p+n$. For $|T|=1$ and $|T|=2$, respectively, the required comparisons follow from
 \begin{align*}
 (2q+3p+n)(q+p)-C_0(2q+p)&=p^2-nq\ge0,\\
 (2q+3p+n)(2q+p)-C_0(3q+p)&=q^2+pq+p^2-nq\ge0.
 \end{align*}
 \item If both neighborhoods contain 1 and have size at least two, use
 \[
 (2q+3p+n)^2-C_0(3q+p)
   =q^2+5pq+7p^2+nq+5np+n^2>0.
 \]
\end{enumerate}
These cases are exhaustive. Dependent pairs and individual squares again have zero $C_2$ contribution and positive gap coefficients. This proves the first inequality in \eqref{mrn:e:tr:eq:target}. The degree-zero proof applies directly even when the graph is disconnected.

\subsection{Eventual strictness and verification}
Outside the already settled small-cover branch, the conditional inequalities just proved for degrees zero and one give the last two positive leading Newton gaps under common right-core scaling. At support orders one, two, three, let $a_1,a_2,a_3>0$ denote the leading coefficients in the scaling variable $\lambda$. They form the support sequence of the induced three-right-vertex graph, so the weighted smaller-shore theorem gives
\[
 a_1^2\ge3a_2,\qquad a_2^2\ge3a_1a_3.
\]
The first two full rank-six gaps consequently have positive leading coefficients
\[
 5a_1^2-12a_2\ge3a_2>0,\qquad
 8a_2^2-15a_1a_3\ge9a_1a_3>0.
\]
At the third gap the square has scaling degree six and the adjacent product has degree at most five. Thus every full gap is eventually positive. Together with the inherited degrees two and three, this proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tr:thm:main}.

For fixed graph and fields, write each gap as
$\Delta_k(\lambda)=a_{k,d_k}\lambda^{d_k}+\sum_{j<d_k}a_{k,j}\lambda^j$,
with $a_{k,d_k}>0$. A computable sufficient threshold is
\[
 \lambda\ge1+\max_{1\le k\le5}
    \frac{\sum_{j<d_k}|a_{k,j}|}{a_{k,d_k}}.
\]
No uniform field-independent threshold is asserted.

The package \mnote{shipped as \path{code/35-two-rows-*.py} and \path{data/35-two-rows-*.json}} includes the 25 exact degree-one coefficient certificates and their standard-library replay, together with 100 direct conditional-support checks for each new row degree. The left flag identities and the grouped degree-zero algebra also have exact polynomial checks. These verify the formulas; the real-field and unbounded-population conclusions follow from the proof.

The next case is a column defect, which is not obtained by transposing this theorem while keeping the same shore unit-weighted. General cores and arbitrary finite-activity rank-six ULC remain open. \mnote{The column defect is source~32; general cores are covered eventually by source~21. The finite-activity status is in Part~III's introduction.}

%% ======================== source 32 ========================
\section{Second route for cores with two complete columns, one weighted shore (source~32)}\label{mrn:src:32}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~32, as delivered.} We prove eventual strict rank-six ultra-log-concavity for bipartite graphs with a displayed $3+3$ minimum cover, two complete core columns, and an arbitrary third core column. Exterior neighborhoods are arbitrary; left activities are one and right activities are positive. This extends the preceding two-complete-row theorem under the same shore convention. The degree-one column uses coupled plane and axis extensions of rank-three matroids, followed by an exact 25-case rational certificate. The zero-column case reduces algebraically to the earlier complete-core inequalities.
\mnote{``The preceding two-complete-row theorem'' is source~35 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:35}); source~32's bibliography does not list it.}

\paragraph{Scope.}
The scaling threshold depends on the graph and its activities. General rank-six one-shore ULC for arbitrary finite activities remains open. The argument is an ordinary mathematical proof with exact algebraic checks, not formal kernel verification or an external priority claim.

\subsection{The family and exact support counts}
Let the shores be $A\sqcup L$ and $B\sqcup R$, where
\[
 A=\{A_1,A_2,A_3\},\qquad B=\{B_1,B_2,B_3\},\qquad \nu(G)=6.
\]
There are no $L$--$R$ edges. The columns $B_2,B_3$ are complete to $A$, while $B_1$ has arbitrary core degree. All left activities are one, and all right activities are positive real numbers.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:tc:thm:main}
For every fixed graph and set of activities as above, sufficiently large common scaling of the three right-core activities makes the matching-support polynomial strictly $\ULC_6$.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{Same theorem as source~21 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:21}), restricted to cores with two complete columns: its eventual statement is the case of that core in source~21's Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:ec:cor:scaling}, and its two conditional inequalities ($8C_1^2>15C_0C_2$, $5C_2^2>12C_1C_3$) follow from source~21's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}, whose constants $2n/(n-1)$ and $21/8$ are larger and which needs no hypothesis on the core; both are the case $a=b=3$ of source~08s's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top} and Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}. Source~32's proof is printed as a second route. What it adds: the coloop branch at every field (a corollary of sources~74 and~72), the reduction of a zero column to source~43's inequalities, and a 25-case certificate.}

Matching supports count each feasible pair of endpoint sets once. Every size-six matching avoids core edges, so both exterior graphs have rank three. The core-column degrees three and two are the earlier complete-core and matching-hole cases \cite{b:m43,b:m38}. We prove degrees one and zero. This is not obtained by transposing the row theorem, because the unit-weighted shore is fixed.

Delete isolated exterior vertices. Let $n,p,q$ be the numbers of independent exterior-left subsets of sizes one, two and three. Let
\[
 E=\{\ell\in L:\ell\not\sim B_1\},\qquad
 e=|E|,\quad d=I_2(E),\quad \mu=n-e,\quad h=p-d.
\]
In a generic representation the $E$ vectors lie in the $B_2,B_3$ coordinate plane. We use the standard bounds
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:basic}
 n\ge3,\quad p\ge2n-3\ge n,\quad q\ge n-2\ge1,\quad
 p^2\ge\frac{3n(n-1)}{2(n-2)}q\ge\frac32nq.
\end{equation}
The last is the ground-set-normalized Mason inequality \cite[Theorem 4.14]{b:BH}; its normalization uses the number $n$ of elements, not matching rank three.

For the exterior-right graph on slots $A$, write $U,a,T$ for its support coefficients of orders one, two and three, and $W,b$ for its weighted independent-set counts of orders one and two. Let $V$ count private-coordinate/right independent pairs: a singleton-neighborhood right vertex contributes twice its activity, and a vertex of degree two or three contributes three times. Let $\omega$ be the total activity of right vertices whose neighborhood is exactly $\{A_3\}$. Let $u,v$ be the support coefficients after projecting right neighborhoods to $\{A_1,A_2\}$.

\subsubsection{The column of degree one}
Relabel so that $B_1$ sees only $A_3$. Condition on selecting all three $B$ vertices and divide out their fixed activity product. The resulting polynomial is
\[
 t^3(C_0+C_1t+C_2t^2+C_3t^3),
\]
where
\begin{align}
 C_0&=q+3p+3n+1-2d-e, \label{mrn:e:tc:eq:counts}\\
 C_1&=qU+pV+nW-d(V-u)-e\omega,\notag\\
 C_2&=qa+pb-d(b-v),\qquad C_3=qT.\notag
\end{align}
An independent $L$ pair in the coordinate plane forces $B_1$ to use $A_3$. Its allowed extension profile is $1+ut+vt^2$, instead of $3+Vt+bt^2$. An $L$ singleton in that plane loses the core pair $\{A_1,A_2\}$ and the right-axis profile $\omega t$. All other profiles are unchanged from the complete core. The empty-$L$ support exists because this core has a perfect matching. This disjoint support partition proves \eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:counts} without matching-allocation multiplicities.

The required shifted inequalities are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:targets}
 8C_1^2>15C_0C_2,\qquad C_2^2>\frac{12}{5}C_1C_3.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Plane and axis extensions}
Adjoin diffuse generic full-space points and diffuse generic points in the $B_2,B_3$ plane to the represented left matroid. Its degree-three basis-polynomial limit is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:left}
 \mathcal L=q t^3+pzt^2+\frac n2z^2t+\frac{z^3}{6}
 +f\left(ht^2+nzt+\frac{z^2}{2}\right)
 +\frac{f^2}{2}(\mu t+z).
\end{equation}
It is Lorentzian by the matroid basis theorem and its nonnegative-specialization and limit closures \cite{b:BH}. The Hessian of $\partial_t\mathcal L$ is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}6q&2p&2h\\2p&n&n\\2h&n&\mu\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its principal $(t,f)$ block has at most one positive eigenvalue, giving
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:hbound}
 h^2\ge\frac32q\mu.
\end{equation}
For the other bound put $Z=z+f$. This is only a congruence for Hessian inertia, not a claim of Lorentzian closure under negative substitutions. The Hessian becomes
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}6q&2p&-2d\\2p&n&0\\-2d&0&-e\end{pmatrix}.
\]
When $e>0$, eliminating the negative entry $-e$ leaves a two-by-two block of nonpositive determinant. When $e=0$, also $d=0$. Thus in all cases
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:dbound}
 nd^2\le e\left(p^2-\frac32nq\right).
\end{equation}
Since $e\le n$, \eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:basic}--\eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:dbound} imply
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:mixture}
 p^2-d^2\ge\frac32nq,\qquad h^2+2eq\ge\frac32nq.
\end{equation}

On the right, adjoin diffuse generic full-space points and one point on the $A_3$ axis. The Lorentzian cubic is
\[
 \mathcal F=\mathcal F_{\rm base}
 +f\left[s^2+ust+vt^2+z\bigl(2s+(W-\omega)t\bigr)+\frac{z^2}{2}\right],
\]
where
\[
 \mathcal F_{\rm base}=s^3+Us^2t+ast^2+Tt^3
 +z(3s^2+Vst+bt^2)+\frac{z^2}{2}(3s+Wt)+\frac{z^3}{6}.
\]
The Hessian of its $t$ derivative, in coordinates $(t,s,z,f)$, is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 6T&2a&2b&2v\\
 2a&2U&V&u\\
 2b&V&W&W-\omega\\
 2v&u&W-\omega&0
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Eliminate its positive $6T$ entry. Evaluating the remaining negative-semidefinite Schur form at $(s,z,f)=(q,h,d)$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:right}
 C_2^2\ge3qT(C_1-nW+e\omega)
 +\frac32T\left[W(p^2-d^2)-2\omega dh\right].
\end{equation}
Since $0\le\omega\le W$, the left side of
\[
 W(p^2-d^2)+2\omega(eq-dh)\ge\frac32nqW
\]
is the mixture of the two expressions in \eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:mixture}, with coefficients $W-\omega$ and $\omega$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:coupled}
 C_2^2\ge3qTC_1-\frac34nqWT.
\end{equation}

Every right activity has $C_1$ coefficient at least $2n$. The axis type has coefficient $q+2h+\mu$. Here $\mu\ge1$ and $h\ge n-1$: if $e=0$, use $p\ge2n-3$; otherwise all $e\mu$ cross-plane pairs are independent, and $e\mu\ge e+\mu-1=n-1$. Thus $q+2h+\mu\ge2n$. Every nonaxis type has coefficient at least $q+2p+n-d=q+p+h+n\ge2n$. It follows that $C_1\ge2nW$, and \eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:coupled} gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:higher}
 C_2^2-\frac{12}{5}C_1C_3\ge\frac9{20}nqWT>0.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The lower conditional gap}
\subsubsection{Coloops and valid count constraints}
If the exterior-left matroid has a coloop $\ell$, deleting it leaves matching rank two. A two-vertex cover of that deletion, together with $\ell$ and all three $A$ vertices, gives a minimum cover of size six meeting the right shore in at most two vertices. The earlier small-cover theorem and equality classification \cite{b:m74,b:m72} \mnote{Theorems~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main} and~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:main}} give strict $\ULC_6$ at every positive field: this degree-one core makes the nontrivial graph connected and nonstar.

It remains to treat the coloop-free case. Then $n\ge4$, $2p\le3q$, and $\mu\ge2$. The last holds because deleting a sole vector outside a plane would lower rank. The first-gap certificate uses the following twenty nonnegative polynomials:
\begin{align*}
g_1&=n-4,&g_2&=p-2n+3,\\
g_3&=q-n+2,&g_4&=3q-2p,\\
g_5&=e,&g_6&=\mu-2,\\
g_7&=d,&g_8&=q-2d,\\
g_9&=e(e-1)-2d,&g_{10}&=h-e\mu,\\
g_{11}&=e\mu+\tfrac12\mu(\mu-1)-h,&g_{12}&=h-n+1,\\
g_{13}&=q-\mu d,&g_{14}&=2p^2-3nq,\\
g_{15}&=n(n-1)-2p,&g_{16}&=2p^2(n-2)-3n(n-1)q,\\
g_{17}&=n(n-1)(n-2)-6q,&g_{18}&=2ep^2-3enq-2nd^2,\\
g_{19}&=2h^2-3q\mu.&&
\end{align*}
The remaining polynomial is
\[
 g_{20}=6\mu d+3e\mu(\mu-1)+\mu(\mu-1)(\mu-2)-6q.
\]
Besides the usual extension and subset upper bounds, Mason and the two flag inequalities, the necessary incidence arguments are elementary. The $h$ pairs consist of all $e\mu$ cross-plane pairs and independent pairs of outside elements, proving $g_{10},g_{11}\ge0$. Every independent $E$ pair extends with each outside element, giving exactly $\mu d$ bases of this type, hence $g_{13},g_8\ge0$. The other possible bases have one $E$ element and two outside elements, or three outside elements, proving $g_{20}\ge0$. The bound $g_{12}\ge0$ was proved above. In a coloop-free rank-three matroid each independent pair has at least two basis extensions; counting pair--basis incidences proves $g_4\ge0$.

\subsubsection{Twenty-five exact coefficient identities}
For a nonempty right neighborhood $S\subseteq\{1,2,3\}$, put $\epsilon(S)=2$ for a singleton and $3$ otherwise. Its $C_1$ coefficient is
\[
 \kappa(S)=q|S|+p\epsilon(S)+n
 -d\bigl(\epsilon(S)-|S\cap\{1,2\}|\bigr)
 -e\,\mathbf1_{\{S=\{3\}\}}.
\]
For an independent pair $S,T$, let $a(S,T)$ count its feasible two-slot supports, and let $\rho(S,T)$ be one when the two projected neighborhoods on $\{1,2\}$ are independent, zero otherwise. Its $C_2$ coefficient is
\[
 \lambda(S,T)=qa(S,T)+p-d(1-\rho(S,T)).
\]
Thus the product coefficient in the first gap is
\[
 J(S,T)=16\kappa(S)\kappa(T)-15C_0\lambda(S,T).
\]
For all 25 independent unordered neighborhood types, the supplied rational data give the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:certificate}
 (n-2)J(S,T)=1+\sum_\alpha r_\alpha
                \prod_{j=1}^{20}g_j^{\alpha_j},
 \qquad r_\alpha\ge0,\quad\alpha_j\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}.
\end{equation}
There are 1134 nonzero terms. A separate standard-library verifier reconstructs every generator, neighborhood profile, target and rational identity, and checks complete coverage and strict remainder one. No numerical optimizer is used in this replay.

All $g_j$ are nonnegative and $n\ge4$, so every $J(S,T)>0$. Dependent repeated-singleton types and individual activity squares have no $C_2$ contribution and a positive $C_1$-square contribution. This proves the first inequality in \eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:targets}, while \eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:higher} proves the second.

\subsection{The zero-column case}
If $B_1$ has no core neighbors, direct support counting gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:tc:eq:zero}
 (C_0,C_1,C_2,C_3)
 =(q+3h+3\mu,\ qU+hV+\mu W,\ qa+hb,\ qT).
\end{equation}
There is no empty-$L$ support. A singleton in $E$ contributes nothing; an outside singleton has profile $3+Wt$. A pair in $E$ contributes nothing; every other independent pair has profile $3+Vt+bt^2$. The triple profile is unchanged.

Here $\mu,q\ge1$ and $h\ge\mu$: for $e=0$ use $p\ge n$, and otherwise use $h\ge e\mu$. Equation \eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:hbound} gives $h^2\ge3q\mu/2$.

The earlier complete-core analytic inequalities \cite{b:m43} apply algebraically to the triple $(n,p,q)=(\mu,h,q)$. For precision, that first-gap proof expands six quadratic coefficients in the activity sums of right neighborhoods of sizes one, two and three; their positivity requires only positive integers $\mu,h,q$ and $h^2\ge\mu q$. Its higher-gap proof additionally uses $h\ge\mu$ and gives a positive lower bound
\[
 \frac3{10}qWT(2q+4h-3\mu)>0.
\]
The complete-core comparison has the larger constant $q+3h+3\mu+1$. Reducing that constant by one strictly increases the first gap and does not change the second. Both inequalities therefore hold for \eqref{mrn:e:tc:eq:zero}. This direct argument allows disconnected graphs and does not require a coloop reduction.

\subsection{Eventual scaling and verification}
Outside the already settled small-cover branch, the two conditional inequalities give positive leading terms in the last two full rank-six Newton gaps when all $B$ activities are multiplied by $\lambda$. At support orders one through three, let $a_1,a_2,a_3>0$ be the leading coefficients in $\lambda$. The induced three-column graph gives
\[
 a_1^2\ge3a_2,\qquad a_2^2\ge3a_1a_3.
\]
The first two full gaps consequently have positive leading coefficients
\[
 5a_1^2-12a_2\ge3a_2>0,\qquad
 8a_2^2-15a_1a_3\ge9a_1a_3>0.
\]
At the third gap, the square has degree six in $\lambda$ and the adjacent product has degree at most five. Every gap is therefore eventually positive. The inherited core-column degrees two and three complete Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tc:thm:main}.

For fixed graph and fields, write each gap as
$\Delta_k(\lambda)=a_{k,d_k}\lambda^{d_k}+\sum_{j<d_k}a_{k,j}\lambda^j$
with $a_{k,d_k}>0$. A computable sufficient bound is
\[
 \lambda\ge1+\max_{1\le k\le5}
 \frac{\sum_{j<d_k}|a_{k,j}|}{a_{k,d_k}}.
\]
No uniform threshold is claimed.

The package \mnote{shipped as \path{code/32-two-columns-*.py} and \path{data/32-two-columns-*.json}} contains all 25 rational identities, their exact standard-library replay, and 100 direct support enumerations for each new column degree. The degree-one checker also verifies all twenty constraints on 66 coloop-free samples. Exact flag-algebra checks accompany them. The finite graph tests are regressions; unbounded population and positive-real-field conclusions follow from the mathematical proof and exact coefficient identities.

The next frontier consists of more general core shapes. This theorem and the corresponding row theorem together handle every core with at most two missing edges, but do not settle arbitrary finite-activity rank-six one-shore ULC. \mnote{The row theorem is source~35; every core is covered by source~21.}

%% ======================== source 29 ========================
\section{Second route for cores with at least six edges, one weighted shore (source~29)}\label{mrn:src:29}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~29, as delivered.} Consider a genuine rank-six bipartite graph with a displayed $3+3$ minimum cover. If its $3\times3$ core has at least six edges, then, with unit left activities and arbitrary positive right activities, sufficiently large common scaling of the three right-core activities makes every actual-rank Newton inequality strict. The three remaining six-edge core types are handled by nested, transverse and two-plane matroid extensions. Their higher conditional gaps follow from a common Schur-complement criterion. Their lower gaps have 75 exact rational coefficient identities, with 5617 positive terms, checked by two independent reconstructions. An explicit orbit classification completes the stated core threshold.

\paragraph{Scope.}
The threshold depends on the graph and its fixed activities. General rank-six one-shore ULC for arbitrary finite activities remains open. This is an ordinary mathematical proof with exact algebraic verification, not formal kernel verification or an external priority claim.

\subsection{The theorem and the finite frontier}
Let the shores of $G$ be $A\sqcup L$ and $B\sqcup R$, with
\[
 A=\{A_1,A_2,A_3\},\qquad B=\{B_1,B_2,B_3\},\qquad \nu(G)=6,
\]
and no $L$--$R$ edges. The core is the graph between $A$ and $B$. All left activities are one and all right activities are positive. A matching support is a feasible pair of endpoint sets, counted once regardless of the number of perfect matchings.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:se:thm:main}
If the core has at least six edges, then for every fixed graph and set of right activities, sufficiently large common scaling of all three $B$ activities makes the matching-support polynomial strictly $\ULC_6$.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{Same theorem as source~21 (Section~\ref{mrn:src:21}), restricted to cores with at least six edges: its eventual statement is the case of that core in source~21's Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:ec:cor:scaling}, and its two conditional inequalities ($8C_1^2>15C_0C_2$, $5C_2^2>12C_1C_3$) follow from source~21's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:ec:thm:main}, whose constants $2n/(n-1)$ and $21/8$ are larger and which needs no hypothesis on the core; both are the case $a=b=3$ of source~08s's Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:tg:thm:top} and Corollary~\ref{mrn:e:tg:cor:cubic}. Source~29's proof is printed as a second route. What it adds: the exhaustive orbit census of cores with six edges, the coloop branch at every field (a corollary of sources~74 and~72), and 75 exact identities for the three remaining six-edge cores.}

Every size-six matching avoids core edges, so both exterior graphs have rank three. Let $n,p,q$ be the exterior-left independent-set counts of orders one, two and three, after removing isolated vertices. Condition on selecting all $B$ vertices and divide out their fixed activity product. Write the resulting sector as
\[
 t^3(C_0+C_1t+C_2t^2+C_3t^3).
\]
The two conditional inequalities needed for the last leading gaps are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:targets}
 8C_1^2>15C_0C_2,\qquad
 C_2^2>\frac{12}{5}C_1C_3.
\end{equation}

We use the established count bounds
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:counts}
 n\ge3,\quad p\ge2n-3\ge n,\quad q\ge n-2\ge1,\qquad
 p^2\ge\frac{3n(n-1)}{2(n-2)}q\ge\frac32nq.
\end{equation}
The last is the ground-set-normalized Mason inequality \cite[Theorem 4.14]{b:BH}. Its normalization is the number $n$ of elements, not matching rank three.

\subsubsection{The coloop branch}
If the exterior-left matroid has a coloop $\ell$, deleting it leaves matching rank two. A two-vertex cover of that deletion, together with $\ell$ and all three $A$ vertices, is a minimum cover of size six meeting the right shore in at most two vertices. The earlier small-cover theorem applies at every positive field \cite{b:m74}. \mnote{Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main}; the equality classification below is Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:main}.} A six-edge core on six vertices contains a cycle, so the full graph is not a union of stars. The established equality classification therefore makes every gap strict \cite{b:m72}, even if the graph is disconnected. We may assume below that the exterior-left matroid is coloop-free.

\subsubsection{Exhaustive core classification}
With at least seven core edges, the missing graph has at most two edges. Zero or one missing edge is the complete-core or one-hole family. Two missing edges are disjoint, incident on one left vertex, or incident on one right vertex. The matching-hole, two-complete-row and two-complete-column theorems cover these possibilities \cite{b:m43,b:m38,b:m35,b:m32}.

For exactly six core edges, the missing graph has three edges. If its maximum degree is three, it is a row star or column star. If its maximum degree is at most one, it is a matching. Otherwise a degree-two vertex exists: the third edge either extends its two-edge star to a three-edge path or is disjoint from it. The disjoint case has two orientations, according to the shore of the degree-two vertex. Thus there are exactly six orbits under independent row and column permutations:
\[
 \text{matching,\ row star,\ column star,\ path,\ star--edge,\ transposed star--edge}.
\]
The first three complements are already settled. Convenient core row-neighborhood representatives for the remaining three are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:cores}
 (123,12,1),\qquad (123,23,1),\qquad (13,12,12).
\end{equation}
Shore transposition is not used as an equivalence of weighted theorems.

\subsection{A common higher-gap criterion}
On the exterior right, let $U,a,T$ be support coefficients of orders one, two and three, and let $W,b$ be weighted independent-set counts of orders one and two. Let $V$ count private-coordinate/right independent pairs: a right vertex of degree one contributes twice its activity, and one of degree two or three contributes three times.

For nonempty $S\subseteq\{1,2,3\}$, let $V_S$ count a generic $S$ vector, one right vertex and a private coordinate forming a basis. A right neighborhood $D$ contributes the number of feasible two-slot supports of $(S,D)$. Let $B_S$ count right pairs which, with a generic $S$ vector, form a basis. Write $W_S$ for the total activity of right vertices meeting $S$. Thus $V_{123}=V$ and $B_{123}=b$.

Adjoining generic vectors in fixed subspaces to a represented matroid preserves representability. Give a family of $N$ generic vectors activity $z/N$ and let $N\to\infty$. The resulting basis-polynomial limits are Lorentzian by the matroid basis theorem and closure under nonnegative specialization and limits \cite{b:BH}. This supplies all flag polynomials used below. Invertible signed coordinate changes are used only for Hessian inertia.

Each right flag gives, after eliminating the positive $6T$ entry of the $t$-derivative Hessian, an inequality of the form
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:schur}
 C_2^2\ge3qT\left(C_1-\sum_D w_D\beta_D\right)
             +\frac32T\sum_Dw_D Q_D.
\end{equation}
Here $w_D$ is an individual right activity, $\beta_D$ is its contribution from singleton exterior-left supports, and $Q_D$ is the evaluated pair quadratic. If
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:criterion}
 Q_D\ge\frac32q\beta_D,\qquad
 \kappa(D)\ge2\beta_D,
\end{equation}
where $\kappa(D)$ is its $C_1$ coefficient, then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:margin}
 C_2^2-\frac{12}{5}C_1C_3
 \ge\frac9{20}qT\sum_Dw_D\beta_D>0.
\end{equation}
In every case below $\beta_D>0$ for nonempty $D$. The last strict inequality uses the genuine rank-six hypothesis.

\subsection{The triangular core}
Take core rows $(123,12,1)$. Let $e$ count axis-$1$ exterior-left vectors, $f$ count vectors in coordinate plane $12$, $d$ count independent pairs whose span contains axis $1$, and $g$ count independent pairs in plane $12$. Put
\[
 u=p-d,\qquad v=d-g,\qquad \mu=n-e,\qquad m=n-f.
\]
Then $e\le f$, $g\le d$, and $m\ge1$. The exact support count is
\begin{align}
 C_0&=q+3p-d-g+3n-f-e+1,\label{mrn:e:se:eq:tri}\\
 C_1&=qU+uV+vV_{12}+gV_1
       +(n-f)W+(f-e)W_{23}+eW_3,\notag\\
 C_2&=qa+ub+vB_{12}+gB_1,\qquad C_3=qT.\notag
\end{align}
For a left pair, core row $1$ always extends it; row $2$ fails precisely for a plane-$12$ pair, and row $3$ fails precisely for an axis-spanning pair. The resulting line profiles have counts $u,v,g$. For a left singleton, the normal supports of its allowed right extension are $123,23,3$, with counts $n-f,f-e,e$. This is a disjoint endpoint-support partition.

The left global/plane/axis flag is
\begin{align*}
 \mathcal L={}&qt^3+pzt^2+\frac n2z^2t+\frac{z^3}{6}\\
 &+y\left((p-g)t^2+nzt+\frac{z^2}{2}\right)
    +\frac{y^2}{2}((n-f)t+z)\\
 &+x\left(ut^2+\mu zt+\frac{z^2}{2}\right)
    +xy(mt+z).
\end{align*}
The global/plane Hessian gives $p^2-g^2\ge3nq/2$, as in \cite{b:m32}. The global/axis Hessian gives
\[
 H=4pu\mu-2nu^2-3q\mu^2\ge0.
\]
The identity
\[
 2\mu\left(p^2-d^2-\frac32q\mu\right)=H+2eu^2
\]
therefore yields $p^2-d^2\ge3q\mu/2$. Finally the principal Hessian on $(t,y,x)$ is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}6q&2(p-g)&2u\\2(p-g)&m&m\\2u&m&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Its determinant is $2m[4(p-g)u-2u^2-3qm]\ge0$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:tri-bounds}
 p^2-g^2\ge\frac32qn,\quad
 p^2-d^2\ge\frac32q\mu,\quad
 u(u+2v)\ge\frac32qm.
\end{equation}

The dual right extension uses generic global points, plane-$12$ points and an axis-$1$ point. Its pair block in the $t$-derivative Hessian is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}W&W&W_{23}\\W&W_3&W_3\\W_{23}&W_3&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Evaluate at pair variables $(u,v,g)$, with the private-coordinate variable equal to $q$. For a right neighborhood $D$,
\[
 \beta_D=m+(f-e)\ind_{D\cap23\ne\varnothing}+e\ind_{3\in D}.
\]
The values of $Q_D$, according as $D$ meets slot $3$, meets $2$ but not $3$, or equals $\{1\}$, are
\[
 p^2-g^2,\qquad p^2-d^2,\qquad u(u+2v).
\]
Thus \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:tri-bounds} proves the first half of \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:criterion}.

By neighborhood monotonicity, the second half reduces to the singleton coefficients
\[
 q+2p-d-g+m,\qquad q+2p-d+\mu,\qquad q+2p-g+n.
\]
The last two use $p\ge n$. For the first, $p-g\ge n-1$: if $f=0$ use $p\ge2n-3$; otherwise the $f(n-f)$ cross-plane pairs suffice. Also $u\ge1$. This proves \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:criterion}, and hence the strict higher gap.

\subsection{A star plus a disjoint missing edge}
Take core rows $(123,23,1)$. Let $e$ count axis-$1$ vectors, $f$ count plane-$23$ vectors, $d$ count axis-spanning pairs and $g$ count independent plane-$23$ pairs. Here the axis and plane are transverse:
\[
 e+f\le n,\qquad d+g\le p,\qquad \mu=n-e,\quad u=p-d.
\]
The exact cubic is
\begin{align}
 C_0&=q+3p-d-g+3n-2e+1,\label{mrn:e:se:eq:star}\\
 C_1&=qU+(p-d-g)V+dV_{12}+gV_{13}
                +(n-e)W+eW_3,\notag\\
 C_2&=qa+(p-d-g)b+dB_{12}+gB_{13},\qquad C_3=qT.\notag
\end{align}
The two pair defects are disjoint because a plane-$23$ pair cannot span axis $1$. A singleton loses core-pair choices exactly when it is on axis $1$, leaving only $A_3$ for the right extension.

The left extension uses global points $z$, one axis point $x$ and diffuse plane points $y$. Its mixed $xyt$ coefficient is $\mu$; an $xy^2/2$ term also occurs. Put $Z=z+y$. The $t$-derivative Hessian in coordinates $(t,Z,x,y)$ is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 6q&2p&2u&-2g\\
 2p&n&\mu&0\\
 2u&\mu&0&0\\
 -2g&0&0&-f
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
When $f>0$, eliminating $-f$ replaces $q$ in the axis Hessian by
$q'=q+2g^2/(3f)$. Its determinant bound gives
\[
 2\mu\left(p^2-d^2-g^2-\frac32q\mu\right)
 \ge2g^2\mu\left(\frac{\mu}{f}-1\right)+2eu^2\ge0.
\]
Here $f\le\mu$. If $f=0$, then $g=0$ and the axis inequality applies directly. Together with the individual axis and plane bounds,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:star-bounds}
 p^2-g^2\ge\frac32nq,\quad
 p^2-d^2\ge\frac32q\mu,\quad
 p^2-d^2-g^2\ge\frac32q\mu.
\end{equation}

On the right use generic coordinate-plane points in $12$ and $13$. The Schur evaluation at weights $(p-d-g,d,g)$ gives
\[
 Q_D=p^2-d^2\ind_{3\notin D}-g^2\ind_{2\notin D},
 \qquad \beta_D=\mu+e\ind_{3\in D}.
\]
The bounds \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:star-bounds}, and $p^2\ge3nq/2$, prove
$Q_D\ge3q\beta_D/2$. Singleton types $1,2,3$ have pair contributions
$2p-d-g,2p-d,2p-g$, all at least $p$. Thus $\kappa(D)\ge2\beta_D$ by $p\ge n$ and monotonicity, proving the strict higher gap.

\subsection{The transposed star--edge core}
Take core rows $(13,12,12)$. Let $f_1,f_2$ count left vectors in planes $13,12$ and $g_1,g_2$ their independent-pair counts. The pair classes are disjoint. The support count is
\begin{align}
 C_0&=q+3p-g_1-2g_2+3n-f_2+1,\label{mrn:e:se:eq:transpose}\\
 C_1&=qU+(p-g_1-g_2)V+g_1V_{23}+g_2V_1
                  +(n-f_2)W+f_2W_{23},\notag\\
 C_2&=qa+(p-g_1-g_2)b+g_1B_{23}+g_2B_1,\qquad C_3=qT.\notag
\end{align}
A plane-$13$ pair loses core row $1$, and a plane-$12$ pair loses both rows $2,3$. A singleton in plane $12$ loses only the core pair $\{A_2,A_3\}$.

For the left two-plane/global extension put $Z=z+y_1+y_2$. The Hessian becomes
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 6q&2p&-2g_1&-2g_2\\
 2p&n&0&0\\
 -2g_1&0&-f_1&0\\
 -2g_2&0&0&-f_2
 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Eliminating negative entries gives
\[
 p^2\ge\frac32nq+n\left(\frac{g_1^2}{f_1}+\frac{g_2^2}{f_2}\right).
\]
Terms with $f_i=0$ are omitted, since then $g_i=0$. As $f_i\le n$,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:two-plane}
 p^2-g_1^2-g_2^2\ge\frac32nq,\qquad
 (p-g_2)^2\ge\frac32q(n-f_2),
\end{equation}
the latter being the individual plane bound.

The right dual extension is plane $23$ plus transverse axis $1$. The Schur quadratic is $p^2-g_1^2-g_2^2$ when $D$ meets $23$ but avoids $1$, $p^2-g_2^2$ when it meets both, and $(p-g_2)^2$ when $D=\{1\}$. The corresponding $\beta_D$ values are $n,n,n-f_2$. Thus \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:two-plane} proves the first criterion. The singleton coefficients prove the second using $p\ge n$ and $p-g_2\ge n-1$. Hence the higher gap is strict.

\subsection{The exact lower-gap certificates}
For each of the three cores and a right neighborhood $S$, let $\kappa(S)$ be the coefficient of its activity in $C_1$. For an independent pair $S,T$, let $\lambda(S,T)$ be the coefficient of its activity product in $C_2$. Both are given directly by the support formulas above: $V_D$ uses two-slot matchability, and $B_D$ uses three-slot matchability.

The product coefficient in $8C_1^2-15C_0C_2$ is
\[
 J(S,T)=16\kappa(S)\kappa(T)-15C_0\lambda(S,T).
\]
For every independent unordered pair of nonempty neighborhoods, the package gives an exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:se:eq:cert}
 (n-2)J(S,T)=1+\sum_\alpha r_\alpha\prod_jG_j^{\alpha_j},
 \qquad r_\alpha>0,\quad\alpha_j\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}.
\end{equation}
There are 25 pair types for each core. The generator counts and positive-term counts are
\[
 \begin{array}{c|r|r|r}
 \text{core}&\text{generators}&\text{identities}&\text{terms}\\\hline
 (123,12,1)&34&25&2369\\
 (123,23,1)&46&25&989\\
 (13,12,12)&32&25&2259
 \end{array}
\]
All 75 identities, totaling 5617 terms, pass a standard-library rational reconstruction and a separately written symbolic reconstruction. The former hardcodes the generator and profile formulas and does not import the discovery code or evaluate stored expressions. No numerical optimizer is used in either replay.

All generators are nonnegative counts or established flag inequalities; the complete families and their justifications are given below. Thus \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:cert} proves strict positivity. Dependent repeated-singleton types and individual activity squares have no $C_2$ contribution and a positive $C_1$-square coefficient. This proves the first inequality in \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:targets}.

\subsubsection{Generator families and their hypotheses}
The base generators are the nonnegative quantities in \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:counts}, the pair and triple subset upper bounds, and $n-4$, $3q-2p$. Coloop-freeness gives $n\ge4$ and every independent pair has at least two basis extensions, so $2p\le3q$.

For an axis class of size $e$, put $\mu=n-e$ and $u=p-d$. The projection onto the complementary coordinate plane is a coloop-free rank-two matroid: a coloop there would put all other original vectors into the preimage of a line, contradicting the original coloop-free rank three. Consequently $\mu\ge3$ and $u\ge\mu$. The axis generators, besides nonnegative counts, are
\begin{align*}
 &d-e\mu,\quad q-eu,\quad q+eu-2d,\\
 &n(n-1)-e(e-1)-2p,\quad \mu(\mu-1)-2u,\\
 &H=4pu\mu-2nu^2-3q\mu^2,\\
 &2p^2-3nq-2d^2+4eq,\quad
 \mu(\mu-1)(\mu-2)+6eu-6q.
\end{align*}
All axis/nonaxis pairs contribute $e\mu$ to $d$; bases containing an axis element number exactly $eu$. A basis containing an axis element has two axis-spanning pairs, and another basis has at most one. Pair--basis incidence counting yields $2d\le q+eu$. The subset upper bounds are immediate. The Hessian gives $H\ge0$, and
\[
 2\mu\left[p^2-\frac32nq-d^2+2eq\right]
       =H+e(2u^2+\mu q)
\]
gives the remaining mixed inequality.

For a plane class of size $f$, pair count $g$, $m=n-f$ and $h=p-g$, the generators are
\begin{align*}
 &f,\ m-2,\ g,\ f(f-1)-2g,\ h-fm,\\
 &2fm+m(m-1)-2h,\quad q-mg,\quad h-n+1,\\
 &6mg+3fm(m-1)+m(m-1)(m-2)-6q,\\
 &2fp^2-3fnq-2ng^2,\quad 2h^2-3qm.
\end{align*}
Coloop-freeness gives $m\ge2$. The $fm$ cross-plane pairs are all independent. Bases containing an independent plane pair number exactly $mg$; the remaining bases have one plane element and two outside elements, or three outside elements. These observations prove the counting inequalities. The last two are the individual plane Hessian bounds.

In the nested case, $e\le f$, and the stronger inequalities used are
\begin{align*}
 &g\ge e(f-e),\qquad 2g\le f(f-1)-e(e-1),\\
 &e(n-f)\le d-g\le e(n-f)+\binom{n-f}{2},\\
 &2u[2(p-g)-u]\ge3q(n-f).
\end{align*}
An axis-spanning pair with one vector in the plane and one outside must have its plane vector on the axis; otherwise both vectors would lie in the plane. The other such pairs have both vectors outside. The final inequality was proved by the nested Hessian.

In the transverse axis/plane case put $k=n-e-f\ge0$. In addition to $d+g\le p$ and the combined bound \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:star-bounds}, the certificate uses
\begin{align*}
 &p-g\ge e\mu+fk,\qquad q\ge eu+kg,\\
 &q\le eu+kg+f\binom{k}{2}+\binom{k}{3},\\
 &2f(n-f)+(n-f)(n-f-1)-e(e-1)-2(p-g)\ge0.
\end{align*}
The first counts axis/nonaxis and plane/outside-both pairs. Bases are partitioned according to whether they contain an axis element; without one, two plane elements and one outside-both element give exactly $kg$. The last upper bound removes the dependent axis pairs from the outside set.

Two stronger linear bounds are also used:
\[
 u\ge2\mu-4,\qquad p-g\ge2n-4.
\]
For the first, every parallel class in the projected coloop-free rank-two matroid has size at most $\mu-2$. If its largest class has size at least two, cross pairs alone give $2\mu-4$; if that size is one, all pairs are independent. For the second, if $2\le f\le n-2$ use $f(n-f)\ge2n-4$, and if $f\le1$ use $g=0$ and $p\ge2n-3$.

Finally the two-plane case adds $p-g_1-g_2\ge0$ and \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:two-plane}. The separate plane pair classes are disjoint. Redundant linear generators such as $d,u,\mu,n,p,q,n-f,p-g,n-e-f$ are explicitly retained when nonnegative in the relevant geometry. This matters for the bounded-degree certificate, although it adds no hypothesis. The exact ordered generator recipes and all rational multipliers are supplied in the package.

\subsection{From conditional gaps to eventual strictness}
Outside the small-cover branch, scale all $B$ activities by $\lambda$. At support orders three through six the leading $\lambda^3$ coefficients are the fixed forced-activity product times $C_0,C_1,C_2,C_3$. Thus \eqref{mrn:e:se:eq:targets} makes the last two leading gaps positive.

At orders one, two and three, write the leading coefficients as $a_1,a_2,a_3>0$. They form the support sequence of the induced graph on the three $B$ vertices, so
\[
 a_1^2\ge3a_2,\qquad a_2^2\ge3a_1a_3.
\]
The first two rank-six gaps have positive leading coefficients
\[
 5a_1^2-12a_2\ge3a_2>0,\qquad
 8a_2^2-15a_1a_3\ge9a_1a_3>0.
\]
At the third gap the square has degree six in $\lambda$ and the adjacent product has degree at most five. This proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:se:thm:main}.

Writing each gap as
$\Delta_j(\lambda)=a_{j,d_j}\lambda^{d_j}+\sum_{i<d_j}a_{j,i}\lambda^i$,
a computable sufficient threshold is
\[
 \lambda\ge1+\max_{1\le j\le5}
             \frac{\sum_{i<d_j}|a_{j,i}|}{a_{j,d_j}}.
\]
The threshold is field-dependent. Nothing here establishes all-finite-activity ULC6.

The package \mnote{shipped as \path{code/29-six-edges-*.py} and \path{data/29-six-edges-*.json}; the three \path{find_*_first.json} files are \path{data/29-six-edges-find_*_first.json}} includes 300 exact support regressions, all generator checks on their coloop-free examples, both complete rational reconstructions and an independent census of the six-edge core orbits. The finite graph tests check the formulas; the unbounded-population and positive-real-activity conclusions follow from the proof and exact identities.

%% ======================== source 08 ========================
\section{Eventual one-shore scaling through rank seven (source~08)}\label{mrn:src:08}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~08, as delivered.} For a bipartite graph of matching rank seven, unit left activities and arbitrary positive right activities, common scaling of the right part of any fixed minimum cover is eventually ultra-log-concave after normalization by the actual matching rank. The new case is the middle gap of the quartic obtained by forcing a $4+3$ cover's three right vertices. We prove the uniform bound $C_2^2\ge(15/8)C_1C_3$, strictly stronger than the required factor $9/5$. The proof compares the conditional quartic with a rank-four matroid specialization. A coefficientwise upper incidence inequality complements the earlier lower incidence bound, and a summation over pair annihilators controls the remaining cubic sector. This closes the last rank-seven eventual-scaling case and improves the known persistent-failure bracket to $[8,38]$. General finite-activity rank-six and rank-seven one-shore ultra-log-concavity remain open.
\mnote{At matching number six, gaps one, two and five hold at every finite one-shore activity (Part~III); gaps three and four, and rank seven at finite activity, stay open.}

\subsection{Main theorem and normalization}
For a finite bipartite graph $G=(X,Y)$, let
\[
 P_G(t)=\sum_{\substack{I\subseteq X,\ J\subseteq Y\\|I|=|J|,\ G[I,J]\text{ perfectly matchable}}}u_Iv_Jt^{|I|}.
\]
Every feasible endpoint pair is counted once, regardless of its number of perfect matchings. If the matching rank is $d$ and $P_G=\sum p_jt^j$, the actual-rank Newton gaps are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:gaps}
 G_j=j(d-j)p_j^2-(j+1)(d-j+1)p_{j-1}p_{j+1}.
\end{equation}
We write $P_G\in\ULC_d$ if all these gaps are nonnegative and there are no internal zeros.

Fix a minimum vertex cover $A\sqcup B$, with $A\subseteq X$, $B\subseteq Y$. Put $L=X\setminus A$ and $R=Y\setminus B$. Give every left vertex activity one and every right vertex a fixed positive activity. In common right-cover scaling, replace $v_y$ by $\lambda v_y$ for $y\in B$ only, with $\lambda>0$.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:r7:thm:global}
For every graph of matching rank at most seven, every fixed minimum cover and every fixed positive right field, $P_G\in\ULC_d$ for all sufficiently large $\lambda$. For rank seven, the gaps are eventually all strict when the cover splits $3+4$ or $4+3$.
\end{theorem}
The global statement is nonstrict: the small-cover cases can include equal-weight unions of stars. The threshold depends on the graph, the cover and the fixed field. \mnote{Source~01 (Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r8:thm:global}) extends Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r7:thm:global} to matching number eight.}

The earlier structural companion \cite{b:m08s}, included unchanged with this report, proves the $3+4$ case and the first and last conditional gaps for a $4+3$ cover. \mnote{Source~08s, printed as Section~\ref{mrn:src:08s}; the embedded archive is not shipped.} We settle its one remaining middle gap. Assume henceforth
\[
 |A|=4,\qquad |B|=3,\qquad \nu(G)=7.
\]
Exterior matchings have ranks three on $L$--$B$ and four on $A$--$R$. Forcing every $B$ vertex into the right endpoint set and dividing out $v_B$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:C}
 t^3C(t),\qquad C(t)=C_0+C_1t+C_2t^2+C_3t^3+C_4t^4,
\end{equation}
with every coefficient positive.

\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:e:r7:thm:middle}
For every genuine $4+3$ cover under the stated activities,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:middle}
 C_2^2\ge\frac{15}{8}C_1C_3>\frac95C_1C_3.
\end{equation}
In particular the conditional middle gap has the uniform strict margin
\[
 C_2^2-\frac95C_1C_3\ge\frac3{40}C_1C_3>0.
\]
\end{theorem}
No hypothesis is imposed on the core's support, rank, connectivity or number of exterior vertices.

\subsection{Two bounds for plane incidence}
Let $\mathcal L$ be a loopless rank-three vector configuration, allowing repeated and parallel labeled vectors, and fix a plane $H$ in its ambient row space. Original elements may have arbitrary positive weights. Let $q$ count weighted independent triples and let $\beta$ be the mass outside $H$.

For every independent pair $P$ whose plane is not $H$, its intersection $\spn(P)\cap H$ is a line. Let $D_H$ be the weighted count of unordered pairs of such labeled pairs having different intersection lines. Equivalently, it counts two pair annihilators independent after restriction to $H$; each virtual pair has weight equal to the product of its original element weights.

\begin{lemma}\label{mrn:e:r7:lem:bounds}
For every such configuration,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:DH}
 \frac34q\beta\le D_H\le q\beta.
\end{equation}
The upper inequality holds coefficientwise in the original element weights.
\end{lemma}
\mnote{The lower bound is source~08s's Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:tg:lem:plane} at $b=3$ (also source~21's Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:ec:lem:incidence}); the coefficientwise upper bound is new here.}
\begin{proof}
For the lower bound, write $h$ for the weighted count of independent pairs not spanning $H$, grouped by intersection-line counts $h_\delta$. Then
\[
 2D_H=h^2-\sum_\delta h_\delta^2\ge\frac32q\beta
\]
by the two-plane incidence lemma of \cite[Lemma 2.1]{b:m08s}. Its proof is a positive-pivot Schur argument for a rank-three matroid with additional generic points in $H$.

For the upper bound, compare coefficients of original element weights. A term in $D_H$ uses three or four distinct labels. A squared-label monomial $w_i^2w_jw_k$ contributes exactly when $i,j,k$ form a basis and $i$ lies outside $H$: the planes of $ij$ and $ik$ meet in the line of $i$. This is exactly its coefficient in $q\beta$. Identical pair labels contribute zero.

Now consider four distinct labels. A loop or total rank at most two makes both coefficients zero. If a pair is parallel and total rank is three, the two remaining pair partitions are good precisely when the common parallel direction is outside $H$; the $q\beta$ coefficient is the same, either zero or two.

If there is no parallel pair but one dependent triple, call it $i,j,k$, with the fourth vector outside their plane. The three pair partitions meet in the lines of $i,j,k$ respectively. Thus both coefficients equal the number of those three vectors outside $H$.

The remaining restriction is $U_{3,4}$, where every triple is independent. The coefficient of $q\beta$ is the number of the four vectors outside $H$. There are at most three pair partitions. At least two vectors lie outside $H$; if exactly two do, the pair of inside vectors spans $H$, invalidating one partition and leaving at most two. Thus $q\beta$ dominates in every case. This proves the coefficientwise inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{mrn:e:r7:lem:adjoint}
For every independent pair $P$ of $\mathcal L$, take an annihilator $v_P$ with activity equal to the product of its original weights. Let $F_{\mathcal L}$ count independent unordered triples of these labeled virtual points. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:adjoint}
 F_{\mathcal L}\ge\frac34q^2.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For fixed $P$, apply the lower bound in \eqref{mrn:e:r7:eq:DH} with $H=\spn(P)$. Let $D_P$ count unordered virtual pairs completing $v_P$ to a basis, and let $\beta_P$ be the original weight outside $\spn(P)$. Then $D_P\ge3q\beta_P/4$. Summing over $P$ gives
\[
 3F_{\mathcal L}=\sum_P w_PD_P
 \ge\frac34q\sum_Pw_P\beta_P=\frac94q^2.
\]
Each original basis has three pair-plus-outside decompositions. Each virtual basis has three choices of marked virtual point. Pairs sharing original labels retain their product weights on both sides. Dividing by three proves the result.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The auxiliary rank four matroid}
Represent graph edges by independent indeterminates. A square minor is nonzero exactly when its endpoints admit a perfect matching. Write $K$ for the $4\times3$ core, $\ell_i$ for the exterior-left rows and $r_j$ for the exterior-right columns. Let $q\ge1$ be the unit count of independent triples of the $\ell_i$.

For every independent labeled $L$ pair $P$, choose an annihilator $v_P$ and introduce the virtual right point
\[
 w_P=Kv_P.
\]
Retain all virtual labels, including loops and parallel elements. Adjoin the four private $A$-coordinate vectors $E$. The resulting rank-four matroid has a Lorentzian basis polynomial \cite{b:BH}. Give the private elements common mark $s$, original $R$ elements mark $t$ times their actual activities, and virtual elements mark $z$ and activity one. Denote the specialization by $F(s,t,z)$.

We name the relevant coefficients as follows:
\begin{align}
 F(s,t,z)={}&Tt^4+Ast^3+Us^2t^2+Vs^3t+s^4\notag\\
 &+Pzt^3+Wzst^2+Xzs^2t+\cdots\notag\\
 &+Dz^2t^2+E_2z^2st+F_3z^3t+\cdots.\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:F}
\end{align}
All are weighted basis counts with no factorial normalization. In particular $D,E_2$ use unordered pairs of virtual labels and $F_3$ uses unordered triples.

Partition the forced coefficients by their selected $L$ size. Let $N_2$ be the $C_2$ sector with one $L$ vertex and all four $A$ vertices. Let $N_1$ be the $C_1$ sector with one $L$ vertex and three $A$ vertices, and let $Z$ be its sector with no $L$ vertices. Thus $Z$ is the sum of activities of $r$ for which $[K\ r]$ is nonsingular. The exact identities are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:sectors}
 \begin{aligned}
 C_1&=qV+X+N_1+Z, & C_2&=qU+W+N_2,\\
 C_3&=qA+P, & C_4&=qT.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Three selected independent $L$ rows contribute the terms multiplied by $q$; two rows yield the virtual point after elimination; one and zero rows give the indicated remaining sectors. Private complements record the selected $A$ coordinates. This is a partition of endpoint sets, not of their matching allocations.

More explicitly, for a fixed independent pair of original $R$ vectors, quotient the right space by their span. If the induced $2\times3$ core has rank two, $N_2$ counts the original $L$ vectors complementary to its row plane; if it has smaller rank, the contribution vanishes. For $N_1$, use instead a pair $(r,e)$ with one original $R$ vector and one omitted private coordinate. Write its outside count as $\beta_{r,e}$; then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:N1}
 N_1=\sum_{r,e}v_r\beta_{r,e}.
\end{equation}
A dependent pair or deficient quotient core has $\beta_{r,e}=0$.

Substitute $s=qu$ and $z=u$ in $F$. This gives a homogeneous Lorentzian quartic $f(t,u)$ whose relevant coefficients are
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:B}
 \begin{aligned}
 [t^3u]f&=C_3,\\
 B_2:=[t^2u^2]f&=q^2U+qW+D=q(C_2-N_2)+D,\\
 B_1:=[tu^3]f&=q^3V+q^2X+qE_2+F_3.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Comparing the three middle coefficients}
Apply the upper bound of Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:r7:lem:bounds} in the quotient for each fixed independent $R$ pair. It gives $D_J\le q\beta_J$; singular quotient maps give zero on both sides. Summing with product activities yields
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:upperB}
 D\le qN_2,\qquad B_2\le qC_2.
\end{equation}
The same quotient geometry for an independent pair $(r,e)$, using the lower bound instead, gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:E2}
 E_2\ge\frac34qN_1.
\end{equation}
Here $E_2$ counts each unordered virtual pair once for each fixed $r,e$, exactly as in Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:r7:lem:bounds}.

For a fixed $r$, quotient the right space by its line. The induced $3\times3$ core map is invertible precisely when $[K\ r]$ is nonsingular. In that case it preserves the independence of every virtual triple; otherwise no three virtual points complete $r$ to a basis. Hence, exactly,
\[
 F_3=ZF_{\mathcal L}\ge\frac34q^2Z
\]
by Lemma~\ref{mrn:e:r7:lem:adjoint}. Together with \eqref{mrn:e:r7:eq:sectors}, \eqref{mrn:e:r7:eq:B} and \eqref{mrn:e:r7:eq:E2}, this proves
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:lowerB}
 B_1\ge q^2\left(C_1-\frac{N_1+Z}{4}\right).
\end{equation}

It remains to bound the lower sectors by $C_1$, and this is where the unit left activities are used. For a fixed independent pair $(r,e)$, let $h_{r,e}$ count virtual points independent of its span. If the quotient core has rank two, this is the number of independent original $L$ pairs whose plane does not contain its row plane $H$. Every original element outside $H$ extends to at least two independent pairs, and each pair contains at most two outside elements. Therefore
\[
 h_{r,e}\ge\beta_{r,e}.
\]
If the quotient core has smaller rank, the right side is zero and the inequality is trivial. For a dependent pair $(r,e)$ we set both quantities to zero.

Every counted $(P,e)$ gives three independent vectors $r,w_P,e$, which can be completed by at least one further private coordinate $e'$. A basis $r,w_P,e,e'$ has two choices of first private coordinate. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:X}
 2X\ge\sum_{r,e}v_rh_{r,e}\ge N_1.
\end{equation}
Also $q\ge1$ and every nonzero $r$ is completed by at least one triple of private coordinates. The indicator contributing to $Z$ is at most one, so $qV\ge Z$. Equation \eqref{mrn:e:r7:eq:sectors} now gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:e:r7:eq:unit}
 C_1\ge\frac32N_1+2Z\ge\frac32(N_1+Z),
 \qquad N_1+Z\le\frac23C_1.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The middle gap and eventual scaling}
The degree-four Newton inequality for $f$ has the factor $\binom42^2/(\binom41\binom43)=9/4$. By \eqref{mrn:e:r7:eq:B}--\eqref{mrn:e:r7:eq:unit},
\begin{align*}
 q^2C_2^2&\ge B_2^2\ge\frac94 B_1C_3\\
 &\ge\frac94q^2\left(C_1-\frac{N_1+Z}{4}\right)C_3
 \ge\frac{15}{8}q^2C_1C_3.
\end{align*}
Cancel $q^2>0$. This proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r7:thm:middle}, with margin $15/8-9/5=3/40$.

For clarity, all remaining inputs to Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r7:thm:global} are in the bundled structural companion \cite{b:m08s}. Its general top-gap theorem gives, for $4+3$,
\[
 C_3^2\ge\frac{50}{21}C_2C_4>\frac73C_2C_4.
\]
The ambient transversal-matroid specialization gives $C_1^2>2C_0C_2$, stronger than the required first factor $5/3$. The new middle theorem is stronger than $9/5$. Thus all three forced quartic gaps are strict for every fixed field.

At indices below $|B|$, the leading coefficients under scaling are those of the graph restricted to right shore $B$. Its degree-$|B|$ matroid normalization is stronger than the actual-rank-seven normalization, so those leading gaps are strictly positive. At index $|B|$, the square has one more power of $\lambda$ than the neighboring product. At the remaining indices, the strict leading terms are exactly the conditional gaps above. Hence every gap polynomial has positive leading coefficient and is positive for sufficiently large $\lambda$.

The $3+4$ case is already proved in \cite{b:m08s}. If a minimum cover has one shore of size at most two, the earlier small-cover theorem gives actual-rank ULC at every positive field; its full proof is included in that companion's prerequisite archive. These exhaust all minimum-cover splits at rank seven. At rank six the only further split is $3+3$, also handled by the structural companion; lower ranks have a cover shore of size at most two. This proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r7:thm:global}.

For any gap polynomial with positive leading coefficient, an explicit field-dependent bound is available. If
\[
 G_j(\lambda)=a_D\lambda^D+\sum_{i<D}a_i\lambda^i,\qquad a_D>0,
\]
then $\lambda\ge1+\sum_{i<D}|a_i|/a_D$ makes its leading term dominate the absolute sum of the others. Taking the maximum of these finitely many bounds gives a sufficient threshold in the new strict-cover cases.

\subsection{Failure ranks and verification scope}
The known upper witness for persistent one-shore failure uses the same activity convention \cite{b:m55}. Let $H_{8,30;32,794}$ have shores $A\sqcup L$ and $B\sqcup R$, of those block sizes, and edges
\[
 (A\times B)\cup(L\times B)\cup(A\times R).
\]
Every left activity and every exterior-right activity is one, and each $B$ activity is $w$. Its matching rank is $38$, and its last gap is
\[
 G_{37}=\frac5{6304}D^2w^{58}
 \bigl(-22w^2+6437219280w+11385894392295\bigr),
 \qquad D=\binom{32}{30}\binom{794}{7}.
\]
It is negative for every sufficiently large $w$. Together with Theorem~\ref{mrn:e:r7:thm:global}, this places the minimum rank of persistent failure under common right-cover scaling in $[8,38]$. \mnote{Improved to $[9,38]$ by source~01.}

This conclusion is distinct from the finite-activity problem. We do not prove ULC at every positive field for general rank-six or rank-seven graphs. The existing finite one-shore-failure bracket remains $[6,38]$ \mnote{its lower end rests on source~74, Part~I}; the two-shore-weighted problem has different counterexamples and is not the subject of this theorem.

The exact checker \mnote{shipped as \path{code/08-rank-seven-*.py} (without \texttt{top-gap}) and \path{data/08-rank-seven-middle_checks.json}, \path{data/08-rank-seven-replay.log}} uses a finite-field representation and independent endpoint-set enumeration on $120$ genuine $4+3$ graphs.
