One standard proof starts from a maximum matching and its alternating paths from unmatched left vertices: if $Z_L,Z_R$ are the reached vertices, then $(L\setminus Z_L)\sqcup Z_R$ meets every edge and takes one endpoint of each matching edge, hence has the matching's size. An augmenting path to an unmatched right vertex is excluded by maximality. Every cover has size at least a matching's size, giving equality.

For $d\le3$ every split of a minimum cover has a part of size at most one. At $d=4$, up to exchanging shores, the splits are $0+4$, $1+3$, and $2+2$. The first two are settled above. We now prove Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:mixed}, which settles the last.

\mnote{Sections~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:sec:path}--\ref{mrn:v:rf4:sec:full} are
source~77's proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:mixed}, a second route to the
univariate conclusion of source~76's Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:sb:thm:main}. For the
first three core types (no core edge, one edge, two adjacent edges) that
conclusion is also \prp's Corollary~94.3 (Part~VI,
\path{ade:mat:cor:block-family}), as noted in Section~\ref{mrn:w:sb:sec:tools}.}

\subsection{Two-center support data and path realizations}\label{mrn:v:rf4:sec:path}

Throughout the mixed-cover proof, write the cover as $\{a_1,a_2\}\subseteq L$ and $\{b_1,b_2\}\subseteq R$. Every exterior left vertex has neighborhood among the $b$-centers, and every exterior right vertex among the $a$-centers. No edge joins two exterior vertices. Ignoring isolated vertices, there are three neighborhood types on each exterior shore. The induced graph on the four centers is called the \emph{core}.

\subsubsection{The local quadratic and rooted path model}
For a component with two centers $a,b$ of activities $u,v$, denote exterior activity sums of neighborhoods $\{a\},\{b\},\{a,b\}$ by $X,Y,Z$. Let
\[
E=\sum_{i<j}z_i z_j=\tfrac12\left(Z^2-\sum_i z_i^2\right),
\qquad0\le E\le Z^2/2,
\]
where $z_i$ are the common-neighbor activities. Then its support polynomial and its root-$a$ deletion are
\begin{align}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:side}
p(t)&=1+(P+Q)t+Bt^2,&q(t)&=1+Qt,\\
P&=u(X+Z),&Q&=v(Y+Z),\notag\\
B&=uv(XY+XZ+YZ+E).&&\notag
\end{align}
Two selected common neighbors contribute $E$, not $2E$. Thus
\[
PQ-B=uv(Z^2-E)\ge0.
\]
In particular the quadratic discriminant is nonnegative:
$(P+Q)^2-4B=(P-Q)^2+4(PQ-B)\ge0$. Its nonconstant zeros are negative.

If $P>0$, take an ordinary edge-weighted four-vertex path rooted at its first endpoint, with successive edge weights
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:path-weights}
P,\qquad Q-B/P,\qquad B/P.
\end{equation}
All weights are nonnegative. Its matching generating polynomial is $p$, because the only two-edge matching uses the first and third edges. Deleting the root leaves a two-edge path of total weight $Q$, with polynomial $q$. If $P=0$, then $B=0$; an isolated root and one edge of weight $Q$ give the same pair, or one may take a limit.

For any weighted path with edge weights $h_i\ge0$, let $J$ be the real symmetric zero-diagonal tridiagonal matrix with adjacent entries $\sqrt{h_i}$. The determinant recurrence gives
\[
\det(xI-J)=\sum_k(-1)^k m_kx^{N-2k},
\]
where $m_k$ are its ordinary matching coefficients. Every eigenvalue of $J$ is real. A nonzero zero $t$ of $\sum m_kt^k$ would satisfy this identity at an $x$ with $x^2=-1/t$, so $x$ is real and $t<0$. This is the classical path matching/spectral mechanism. Its application to supports below is justified by explicit polynomial identities.

\subsubsection{No core edges or one core edge}
With no core edge, the two two-center components are disjoint and $p_G=p_1p_2$, a product of real-rooted quadratics. With the sole core edge joining distinguished roots $a$ and $b$, let $c=u_av_b$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:bridge}
p_G=p_1p_2+ctq_1q_2.
\end{equation}
To see that no support is double-counted, separate it by its shore imbalance in the two components after deleting the core edge. A support not using that edge is balanced in each component and contributes to $p_1p_2$. A support using it has imbalance one, of opposite signs in the pieces, and after removing $a,b$ gives one pair of root-deleted balanced supports. These cases are disjoint and exhaustive.

Replace each component by its rooted path model \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:path-weights} and join the roots with weight $c$. This gives an eight-vertex weighted path satisfying the same identity \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:bridge}. Therefore its ordinary matching polynomial equals $p_G$, proving real-rootedness.

\subsubsection{Two adjacent core edges}
After transposing if needed, let the common endpoint $a$ of the two core edges lie in the first component, and let its neighbors be both centers of the second. Write $c_a$ for the activity of $a$, and $u,v,X,Y,Z,E$ for the second component's data. Put $N=X+Y+Z$ and
\[
p_2=1+At+Bt^2,\qquad Q_* =\frac{uvN}{u+v}.
\]
An imbalanced support contains $a$. In the second component it either uses one center and no exterior vertex, of total weight $u+v$, or both centers and one nonisolated exterior vertex, of total weight $uvN$. The latter support is counted once even if that exterior vertex can use either center. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:star}
p_G=p_1p_2+c_a(u+v)tq_1(1+Q_*t).
\end{equation}
Set $\theta=u/(u+v)$. Expansion gives the exact nonnegative certificate
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:star-margin}
Q_*(A-Q_*)-B
=uv\left(\left[\theta X-(1-\theta)Y+(\theta-\tfrac12)Z\right]^2
+\tfrac34Z^2-E\right)\ge0.
\end{equation}
Also $Q_*\le A$, since $A\ge\min(u,v)N$ and $uv/(u+v)\le\min(u,v)$. Thus $p_2$ and $1+Q_*t$ have the rooted path realization \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:path-weights}, now with $P=A-Q_*$ and $Q=Q_*$. Connect this path to the first rooted path with edge weight $c_a(u+v)$. Identity~\eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:star} again proves the desired real-rootedness. Degenerate zero parameters follow by limits.

\subsection{Hermitian realizations for the next two core patterns}\label{mrn:v:rf4:sec:hermitian}

Write the center activities as $\alpha_1,\alpha_2$ for $a_1,a_2$ and $\beta_1,\beta_2$ for $b_1,b_2$. For exterior left types let their sums be $L_1,L_2,Z$ and their common-type second elementary symmetric sum be $E$. On the right use $R_1,R_2,W,F$. Set
\[
c=\sqrt{Z^2-E},\qquad d=\sqrt{W^2-F},\qquad
Z/\sqrt2\le c\le Z,\quad W/\sqrt2\le d\le W.
\]
Replace the exterior left component by one common leaf adjacent to both $b$-centers and two private leaves. In an ordinary weighted matching model assign the two common edges weights $\beta_1c,\beta_2c$, and the two private edges weights
\[
\beta_1(L_1+Z-c),\qquad\beta_2(L_2+Z-c).
\]
Its matching polynomial is exactly the original two-center support polynomial: the two-edge coefficient is
$\beta_1\beta_2[(L_1+Z)(L_2+Z)-c^2]$.
The identity also holds after deleting either or both centers. Make the analogous replacement on the right using $d$, and restore each core edge with matching weight $\alpha_i\beta_j$. Denote the ordinary matching polynomial of the resulting surrogate by $M(t)$. We compare $M$ to $p_G$ explicitly; equality is not assumed.

\subsubsection{Cycle interference in a Gram determinant}
For a complex bipartite biadjacency matrix $B$, Cauchy--Binet gives
\[
\det(I+tBB^*)=\sum_{k,I,J}|\det B[I,J]|^2t^k.
\]
The terms pairing a matching with itself give its ordinary weighted matching polynomial when each matrix entry has modulus the square root of that edge's weight. Phases affect only the cross terms. If the surrogate has no two vertex-disjoint cycles, two different matchings on a fixed support differ on a single even cycle $C$ of length $2h$. Pairing its two alternating choices gives the correction
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:cycle}
2(-1)^{h-1}\Re\left(\prod_C\text{oriented amplitudes}\right)
 t^h M_{G'-V(C)}(t),
\end{equation}
where $G'$ is the surrogate and the product uses conjugates on alternate edges. The determinant signs differ by an $h$-cycle. Outside $C$ the matching is identical, which gives the deleted-complement polynomial. This derives the particular cycle expansion needed here from determinant permutations. A four-cycle contributes negatively and a six-cycle positively.

The cases below have intersecting cycles, and their deleted complements are forests. A successful choice of phases therefore gives $p_G=\det(I+tBB^*)$. The matrix $BB^*$ is positive semidefinite, so such a polynomial is a product of factors $1+\lambda t$, $\lambda\ge0$, and has only negative real zeros.

\subsubsection{Two disjoint core edges}
Choose core edges $a_1b_2,a_2b_1$. The surrogate has one cycle: a six-cycle through all four centers and the two common leaves. Its deleted complement has no edges. Set $T=\alpha_1\alpha_2\beta_1\beta_2$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:matching-correction}
M(t)-p_G(t)=TZWt^3.
\end{equation}
For justification, separate supports by the number of core edges in a realizing matching, which is fixed by their component imbalance. Zero core edges are handled exactly by the two local models. Two core edges use all four centers and no exterior vertices and have a unique realization. With one core edge, different choices can realize the same support only when it contains all four centers and one common exterior vertex on each shore. There are exactly two such choices, and the aggregate extra weight is $TZW$. This proves \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:matching-correction} at the support level.

The cycle amplitude has modulus $Tcd$. Choose its phase $\phi$ so that
\[
\cos\phi=-\frac{ZW}{2cd},
\]
which is possible because $2cd\ge ZW$. Equation~\eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:cycle} supplies exactly $-TZWt^3$ and yields the required Gram determinant. If $ZW=0$, the conclusion follows directly or by continuity.

\subsubsection{Three core edges and two coupled phases}
Take core edges $a_1b_1,a_1b_2,a_2b_1$. The surrogate has two four-cycles and one six-cycle: the four-cycles pass through $a_1$ with the left common leaf and through $b_1$ with the right common leaf; the six-cycle passes through all four centers and both common leaves. Choose oriented phase coordinates $\theta,\eta$ for the four-cycles. The six-cycle phase is $\theta-\eta$ after choosing consistent orientations. The cycle space has dimension two, so these two phases may be assigned independently, for instance using a spanning tree and the two non-tree edges.

Put
\[
x=2c\cos\theta,\quad y=2d\cos\eta,\quad
U=\alpha_1\beta_1\beta_2,\quad V=\alpha_1\alpha_2\beta_1,
\quad b=R_2+W,\quad a=L_2+Z.
\]
The two four-cycle corrections and the six-cycle correction are respectively
\[
-Uxt^2(1+\alpha_2bt),\qquad
-Vyt^2(1+\beta_2at),\qquad
2Tcd\cos(\theta-\eta)t^3.
\]
The deleted four-cycle complements are stars with the displayed total edge weights. Direct support counting gives
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:three-correction}
M(t)-p_G(t)=(UZ+VW)t^2+T(Zb+Wa)t^3.
\end{equation}
Here the $UZ$ and $VW$ terms are the duplicated supports consisting of the three centers of a four-cycle and one common neighbor. They may additionally include the remaining center and a neighbor of that center, giving $TZb$ and $TWa$. At their overlap, with all centers and two common neighbors, there are three realizing one-core-edge matchings, so the two corrections correctly subtract two copies. No other imbalance-one support has more than one core choice; the imbalance-zero local models already count supports correctly, and the two-core-edge matching is unique. This proves \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:three-correction} without counting matching multiplicities as support weights.

It remains to find phases solving
\begin{align}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:phase-target}
Ux+Vy&=UZ+VW,\\
2cd\cos(\theta-\eta)&=(x-Z)b+(y-W)a.\notag
\end{align}
The following elementary argument supplies them. Let $A=2c$, $B=2d$. The line $Ux+Vy=UZ+VW$ cuts the rectangle $[-A,A]\times[-B,B]$ in a closed segment containing $(Z,W)$ in its relative interior when the common-type sums are positive. At its endpoints a rectangle coordinate saturates. For each point on the segment the two possible values of the left side of the second equation are
\[
H_\pm(x,y)=\frac{xy\pm\sqrt{(A^2-x^2)(B^2-y^2)}}2.
\]
They are continuous and coincide at the segment endpoints. The affine target
$h(x,y)=(x-Z)b+(y-W)a$ vanishes at $(Z,W)$. At this point the branches straddle zero, because
\[
(A^2-Z^2)(B^2-W^2)\ge Z^2W^2.
\]
Indeed, writing $c^2=Z^2/2+s$ and $d^2=W^2/2+t$ with $s,t\ge0$, the difference is
$4sW^2+4tZ^2+16st\ge0$.

Move from $(Z,W)$ to either endpoint. Initially $H_- -h\le0\le H_+-h$, whereas at the endpoint both differences are equal. If that common value is positive, the lower branch crosses zero; if negative, the upper one does; if zero, the endpoint works. The intermediate value theorem gives phases solving \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:phase-target}. The corrections then turn $M$ into $p_G$, establishing its Gram determinant and real-rootedness. Absent common types or zero activities follow by adding arbitrarily small positive activities on auxiliary vertices and passing to a coefficientwise limit. A limit of real-rooted polynomials of bounded degree with constant term one remains real-rooted; roots may escape to infinity as the degree drops.

\subsection{The complete core and a positive semidefinite block matrix}\label{mrn:v:rf4:sec:full}

The full $K_{2,2}$ core requires a different aggregate construction. We first establish a two-center inequality that supplies enough phase freedom in a $2\times2$ coupling matrix.

\subsubsection{A side matrix and its variance margin}
Use positive center activities $u,v$ and exterior data $X,Y,Z,E$ from \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:side}. Put
\[
U=u+v,\qquad a=uv,\qquad
A=u(X+Z)+v(Y+Z),\qquad B=a(XY+XZ+YZ+E),
\]
\[
\alpha=\frac{a(X+Y+Z)}U,\qquad
G=\begin{pmatrix}
u(X+Z)&\sqrt{a(Z^2-E)}\\
\sqrt{a(Z^2-E)}&v(Y+Z)
\end{pmatrix},\qquad H=\adj(G).
\]
Both diagonal entries are nonnegative and $\det G=B\ge0$, so $G$ and $H$ are positive semidefinite, with trace $A$ and determinant $B$.

Write
\[
\theta=\frac uU,\quad z=\theta-\tfrac12,\quad h=\frac a{U^2},\quad
L=Z^2-E,\quad T=\theta X-(1-\theta)Y+zZ.
\]
Direct expansion gives the following exact certificates:
\begin{align}
D:=\alpha(A-\alpha)-B
 &=a(T^2+L-Z^2/4),\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:variance1}\\
\Delta:=A^2-4B
 &=U^2\bigl[(T+zZ)^2+4hL\bigr],\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:variance2}\\
2D-h\Delta
 &=a\bigl[(T-zZ)^2+(1+4z^2)(L-Z^2/2)\bigr]\ge0.\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:variance3}
\end{align}
Here $L\ge Z^2/2$, so all indicated margins are nonnegative. The first identity is also the adjacent-core certificate \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:star-margin}.

Let $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$ be the eigenvalues of $H$. Since
$D=(\alpha-\lambda_1)(\lambda_2-\alpha)$ after ordering them, $\alpha$ lies between them. If $\Delta>0$, choose probabilities $p_1,p_2$ with
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:side-probabilities}
p_1+p_2=1,\quad p_1\lambda_1+p_2\lambda_2=\alpha,
\qquad p_1p_2=\frac D\Delta\ge\frac a{2U^2}.
\end{equation}
If $\Delta=0$, the matrix $G$ is scalar. Its off-diagonal entry vanishes, so $Z^2-E=0$; combined with $E\le Z^2/2$ this gives $Z=0$. Equality of its diagonals gives $uX=vY$, whence $\alpha=A/2$ is its repeated eigenvalue. Take $p_1=p_2=1/2$, which satisfies the bound in \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:side-probabilities} because $a/U^2\le1/4$.

Repeat this construction for the other exterior component. Denote its quantities by $G',H',A',B',\beta,V,b$, and eigenvalue probabilities $q_1,q_2$. Thus
\[
q_1q_2\ge\frac b{2V^2},\qquad
q_1\lambda'_1+q_2\lambda'_2=\beta.
\]
These identities cover empty exterior populations as well as positive ones.

\subsubsection{The full-core support identity}
Set $p_A=1+At+Bt^2$, $p_B=1+A't+B't^2$, $c=UV$, and $d_0=ab$. For the complete core,
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:full-support}
p_G(t)=p_A(t)p_B(t)+ct(1+\alpha t)(1+\beta t)+d_0t^2.
\end{equation}
This follows by component imbalance. At imbalance zero no core edge is used, giving the product. At imbalance one, a component selects either one center and no exterior vertex, or both centers and one exterior vertex. Its total polynomial is
\[
U+a(X+Y+Z)t=U(1+\alpha t).
\]
Every such pair of component supports can be joined because every core edge is present, and each pair is counted once irrespective of available choices. Finally imbalance two consists solely of the four centers and contributes $ab t^2$ once. The complete core has two perfect matchings on that support, but there is only one support.

\subsubsection{An aggregate coupling with a prescribed determinant}
Choose eigenbases of $H,H'$ and regard $G,G'$ in the same respective bases. Let $C$ be a complex $2\times2$ matrix whose squared entry moduli are
\[
|C_{ij}|^2=cp_iq_j.
\]
Then, for every choice of entry phases,
\begin{align}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:trace-moments}
\tr(CC^*)&=c,&\tr(HCC^*)&=c\alpha,\\
\tr(CH'C^*)&=c\beta,&\tr(HCH'C^*)&=c\alpha\beta.\notag
\end{align}
Each identity is the corresponding sum of $cp_iq_j$ times zero, one, or two eigenvalues; the phases cancel because both $H,H'$ are diagonal.

Begin with nonnegative real entries and vary only the phase of $C_{22}$. The two terms in $\det C$ then have equal modulus
$c\sqrt{p_1p_2q_1q_2}$. Therefore $|\det C|^2$ takes every value in
\[
[0,4c^2p_1p_2q_1q_2].
\]
By \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:side-probabilities} its upper endpoint is at least
\[
4U^2V^2\frac a{2U^2}\frac b{2V^2}=ab=d_0.
\]
Choose the phase so that $|\det C|^2=d_0$. This enforces the four-center support coefficient without changing the four trace moments.

\subsubsection{The block determinant identity}
Let $G^{1/2},G'^{1/2}$ be positive semidefinite square roots and form
\[
\mathcal M=\begin{pmatrix}C&G^{1/2}\\G'^{1/2}&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
For $t>0$, the lower-right block $I+tG'$ of $I+t\mathcal M\mathcal M^*$ is invertible. Its Schur complement gives
\[
\det(I+t\mathcal M\mathcal M^*)
=\det(I+tG')\det\bigl(I+tG+tC(I+tG')^{-1}C^*\bigr).
\]
For $2\times2$ matrices, the determinant is quadratic in the added matrix. Expansion of the last expression yields
\begin{align}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:block-determinant}
\det(I+t\mathcal M\mathcal M^*)={}&\det(I+tG)\det(I+tG')\notag\\
&+t\tr\bigl(\adj(I+tG)C\adj(I+tG')C^*\bigr)
+t^2|\det C|^2.
\end{align}
Both sides are polynomials, so the identity holds for every $t$. Since
$\adj(I+tG)=I+tH$ and similarly on the other side, substituting \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:trace-moments} gives
\[
\det(I+t\mathcal M\mathcal M^*)
=p_Ap_B+ct+c(\alpha+\beta)t^2+c\alpha\beta t^3+d_0t^2=p_G(t).
\]
The matrix $\mathcal M\mathcal M^*$ is positive semidefinite. This proves real-rootedness for the full core. The construction matches the aggregate coefficient polynomial; it does not claim a faithful determinant realization of each individual support minor of the original graph.

\subsection{Completion and the real-rootedness boundary}\label{mrn:v:rf4:sec:completion}

The six core types above exhaust all subsets of the four possible core edges up to permutation of the two centers on either shore and transposition: their orbit sizes are $1,4,4,2,4,1$, totaling sixteen. Sections~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:sec:path}--\ref{mrn:v:rf4:sec:full} prove Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:mixed} for positive activities, and coefficientwise limits give nonnegative activities. Newton's classical inequalities give \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:newton} with the actual degree of each real-rooted polynomial. The constant term is one and all coefficients are nonnegative, so no nonconstant factor has a zero at zero or at a positive real number.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:main}]
Delete every zero-activity vertex. This preserves $p_G$ and leaves a positive-activity graph with matching number exactly $d\le4$. Remove isolated vertices and choose a minimum cover of size $d$ by K\H{o}nig's theorem. If one cover part has size at most one, Proposition~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:prop:thin} applies. The only remaining split is $2+2$, for which Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:mixed} and Newton's inequalities apply. Positivity of every coefficient through $d$ follows from submatchings. This proves the asserted actual-degree statement, including all zero-activity degeneracies.
\end{proof}

This initial deletion is essential for interpreting the normalization: merely taking a limit of inequalities normalized by the original rank gives that old normalization, which can be weaker than normalization by the surviving degree. The cover reduction is applied anew after deletion.

\subsubsection{Real roots can fail already at rank three}\label{mrn:v:rf4:sec:rank3-example}
Take three left centers and $n$ right vertices of each of the three pair-neighborhood types $\{1,2\}$, $\{1,3\}$, and $\{2,3\}$, all with unit activities. The support polynomial is
\[
p_n(t)=1+6nt+\frac{3n(7n-1)}2t^2+(4n^3-3n^2)t^3.
\]
Indeed each chosen left pair has two singleton exterior types of size $n$ and one common type of size $n$, giving $3n^2+\binom n2$ supports. Multiply by the three left pairs for the quadratic coefficient. A three-vertex right support is matchable to all three centers unless all its vertices belong to one pair type: every one- or two-vertex subset has at least its size in neighbors, and the full neighbor union has size three exactly in the remaining cases. Hall's elementary condition therefore gives
\[
a_3=\binom{3n}{3}-3\binom n3=4n^3-3n^2.
\]
The cubic discriminant is
\[
-\frac{27n^3}2(n^3-3n^2-3n-1).
\]
At $n=4$, the graph has matching number three and
\[
p_4(t)=1+24t+162t^2+208t^3,\qquad\operatorname{disc}(p_4)=-2592.
\]
It has a nonreal conjugate pair while its rank-normalized sequence $1,8,54,208$ is log-concave. The coefficient calculation and discriminant are verified exactly by the companion checker. Section~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:sec:minimum} proves its vertex minimality within unit-weight matching rank three. Historical priority and vertex minimality across other ranks are not asserted.
\mnote{Neither \prp{} nor Parts~I--IV print a non-real-rooted support
polynomial of matching number three. The graph's three centres form its only
minimum cover, on one shore, so it is $\ULC_3$ by
Lemma~\ref{mrn:w:rf:lem:singleton}; it has no $2+2$ or $1+2$ cover, for which
real-rootedness holds (Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:sb:thm:main}, \prp's Theorem~207.1).}


For comparison, the source's known rank-four theta graph of three internally disjoint length-three paths has polynomial
$1+9t+24t^2+16t^3+t^4$ and discriminant $-5243$ \cite[equation \texttt{lor:sp:eq:theta}]{b:PRP}. Its normalized sequence is $1,9/4,4,4,1$. This prior example is credited to the source and its cited antecedent. Both examples show why real-rootedness is restricted to the mixed-cover theorem, rather than asserted for every graph of matching number at most four.

\subsubsection{Why one weighted twin is not always enough}
Pendant compression works because at most one member of its class can be selected. A common-neighbor class adjacent to two centers can contribute two selected vertices and requires its second elementary symmetric activity sum. Even replacing $n\ge3$ unit-weight common twins by two independent nonnegative weights would require
\[
w_1+w_2=n,\qquad w_1w_2=\binom n2.
\]
Their discriminant would be $n^2-4\binom n2=n(2-n)<0$. Such a replacement is impossible. The path and matrix constructions instead preserve the needed aggregate support quantities, including this second moment.

\subsection{The smallest unit-weight counterexample at rank three}\label{mrn:v:rf4:sec:minimum}

The example of Section~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:sec:rank3-example} is minimal within its matching rank. The minimality part is computer-assisted: a structural reduction leaves a finite exhaustive integer calculation, implemented independently in two ways in the package.

\begin{theorem}[Sharp vertex minimum at matching rank three]\label{mrn:v:rf4:thm:minimum}
Among finite simple bipartite graphs with matching number exactly three and unit vertex activities, the smallest order for which the matching-support polynomial is not real-rooted is fifteen. Up to graph isomorphism and interchange of shores, the unique fifteen-vertex example has three vertices on one shore and four vertices of each two-element neighborhood type on the other. Its polynomial is
\[
1+24t+162t^2+208t^3,
\]
with discriminant $-2592$.
\end{theorem}

This is minimality within matching number three and unit activities. It is not a smallest non-real-rooted graph across all matching ranks: the credited rank-four theta graph above has eight vertices.
\mnote{Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:minimum} is computer-assisted. At the
placement of batch~73 the enumeration was repeated independently (a separate
Hall test and composition generator, not shipped), with the same counts,
$30{,}699$ rank-three vectors for $N\le11$ and $18{,}288$ at $N=12$, and the
same unique negative discriminant. The theorem concerns real-rootedness, not
$\ULC$: the least matching number of a unit-weight $\ULC$ failure is not
addressed (it lies in $[6,3450]$, Part~II). Matching number three is the only
case with content: a graph of matching number $\nu$ has at least $2\nu$
vertices, and for $\nu\ge4$ the theta graph with $\nu-4$ disjoint edges added
has exactly $2\nu$ vertices and the non-real-rooted unit polynomial
$(1+9t+24t^2+16t^3+t^4)(1+t)^{\nu-4}$; at $\nu\le2$ every support polynomial is
real-rooted (its degree is at most two and its discriminant is nonnegative by
\prp's Theorem~180.2).}


\subsubsection{A direct lemma for one by two covers}
\begin{lemma}\label{mrn:v:rf4:lem:one-two}
Every graph admitting a cover with one left vertex and two right vertices has a real-rooted support polynomial under arbitrary nonnegative vertex activities. The cover need not be minimum.
\end{lemma}
\mnote{This is \prp's Theorem~207.1 (Part~XIII, \path{lc:thm:real}), proved
there by an interlacing argument; its conclusion also follows from \prp's
Part~VI. Source~77's rooted-path proof is printed as a second route, because
the proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:minimum} uses the lemma.}

\begin{proof}
Write the cover as $\{a\}\sqcup\{b,c\}$. After deleting the core edges, the components are a rooted star at $a$ and a two-center component at $b,c$. If $a$ has activity $\alpha$ and its exterior neighbor activities sum to $R$, the star has polynomial $p_1=1+\alpha Rt$ and root-deletion polynomial one. \emph{Replace this rooted star by one ordinary weighted edge of weight $\alpha R$}, rooted at one endpoint; it preserves both polynomials. This replacement is necessary before the joined object is called a path.

For the two-center component use \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:side}, so $p_2=1+At+Bt^2$ and its rooted deletion at $b$ is $1+Qt$. There are three possible core types. With no core edge the polynomial is $p_1p_2$. With only $ab$, the support-imbalance argument gives
\[
p_G=p_1p_2+\alpha u\,t(1+Qt),
\]
where $u$ is the activity of $b$. Replace the second component by its rooted three-edge path \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:path-weights}, and join it to the replacement edge at the roots with weight $\alpha u$. This is an ordinary weighted path with polynomial $p_G$.

With both core edges, the same support count as \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:star} gives
\[
p_G=p_1p_2+\alpha(u+v)t(1+Q_*t),
\qquad Q_* =\frac{uv(X+Y+Z)}{u+v}.
\]
Certificate~\eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:star-margin} makes $p_2,1+Q_*t$ a rooted-path pair. Join that path to the replacement edge with weight $\alpha(u+v)$. All three cases are real-rooted by the path spectral argument. When the denominators are positive the constructions apply directly; zero denominators and zero activities follow by continuity.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{An exact reduction to seven populations}
Delete isolated vertices from any candidate. K\H{o}nig's theorem supplies a minimum cover of size three. Up to transposition it is split $0+3$ or $1+2$. The second case is real-rooted by Lemma~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:lem:one-two}. In the first, every nonisolated vertex on the cover's shore belongs to those three centers. Thus a counterexample has a shore of size exactly three, and is determined by the seven populations
\[
(n_1,n_2,n_{12},n_3,n_{13},n_{23},n_{123})
\]
of its nonempty opposite-neighborhood types, up to permuting the centers. Write $N$ for their sum. Fewer than fifteen vertices requires $N\le11$; at fifteen vertices, $N\le12$.

Here are the exact Hall formulas used by the independent enumeration. Index the types by nonempty subsets $S\subseteq\{1,2,3\}$, and set $p(t)=1+at+bt^2+ct^3$. Then
\[
a=\sum_S |S|n_S.
\]
For each center pair $i,j$, let $L_{ij}$ count opposite vertices meeting $\{i,j\}$, and $I_{ij},J_{ij}$ count those meeting that pair only at $i$ or only at $j$. Its size-two contribution is
\[
\binom{L_{ij}}2-\binom{I_{ij}}2-\binom{J_{ij}}2;
\]
summing over the three pairs gives $b$. For each center $i$, let $M_i=\sum_{S:i\notin S}n_S$. Inclusion-exclusion first counts triples whose neighborhood union is all three centers. Among those triples the only remaining Hall obstruction is two vertices of the same singleton type. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:hall-cubic}
c=\binom N3-\sum_i\binom{M_i}3+\sum_i\binom{n_{\{i\}}}3
-\sum_i\binom{n_{\{i\}}}2
\bigl(n_{\{1,2,3\}\setminus\{i\}}+n_{\{1,2,3\}}\bigr).
\end{equation}
The bad triples in the last sum are disjoint for different $i$. These formulas count supports, without a choice of realizing matching. Rank three is equivalent to $c>0$.

For a genuine real cubic of this form, all roots are real, allowing repetitions, exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:cubic-discriminant}
\mathcal D=a^2b^2-4a^3c-4b^3-27c^2+18abc\ge0.
\end{equation}
The independent checker enumerates every weak composition of each $N=0,\ldots,12$ into seven populations by six separator positions among $N+6$ places, asserting that exactly $\binom{N+6}{6}$ vectors are visited at every $N$. It discards only $c=0$ vectors. The second implementation instead precomputes pair and triple feasibility by enumerating bijections to selected centers, and then weights type multisets by products of integer binomial coefficients. It uses a separate recursive composition enumeration.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
Opposite population & Rank-three vectors & Negative discriminants\\
\midrule
$0\le N\le11$ & $30{,}699$ & $0$\\
$N=12$ & $18{,}288$ & $1$\\
\midrule
Total & $48{,}987$ & $1$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Both exact computations find only the vector $(0,0,4,0,4,4,0)$, with coefficients $(1,24,162,208)$ and discriminant $-2592$. Every labeled population vector is checked, not merely an asserted list of orbit representatives. The unique negative vector is invariant under all center permutations, so it gives one graph up to isomorphism and shore exchange. This finite exhaustive calculation, combined with the structural reduction, proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:minimum}. A fifteen-vertex example cannot contain an isolate, since deleting it would contradict the smaller-order exclusion.

\subsection{A restricted rank-five family with four activity blocks}\label{mrn:v:rf4:sec:rank-five}

The next theorem settles one natural continuation of the known complete-core obstruction family. It imposes a complete $2+3$ core and activities constant within each of four blocks.

\begin{theorem}[Strict rank-five normalization for a complete block family]\label{mrn:v:rf4:thm:five}
Let $G$ have shores $A\sqcup X$ and $B\sqcup Y$, with
\[
|A|=2,\quad |B|=3,\quad |X|=n\ge3,\quad |Y|=m\ge2,
\]
and exactly the edge blocks $A\times B$, $A\times Y$, and $X\times B$. Give each of $A,B,X,Y$ its own constant positive vertex activity. Then $\nu(G)=5$ and all four order-five Newton inequalities are strict:
\[
k(5-k)a_k^2>(k+1)(6-k)a_{k-1}a_{k+1}\qquad(1\le k\le4).
\]
\end{theorem}
\mnote{The graph is source~75's full Hall graph $H_{2,3;n,m}$ with activities
constant on each of its four blocks. Source~75's
Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:fh:thm:classification} (case $\min(a,b)=2$) gives all four
inequalities strictly for \emph{every} positive activity assignment, and
source~72's Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:se:cor:smallrank} gives strictness for every
connected graph of matching number five; the nonstrict inequalities are
source~74's Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive}. Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:five}
is therefore a special case of Part~I. Source~77's finite certificate proof is
printed as a computer-assisted second route; the certificate is shipped as
\path{data/77-rank-four-rank-five-certificate.json}.}


The exterior populations $n,m$ are unbounded within the stated ranges. Unequal activities \emph{within} a block, missing core edges, and general rank-five graphs are not covered.

\subsubsection{Support formula and removal of exterior activities}
The five core vertices form a cover, while disjoint matchings from $A$ into $Y$ and from $B$ into $X$ attain size five. Thus the matching number is five. Suppose the block activities are $U,V,\ell,r>0$. Scaling all left activities by $1/\ell$ and all right activities by $1/r$ makes the exterior activities one and the core activities
\[
u=U/\ell,\qquad v=V/r.
\]
Every coefficient $a_k$ is multiplied by $(\ell r)^{-k}$, so its Newton margin is multiplied by the positive factor $(\ell r)^{-2k}$. Both the inequality and strictness are preserved.

In this normalized setting, a size-$k$ support selecting $i$ vertices of $A$ and $j$ vertices of $B$ is feasible exactly when $i+j\ge k$. Necessity follows because its $k-i$ exterior left vertices must use selected $B$ vertices, and its $k-j$ exterior right vertices must use selected $A$ vertices. Under the same inequality, match the exterior vertices into the opposite core, then match the equally sized remaining core subsets using the complete core. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:rf4:eq:rank-five-coefficients}
a_k=\sum_{\substack{0\le i\le2,\ 0\le j\le3\\i+j\ge k}}
\binom2i\binom3j\binom n{k-i}\binom m{k-j}u^iv^j,
\qquad0\le k\le5,
\end{equation}
with out-of-range binomial coefficients zero. This is a support count, not a matching count.

\subsubsection{A finite positive-coefficient certificate for all parameters}
Put $x=n-3\ge0$ and $y=m-2\ge0$. Every positive pair $u,v$ belongs to one or both of the following branches:
\[
\begin{array}{c|cc|cc}
&u&v&a&z\\\hline
0&(x+3)(a+z)&(y+2)a&v/m&u/n-v/m\ge0\\
1&(x+3)a&(y+2)(a+z)&u/n&v/m-u/n\ge0
\end{array}
\]
In both rows $a>0$. Equality of the two activity ratios is included as $z=0$ in either branch. For each branch substitute \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:rank-five-coefficients} into
\[
D_k=k(5-k)a_k^2-(k+1)(6-k)a_{k-1}a_{k+1},\qquad1\le k\le4.
\]
Using the polynomial convention $\binom Nq=\prod_{h=0}^{q-1}(N-h)/q!$ for $q\ge0$, expansion in $x,y,a,z$ gives only positive nonzero rational coefficients. The complete coefficient lists are included as \texttt{rank-five-certificate.json}; the following table records their sizes and smallest coefficients.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ccrrrr@{}}
\toprule
Branch & Certificate statistic & $D_1$ & $D_2$ & $D_3$ & $D_4$\\
\midrule
0 & Nonzero terms & 81 & 625 & 1225 & 1215\\
0 & Minimum coefficient & $10$ & $3/2$ & $6$ & $1/6$\\
1 & Nonzero terms & 81 & 625 & 1715 & 1701\\
1 & Minimum coefficient & $20$ & $15/2$ & $1/6$ & $1/6$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The package provides an exact symbolic generator and an independent checker using sparse polynomials with rational coefficients. The latter reconstructs the coefficients by exterior selection counts $p,q$, with core counts $i=k-p$, $j=k-q$ and $p+q\le k$, then compares all eight coefficient dictionaries to the certificate monomial by monomial. It uses neither the symbolic generator nor its polynomial implementation. Thus the supplied finite algebraic certificate establishes nonnegativity on the entire parameter domain, rather than on a numerical sample.

For strictness, at $x=y=z=0$ both branches give
\begin{align*}
D_1&=990a^2+5400a^3+4104a^4,\\
D_2&=192006a^4+469584a^5+548208a^6+326592a^7+69984a^8,\\
D_3&=858984a^6+2735424a^7+3100032a^8+1492992a^9+279936a^{10},\\
D_4&=272160a^8+881280a^9+590976a^{10}.
\end{align*}
These strictly positive monomials remain in the coefficientwise positive expansions for all $x,y,z\ge0$ and $a>0$. Therefore every $D_k>0$ throughout the stated domain. Submatchings of a size-five matching give $a_0,\ldots,a_5>0$, completing the proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:rf4:thm:five} with its exact finite certificate.

This family rules out a rank-five counterexample obtained by deleting one core vertex from the corresponding complete $3+3$-core Hall construction, with unequal exterior populations and arbitrary positive activities constant on each of the four blocks. It leaves the unrestricted weighted rank-five question open.
\mnote{Part~I rules out more: by the theorems cited above, no graph with a
$2+3$ minimum cover fails at any positive activities, so weighted rank five
holds without restriction (source~74) and the least weighted failure rank is
exactly six.}


\subsection{Verification and further questions}\label{mrn:v:rf4:sec:verification}

The accompanying exact checker enumerates all support subsets on 128 rationally weighted mixed-cover graphs, covering all sixteen core masks. It compares these labeled counts with a Boolean neighborhood-type kernel and with an independent formula obtained from the number of selected core edges. It checks all three rank-four Newton gaps with exact rational arithmetic. Additional symbolic checks verify the scalar margins, block determinant identity, the theta discriminant, and the pair-neighborhood cubic discriminant. The latter family is also counted directly for $1\le n\le6$. These checks corroborate the rank-four proof; their exact scopes and reproduction commands appear in the README and receipt.
\mnote{The delivered README is not shipped. The receipts are
\path{data/77-rank-four-verification.json},
\path{data/77-rank-four-rank-three-minimum-verification.json},
\path{data/77-rank-four-rank-three-bijections-verification.json},
\path{data/77-rank-four-rank-five-verification.json} and
\path{data/77-rank-four-rank-five-generation.json}; the commands are in this
report's README.}


The polynomial identity methods are not a numerical real-root search. The phase-existence argument is proved by continuity, and the full-core determinant capacity is certified by \eqref{mrn:v:rf4:eq:variance3}. The separate rank-three minimality theorem uses a complete exact enumeration after its structural reduction. The restricted rank-five theorem uses a finite rational coefficient certificate for an unbounded family, independently regenerated and checked. Neither result relies on a random screen. No Lean or other formal proof-assistant artifact is included.

The general theorem and restricted rank-five family leave several precise questions. Does arbitrary nonnegative two-shore weighting satisfy actual-degree normalization at matching number five? The inspected source has a rank-six weighted obstruction, so after the present theorem the least possible weighted counterexample rank is either five or six. For unit activities, is actual-rank normalization true at every matching number? The weighted counterexample does not settle that question. One may also ask which larger cover patterns admit aggregate Hermitian realizations, and whether the rank-four constructions can give useful equality or quantitative deficit classifications.
\mnote{Settled within this report: weighted rank five holds and the least
weighted failure rank is exactly six (source~74,
Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive}); unit-weight normalization fails at
matching number 3450 (source~71, Theorem~\ref{mrn:u:uw:thm:main}). Equality in
the rank-four inequalities is classified by source~72's
Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:main}. The shipped \path{77-rank-four-REVISION.md}
likewise calls general weighted rank five and the unit-weight all-rank question
unresolved; it is kept as delivered.}


The previously published smaller-shore inequality remains a separate, general theorem. The present proof uses that theorem in the thin-cover cases and additional support-specific structure in the mixed cover. It does not assert actual-rank ultra-log-concavity for arbitrary representable bimatroids; the source already supplies an obstruction to that broader statement at linear rank three.
\mnote{The obstruction at linear rank three is \prp's Theorem~192.1
(Part~XII, \path{mr:thm:matrix}).}

%% ======================== source 78 ========================
\section{Two-by-three cores with complete exteriors, and stable star cores (source~78)}\label{mrn:src:78}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~78, as delivered.} We prove actual-degree ultra-log-concavity for every bipartite graph with an arbitrary two-by-three internal core and two complete exterior blocks, under independent nonnegative vertex activities. All four inequalities are strict at degree five. The proof combines ordinary support and moment reductions with 26 exact rational certificates covering all 64 labeled cores. Two independent solver-free checkers replay the certificates. Empty and star cores satisfy the stronger conclusion of real-rootedness, by an ordinary stable-monomer argument. The complete-exterior hypothesis is essential to the scope of the result.
\mnote{Source~78 is batch-73 manuscript~08,
\path{all-two-by-three-cores-result.zip} (title page ``Research manuscript
prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov''; its PDF author field reads ``Research
manuscript prepared with OpenAI''). It cites only source~77 and the batch-73
archive \path{complete-two-by-three-result.zip} (manuscript~09), which it
extends: that archive's graph is the core mask~63 here, which is source~75's
full Hall graph $H_{2,3;n,m}$, and its theorem is the case $s=3$ of source~75's
Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:fh:thm:classification}; it is not printed. The displayed
cover $P\sqcup Q$ has five vertices, so Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:st:thm:main} is a
case of source~74's Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive}, and strictness at
$d=5$ is source~72's Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:main}, since such a graph is
not a forest of stars. Its proof by 26 exact certificates is printed as a
computer-assisted second route. Of Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:st:thm:star} only the
exceptional two-neighbour seed is new (see the note there).}

\subsection{Statement and normalization}
Let $P,Q,X,Y$ be disjoint finite sets with $|P|=2$, $|Q|=3$. Let $H$ be
any subset of $P\times Q$. Our bipartite graph has shores $P\sqcup X$ and
$Q\sqcup Y$ and edge set
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:st:eq:graph}
 H\ \cup\ (P\times Y)\ \cup\ (X\times Q).
\end{equation}
Give each left vertex activity $u_i\ge0$ and each right vertex activity
$v_j\ge0$. A feasible support $(S,T)$ has $S,T$ on the two shores,
$|S|=|T|$, and a perfect matching between them. Define
\[
 \Gamma(t)=\sum_{k\ge0}\gamma_kt^k,\qquad
 \gamma_k=\sum_{\substack{(S,T)\text{ feasible}\\|S|=|T|=k}}u^Sv^T.
\]
Each endpoint pair contributes once, regardless of its matching witnesses;
$\gamma_0=1$. The actual degree $d$ is the matching number after
zero-activity vertices are deleted. Positive submatchings give no internal
zeros. Rank-ultra-log-concavity means
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:st:eq:ulc}
 k(d-k)\gamma_k^2\ge(k+1)(d-k+1)\gamma_{k-1}\gamma_{k+1}
 \qquad(1\le k<d).
\end{equation}
\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:v:st:thm:main}
For every graph \eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:graph}, \eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:ulc} holds at the actual
surviving degree, with arbitrary independent nonnegative vertex activities.
If $d=5$, all four inequalities are strict.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{A case of Part~I: Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive} (source~74) for
the inequalities, Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:se:thm:main} (source~72) for
strictness. Sections~\ref{mrn:v:st:sec:boolean}--\ref{mrn:v:st:sec:outer}
below are source~78's computer-assisted second route.}

The physical cover $P\sqcup Q$ gives $d\le5$. If $d\le4$, the previously
proved arbitrary-weight bipartite rank-four theorem applies to the active
graph \cite[Theorem 1.1]{b:m77}. Its frozen proof package is included
inside the dependency archive. The new argument treats degree five, where
both $P$ activities, all three $Q$ activities, and at least three activities
in $X$ and two in $Y$ are positive. No equality of activities within a block
or bound on the exterior populations is assumed.

\subsection{Boolean support reconstruction}\label{mrn:v:st:sec:boolean}
A size-$k$ support selecting core subsets $I\subseteq P$, $J\subseteq Q$
has exterior selection sizes $k-|I|$ and $k-|J|$. It is feasible exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:st:eq:boolean}
 0\le |I|+|J|-k\le\nu(H[I,J]).
\end{equation}
Indeed a witnessing matching must use exactly $|I|+|J|-k$ internal edges.
Conversely, any internal matching of that size extends over the selected
exterior vertices because the two exterior blocks are complete. Thus
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:st:eq:coeff}
 \gamma_k=\sum_{I\subseteq P,\,J\subseteq Q}
 \mathbf1_{\eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:boolean}}\,u_Iv_J\,
 e_{k-|I|}(X)e_{k-|J|}(Y),
\end{equation}
with out-of-range elementary symmetric sums zero. This is a Boolean
support identity, not a count of internal matching witnesses.

At degree five, divide all left activities by $e_1(X)$ and all right
activities by $e_1(Y)$. Every coefficient is scaled by a positive $k$th
power, preserving the Newton signs. Now $e_1(X)=e_1(Y)=1$; write
\[
 M=e_2(X),\qquad N=e_3(X),\qquad S=e_2(Y).
\]
The elementary identities
\begin{align}
 e_1(x)^2-2e_2(x)&=\sum_i x_i^2,\nonumber\\
 2e_2(x)^2-3e_1(x)e_3(x)
 &=2\sum_{i<j}x_i^2x_j^2+
   \sum_{i<j<k}x_ix_jx_k(x_i+x_j+x_k)\label{mrn:v:st:eq:moment}
\end{align}
give the strict bounds
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:st:eq:strictdomain}
 0<2M<1,\qquad 0<2S<1,\qquad 0<\frac{3N}{2M^2}<1.
\end{equation}
Hence every finite degree-five instance is represented by positive $V,W,Z$:
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:st:eq:substitution}
 M=\frac{V}{2(1+V)},\quad S=\frac{W}{2(1+W)},\quad
 N=\frac{V^2Z}{6(1+V)^2(1+Z)}.
\end{equation}
Explicitly, $V=2M/(1-2M)$, $W=2S/(1-2S)$, and $Z=z/(1-z)$ for
$z=3N/(2M^2)$. Core activities remain arbitrary positive variables.

\subsection{Exact middle gap certificates}
Let $g_0,\ldots,g_5$ denote the normalized coefficients reconstructed by
\eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:coeff}. At degree five the middle inequalities are
\[
 g_2^2\ge2g_1g_3,\qquad g_3^2\ge2g_2g_4.
\]
The coefficient of $N$ in $g_3$ is always
$E=v_{Q_0}v_{Q_1}v_{Q_2}$, while $g_1,g_2$ do not depend on $N$.
Thus the first gap is nonincreasing in $N$. Replace $N$ by the upper bound
$2M^2/3$, and then substitute $M,S$ from \eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:substitution}.
Multiplying the resulting boundary gap by $(1+V)^2(1+W)^2$ gives a
rational-coefficient polynomial $F_{H,2}$.
For the second gap use \eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:substitution} exactly and multiply by
$(1+V)^4(1+W)^2(1+Z)^2$, obtaining $F_{H,3}$.
All denominator multipliers are positive. The variable order is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:st:eq:variables}
 \boldsymbol{x}=(u_{P_0},u_{P_1},v_{Q_0},v_{Q_1},v_{Q_2},V,W,Z);
\end{equation}
$F_{H,2}$ is independent of $Z$.

Encode $H$ by a six-bit mask with bit $3i+j$ representing $P_iQ_j$.
Independent row and column permutations give 13 core orbits. Their least
masks are
\[
 0,1,3,7,9,10,11,14,15,27,29,31,63.
\]
Relabeling only permutes the independent core activities, so one pair of
certificates per orbit suffices. For every representative and $k=2,3$ the
supplied data give the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:st:eq:certificate}
 F_{H,k}(\boldsymbol{x})=
 \sum_\ell\lambda_\ell\boldsymbol{x}^{m_\ell}
       (r_\ell\boldsymbol{x}^{a_\ell}-\boldsymbol{x}^{b_\ell})^2
       +R_{H,k}(\boldsymbol{x}).
\end{equation}
Here $\lambda_\ell,r_\ell$ are positive rationals, all exponent vectors are
nonnegative integral, and every coefficient of $R_{H,k}$ is nonnegative.
Every remainder is nonzero. Since all variables in \eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:variables}
are positive, \eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:certificate} is strictly positive. This proves both
strict middle inequalities; monotonicity transfers the first from the
boundary back to the actual $N$.

\begin{center}
\small\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.08}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrr@{}}\toprule
Core mask&Orbit size&Squares for $k=2$&Squares for $k=3$&Positive remainder terms\\\midrule
0&1&24&46&708\\
1&6&35&46&1443\\
3&6&35&44&1981\\
7&2&38&44&2063\\
9&3&23&44&1846\\
10&6&23&44&2827\\
11&12&23&44&2929\\
14&6&23&44&3151\\
15&6&23&44&3151\\
27&3&23&44&3037\\
29&6&23&44&3394\\
31&6&23&44&3394\\
63&1&23&44&3394\\
\bottomrule\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The 26 certificates contain 915 square terms and reconstruct 33,318
positive remainder terms. Both checkers reconstruct the target polynomial,
subtract every expanded square, and inspect all resulting coefficients,
including any monomials absent from the target. The remainder is not
trusted as prelisted data. One checker uses the core-rank criterion;
the other reconstructs selected full subgraphs and applies Hall's condition,
with a distinct packed-exponent rational implementation.
Both verify all 64 masks, all 13 orbits, and all target/remainder hashes.
The independent implementation also verifies 384 coefficient relabelings
and 52 rational substitution identities.
A floating-point linear program found candidate squares, but every accepted
coefficient and identity is checked exactly. No optimizer is needed for
replay, and no numerical solver status is a proof input.

\subsection{The outer gaps and degree drops}\label{mrn:v:st:sec:outer}
For the first gap, take the compatibility graph on positive-weight edges,
joining two when their physical endpoints are disjoint. Its clique number
is five. Assign product endpoint activities as vertex masses. Merging two
nonadjacent masses toward the vertex with larger weighted neighbor sum
preserves total mass and does not decrease the edge sum. After finitely
many merges the support is a clique of at most five vertices, whose
weighted edge sum is at most $2\gamma_1^2/5$ by Cauchy's inequality.
The initial sum counts matching witnesses. The positive complete block
$P\times Y$ contains a $K_{2,2}$, giving a support with two positive
witnesses. Consequently $\gamma_2<2\gamma_1^2/5$.
\mnote{This is the Motzkin--Straus argument of \prp's Theorem~180.2
(Part~XI, \path{hb:thm:firstidentity}), printed in Part~I as second routes of
sources~74 and~72 (Sections~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:firstgap}
and~\ref{mrn:src:72}).}


For the last gap, use unnormalized elementary sums
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 (A,B)&=(e_1(P),e_2(P)),& (C,D,E)&=(e_1(Q),e_2(Q),e_3(Q)),\\
 (L,M,N)&=(e_1(X),e_2(X),e_3(X)),& (R,S)&=(e_1(Y),e_2(Y)).
 \end{aligned}
\]
All denominators below are positive. For nonempty $H$, formula
\eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:coeff} gives
\[
 \gamma_4=AENR+BDMS+BEMR,\qquad \gamma_5=BENS.
\]
These are independent of the nonempty core mask, whereas $\gamma_3$ can
only increase when the core is completed. For the complete core, set
\[
 x=\frac{AN}{BM},\quad y=\frac{DS}{ER},\quad
 b=\frac B{A^2}\le\frac14,\quad c=\frac{CE}{D^2}\le\frac13,
 \quad h=\frac{LN}{M^2}\le\frac23,\quad s=\frac S{R^2}\le\frac12.
\]
The core bounds are squared-difference identities, and the exterior bounds
follow from \eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:moment}. The complete-core computation
\cite[Section 3]{b:c09} is
\begin{align}
 \frac{4\gamma_4^2-10\gamma_3\gamma_5}{B^2E^2M^2R^2}
 &=4(x+y+1)^2\nonumber\\
 &\quad-10(xy+xs+chy^2+hy+hs+bsx^2)\nonumber\\
 &\ge(x-y)^2+\frac74x^2+\frac79y^2+3x+\frac43y+\frac23>0.
 \label{mrn:v:st:eq:last}
\end{align}
It therefore holds for every nonempty $H$. For $H=\varnothing$, the same
normalized gap equals
\[
 4(x+y)^2-10(xy+chy^2+bsx^2)
 \ge(x-y)^2+\tfrac74x^2+\tfrac79y^2>0.
\]
Thus every degree-five gap is strict. For $d\le4$, use the separately
approved rank-four theorem on the active graph; padded order-five
inequalities are not substituted for its stronger normalization.
This completes the proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:st:thm:main}.

\mnote{\cite{b:c09} is the batch-73 archive
\path{complete-two-by-three-result.zip}, which is not printed. Its Section~3
is the complete-core computation~\eqref{mrn:v:st:eq:last}; for the complete
core the graph is source~75's $H_{2,3;n,m}$, whose gaps are strict by
Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:fh:thm:classification}.}

\subsection{Stable monomers for empty and star cores}
There is a stronger ordinary result for 18 of the 64 masks.
For a graph on physical vertex set $V(G)$, define its signed support monomer
polynomial by
\[
 \mu(\boldsymbol{z})=\sum_{(S,T)\text{ feasible}}
 (-1)^{|S|}u^Sv^T\prod_{a\notin S\cup T}z_a.
\]
A real polynomial is real stable if it is nonzero whenever every variable
lies in $\HH=\{z:\operatorname{Im}z>0\}$.
\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:v:st:thm:star}
If $\nu(H)\le1$, then $\mu$ is real stable for arbitrary independent
nonnegative vertex activities. Hence $\Gamma$ has only negative real zeros
and is ULC at its actual surviving degree.
\end{theorem}
\mnote{By \prp's Corollary~94.3 (Part~VI, \path{ade:mat:cor:block-family})
the empty core and every star core except one are already covered: a star at a
vertex of $P$ with one or three neighbours in $Q$, and every star at a vertex
of $Q$, makes the graph a vertex sum of complete bipartite graphs and edges.
The remaining case, a star at a vertex of $P$ with exactly two neighbours in
$Q$, needs the exceptional two-neighbour seed below; that seed is new.}


\paragraph{Seed transfer.}
For a fixed head side $Q$, adjoin a private dummy $d_j$ for each head slot.
The transversal matroid on tails and dummies has a basis for each endpoint
support, with dummies encoding unused heads. If its basis polynomial
$B(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{d})$ is stable, then for positive activities
\[
 \mu(\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b})=
 \Bigl(\prod_{\text{tails }i}a_i\Bigr)
 \Bigl(\prod_{\text{heads }j}v_j\Bigr)
 B\bigl(x_i=-u_i/a_i,\ d_j=b_j/v_j\bigr)
\]
is stable, since all transformed arguments stay in $\HH$.
Limits give zero activities, and the all-unused monomial excludes the zero
polynomial. A complete bipartite graph has a uniform dummy matroid, with
stable elementary symmetric basis polynomial. An exceptional tail with a
singleton neighborhood is parallel to the corresponding private dummy and
is handled by a nonnegative linear substitution in that elementary polynomial.

\mnote{The seed transfer is also the seed construction of the batch-73
archive \path{bipartite-and-pendant-support-result.zip}, which concerns
directed endpoint supports and is placed with the batch's directed-support
manuscripts, not in this report; the shipped
\path{78-two-three-star-proof-notes-STAR_CORE_REAL_ROOTEDNESS.md} calls it
``the seed part of the previously approved pendant-head theorem''.}

\paragraph{The exceptional two-neighbor seed.}
Suppose there are three head slots, any number of universal tails $X$, and
one exceptional tail $p$ adjacent to heads 1 and 2. The rank-three dummy
matroid has every three-subset as a basis except $\{p,d_1,d_2\}$.
Put $L_0=\{p,d_1,d_2\}$ and $O=X\sqcup\{d_3\}$, with $|O|=m\ge1$.
Its basis polynomial is $e_3(L_0\sqcup O)-pd_1d_2$, symmetric separately
in the two groups. Its two-block diagonal is
\[
 y\left(3mx^2+3\binom m2xy+\binom m3y^2\right).
\]
The quadratic has nonnegative coefficients and discriminant
\[
 9\binom m2^2-12m\binom m3=\frac{m^2(m-1)(m+7)}4\ge0.
\]
It factors into linear forms with nonnegative coefficients, including the
cases $m=1,2$. The diagonal is stable, and polarization with degree bounds
$(3,m)$ recovers the basis polynomial. Stability follows from
Borcea--Br\"and\'en \cite[Proposition 2.4]{b:BBi}. Empty, singleton and universal
exceptional neighborhoods use the elementary seeds above.

\paragraph{Gluing and the star cases.}
For two relations meeting only at one vertex, use separate unused-vertex
monomers $a,b$. Their stable product is multiaffine in these variables,
say $f=Aab+Ba+Cb+D$. The exact physical gluing map is
\[
 ab\mapsto z,\quad a\mapsto1,\quad b\mapsto1,\quad1\mapsto0,
 \qquad Az+B+C=\left.\partial_t f(t,t)\right|_{t=z/2}.
\]
It preserves stability by diagonal substitution, differentiation and
positive scaling. It also counts supports exactly: if the shared vertex is
used, the imbalance of the other selected tail/head endpoints in each piece
uniquely determines which piece contains its matched edge. It cannot be
used in both pieces.
\mnote{The gluing identity is \prp's Lemma~93.1 (Part~VI,
\path{ade:mat:lem:vertex-sum}), with $\Phi_G=z_vA_1A_2+B_1A_2+A_1B_2$, and its
stability is \prp's Theorem~93.2 (Lemma~\ref{mrn:w:sb:lem:gluing} here).}


A nonempty bipartite graph of matching number one is a star. If its center
is $p\in P$, glue the complete piece on $P\sqcup Y$ to the piece on
$\{p\}\sqcup X\sqcup Q$. The latter has universal tails $X$ and the one
exceptional tail just analyzed. If its center is $q\in Q$, glue the
complete piece on $X\sqcup Q$ to the piece on $P\sqcup Y\sqcup\{q\}$.
After interchanging shores, the latter has two head slots and only
singleton or universal neighborhoods. The empty core is a disjoint
product. This proves monomer stability in every case.
Finally, diagonal specialization gives
$\mu(x,\ldots,x)=x^{|V(G)|}\Gamma(-x^{-2})$, so all zeros of $\Gamma$ are
negative real. Newton's inequalities use its actual surviving degree.

\subsection{Verification and boundaries}
The all-mask ULC theorem is computer-assisted through its 26 finite rational
identities after an ordinary unbounded-population reduction. The star-core
stability theorem is an ordinary proof. Independent additional checks
verify all 64 Boolean core kernels, 2,304 weighted Hall cases, 216 formal
gluing identities, and 714 exceptional-seed triples. These supplement the
proofs and are not finite-population assumptions.

The full core need not be real-rooted: with ten vertices in each exterior
block, core activities 100 and exterior activities 1, the scaled polynomial
\[
 \Gamma(t/100)=1+650t+39780t^2+143170t^3+53475t^4+5400t^5
\]
has negative discriminant and one nonreal conjugate pair
\cite[Section 5]{b:c09}. Thus Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:st:thm:star} is not silently
extended to the matching-two cores.
\mnote{\cite{b:c09} again. Its example is the sibling, at $n=m=10$, of
source~75's rank-five example at $n=m=9$ (Section~\ref{mrn:src:75}); the same
polynomial appears in the batch-73 archive
\path{weighted-bipartite-rank-five-result.zip}, which is not printed.}

Missing exterior edges, arbitrary orientations and independent edge
activities remain outside the conclusions. The general two-tail/three-head
role-cover question is still open. No proof-assistant formalization,
literature-wide priority claim or external publication is asserted.
\mnote{The role-cover question concerns directed endpoint supports (\prp's
Part~XII); it stays open here (Section~\ref{mrn:v:sec:questions}). Source~78's
delivered README, which is not shipped, says that general rank-five graphs
remain open; source~74 settles weighted rank five
(Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive}).}

%% ======================== source 79 ========================
\section{A two-element Lorentzian deformation of every matroid basis polynomial (source~79)}\label{mrn:src:79}
\paragraph{Abstract of source~79, as delivered.} We prove a homogeneous Lorentzian deformation associated with two distinguished elements of an arbitrary finite matroid. All original element variables remain separate; three finite populations of nonnegative weights enter as parameters. Principal extensions supply a quadratic-line seed, while matroid union proves the required support exchange property. Contraction reduces the remaining Hessian verification to ranks zero through four. No representability or spanning hypothesis is needed. A transversal specialization retains every selected-tail variable in bipartite endpoint-support polynomials with a two-tail vertex cover. Its diagonal consequence gives actual-degree ultra-log-concavity for all independently vertex-weighted bipartite graphs of matching rank at most five. The proof is ordinary; exact finite checks are supplied only as corroboration.
\mnote{Source~79 is batch-73 manuscript~02,
\path{two-element-matroid-lorentzian-result.zip} (title page ``Research
note''). It cites no repository source. Its graph statements are Part~I's:
Corollary~\ref{mrn:v:te:cor:graph} is source~74's left marginal
(Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main}) with the private variables of the right
cover part kept as variables of their own, and
Corollary~\ref{mrn:v:te:cor:rankfive} is source~74's
Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive}. New as a statement is
Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:te:thm:main} for every finite matroid. Source~80's
Appendix~A proves it in one page by matroid union, with the six basis cases of
source~74's lift (Section~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:fulllift}); that proof is printed
first, in Section~\ref{mrn:v:tt:sec:unionappendix}, and source~79's own proof
(a principal-line seed and contraction to ranks at most four) follows as a
second route. Its predecessor, the batch-73 archive
\path{weighted-bipartite-rank-five-result.zip} (manuscript~03), is not printed:
it proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main} and
Corollary~\ref{mrn:w:rf:cor:rankfive} by a third route, an explicit positive
semidefinite decomposition, from the same principal-line seed, which is the
limit of source~74's generic-line model (Section~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:line}) as
its number of line elements grows.}

\subsection{Statement and conventions}
Let $M$ be a finite rank-$q$ matroid on $E\sqcup\{A,B\}$, with $A\ne B$. Loops and parallel distinguished elements are allowed. For $I\subseteq E$, write $w^I=\prod_{e\in I}w_e$. Define five Boolean basis-family polynomials:
\begin{align*}
 U_0&=\sum_{I\subseteq E:\ I\text{ a basis of }M}w^I,&
 U_A&=\sum_{I\subseteq E:\ I\cup\{A\}\text{ a basis}}w^I,\\
 U_B&=\sum_{I\subseteq E:\ I\cup\{B\}\text{ a basis}}w^I,&
 V&=\sum_{I\subseteq E:\ I\cup\{A,B\}\text{ a basis}}w^I.
\end{align*}
The polynomial $U$ counts the union of the $U_A$ and $U_B$ families, each old subset once. Thus $U$ is generally not $U_A+U_B$. The degrees of $(U_0,U_A,U_B,U,V)$ are $(q,q-1,q-1,q-1,q-2)$; negative-cardinality families are zero. In particular, $U_0$ may vanish when $E$ does not span $M$: it always counts full rank-$q$ bases, not bases of a lower-rank restriction.

Take three finite populations of nonnegative real weights, called private-$A$, private-$B$, and common. Let $L_0,L_1,H$ be their sums, and let $S$ be the second elementary symmetric sum of the common population. Put
\begin{align}
 R_0&=L_0+H, &R_1&=L_1+H,\nonumber\\
 \mu&=L_0L_1+H(L_0+L_1)+S,&
 \bar\mu&=L_0L_1+H(L_0+L_1)+H^2/2,\label{mrn:v:te:eq:params}\\
 W&=L_1U_A+L_0U_B+HU.\nonumber
\end{align}
All population weights are parameters. The variables are $\mathbf w,a,b,z$.
\begin{theorem}\label{mrn:v:te:thm:main}
For every such finite matroid and populations, the degree-$(q+2)$ polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:te:eq:main}
 F_M=z^2U_0+z(aR_0+bR_1)U_0+ab\mu U_0
       +z^2(aU_A+bU_B)+abzW+abz^2V
\end{equation}
is Lorentzian.
\end{theorem}
The polynomial is nonzero: its $z^2$ coefficient is the full basis polynomial
$U_0+aU_A+bU_B+abV$. The theorem makes no joint Lorentzian assertion in the population parameters.

We use the following precise form of Lorentzian theory \cite{b:BH}. A nonnegative homogeneous polynomial of degree $d\ge2$ is Lorentzian exactly when its exponent support is $M$-convex and every order-$(d-2)$ partial derivative has a Hessian with at most one positive eigenvalue. Here $M$-convexity means symmetric unit exchange of exponent vectors. Nonnegative linear substitutions, differentiation, products, and coefficient limits preserve Lorentzianity (allowing zero). Matroid basis polynomials are Lorentzian. These are Theorems 2.10, 2.25 and 3.10 and Corollary 2.32 of \cite{b:BH}; nonnegative directional differentiation follows by substituting $s_i\mapsto s_i+r_i y$, differentiating in $y$, and setting $y=0$.
\subsection{A direct all-matroid extension of the intake lift (source~80, Appendix~A)}\label{mrn:v:tt:sec:unionappendix}
\mnote{Printed here, from source~80's Appendix~A, as the first proof of
Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:te:thm:main}. The intake is source~74 at commit
\texttt{1512ef835}, and its \texttt{full\_lift.tex} is
Section~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:fulllift}; the bundled earlier note \cite{b:m79} is
source~79. Source~80 restates the theorem in its own letters: $h_1,\dots,h_m$
are the common weights, and $W$ of~\eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:params} is written out
in~\eqref{mrn:v:tt:eq:twoelement}.}

This short proof adapts the rank-compression construction and six basis cases of the intake's \texttt{full\_lift.tex} \cite{b:m74}. Matroid union removes its transversal-seed requirement. It supplies a shorter proof of the unrestricted two-element theorem in the bundled earlier note \cite{b:m79}; that release remains unchanged.

Let $M$ be any rank-$q$ matroid on $E\sqcup\{A,B\}$. In old variables $\mathbf w$, let $U_0$ count old full bases, $U_A,U_B$ old subsets completed to a basis by $A,B$, respectively, $U$ the Boolean union of those two families, and $V$ the old $(q-2)$-subsets $I$ for which $I\cup\{A,B\}$ is a basis. Negative-cardinality families vanish. No old-spanning hypothesis is imposed; $U_0$ can be zero.

For nonnegative masses $L_0,L_1$ and common weights $h_1,\ldots,h_m$, put
\[
 H=\sum_jh_j,\quad S=\sum_{j<k}h_jh_k,\quad
 R_i=L_i+H,\quad \mu=L_0L_1+H(L_0+L_1)+S.
\]
The degree-$(q+2)$ polynomial
\begin{align}\label{mrn:v:tt:eq:twoelement}
 F={}&z^2U_0+z(aR_0+bR_1)U_0+ab\mu U_0+z^2(aU_A+bU_B)\nonumber\\
 &+abz(L_1U_A+L_0U_B+HU)+abz^2V
\end{align}
is Lorentzian for every finite $M$, including loops and dependent distinguished elements.

To prove it, adjoin plane elements $P_1,P_2,Z_1,\ldots,Z_m$. Let $M_1$ extend $M$ by loops on the plane. On the same ground let $M_2$ be the rank-two transversal matroid with two slots: $A,P_1$ use only slot 1; $B,P_2$ use only slot 2; each $Z_j$ uses either; old $E$ elements are loops. Their union $K=M_1\vee M_2$ is a matroid \cite[Section 2]{b:BK}. Its rank is $q+2$: any $M$ basis together with $P_1,P_2$ witnesses the upper bound given by the sum of the two ranks.

A $K$ basis partitions into full bases of the two factors. Old elements must enter $M_1$ and plane elements $M_2$. If $k$ distinguished and $\ell$ plane elements are selected, exactly $k+\ell-2$ distinguished elements enter $M_1$. A basis is counted once, not once per partition.

Assume $\mu>0$. Keep $\mathbf w,a,b$, substitute
\[
 P_1=L_1z/\mu,\qquad P_2=L_0z/\mu,\qquad Z_j=h_jz/\mu,
\]
and multiply the basis polynomial by $\mu$. Every distinct plane pair fills the two auxiliary slots, and its total specialized weight is
\[
 (z^2/\mu^2)\bigl[L_0L_1+(L_0+L_1)H+S\bigr]=z^2/\mu.
\]
There are exactly six possible $(k,\ell)$ classes, with contributions
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}cl@{}}\toprule
$(k,\ell)$&Contribution after multiplication by $\mu$\\\midrule
$(0,2)$&$z^2U_0$\\
$(1,1)$&$z(aR_0+bR_1)U_0$\\
$(1,2)$&$z^2(aU_A+bU_B)$\\
$(2,0)$&$ab\mu U_0$\\
$(2,1)$&$abz(L_1U_A+L_0U_B+HU)$\\
$(2,2)$&$abz^2V$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the sole ambiguous class $(2,1)$, $P_1$ forces $B$ into the second factor and $A$ into the first, giving $L_1U_A$; $P_2$ gives $L_0U_B$. A common plane element permits either allocation, so its old subset belongs to the Boolean union $U$ and is still one basis. This explains the common coefficient without witness multiplicity. The other classes follow immediately from the forced factor cardinalities and plane-pair sum.

The six terms sum to \eqref{mrn:v:tt:eq:twoelement}. Matroid-basis Lorentzianity and nonnegative linear substitution prove the claim. When $\mu=0$, replace both private masses by $L_i+\varepsilon$, apply the positive case, and take a coefficient limit as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. The result is nonzero because the coefficient of $z^2$ is the full basis polynomial $U_0+aU_A+bU_B+abV$. Thus no representation, old-spanning assumption, principal-extension limit, or low-rank Hessian induction is needed in this attributed union proof.
\mnote{Second proof of Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:te:thm:main}: source~79's own,
Sections~\ref{mrn:v:te:sec:constructions}--\ref{mrn:v:te:sec:padding}.}


\subsection{Two matroid constructions}\label{mrn:v:te:sec:constructions}
We first prove Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:te:thm:main} when $E$ spans $M$; Section~\ref{mrn:v:te:sec:padding} removes this temporary hypothesis. No representation over a field is used.

\subsubsection{A principal-line seed}
Write
\[
 \phi(s)=s_As_B+s_As_U+s_Bs_U+s_U^2/2,
 \qquad J=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&1\\1&0&1\\1&1&1\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad \phi(s)=\tfrac12s^TJs.
\]
\begin{lemma}\label{mrn:v:te:lem:seed}
The homogeneous degree-$q$ polynomial
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:te:eq:seed}
 \mathcal S_M(\mathbf w,s)=U_0+s_AU_A+s_BU_B+s_UU+\phi(s)V
\end{equation}
is Lorentzian.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Add $m$ elements freely on the flat $\cl_M\{A,B\}$. For original $X$ and a set $Y$ of new elements, the extension rank is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:te:eq:principal}
 r_+(X\cup Y)=\min\{r_M(X\cup\{A,B\}),\ r_M(X)+|Y|\}.
\end{equation}
This is the free-on-flat extension formula of Bonin--Kung \cite[Lemma 3.1]{b:BK}. For an old $(q-1)$-set, a single new element completes a basis exactly when $A$ or $B$ does. For an old $(q-2)$-set, two new elements, or either distinguished element together with one new element, complete a basis exactly when $A,B$ do. Three line elements are dependent, since their flat has rank at most two. The extension's basis polynomial is therefore
\[
 U_0+s_AU_A+s_BU_B+\Bigl(\sum_i h_i\Bigr)U
 +\Bigl[s_As_B+(s_A+s_B)\sum_i h_i+\sum_{i<j}h_ih_j\Bigr]V.
\]
Set $h_i=s_U/m$. Matroid-basis Lorentzianity and nonnegative substitution apply for every $m$; the coefficient of $s_U^2V$ tends to $1/2$. Closedness proves \eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:seed}, including loop and parallel cases.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{Matroid union supplies the support}
Let $Y$ index all population members, $n=|Y|$, and introduce dummy elements $D=\{d_y:y\in Y\}$. On the common ground set $E\sqcup\{A,B\}\sqcup D$, let $M_1$ be $M$ extended by loops on $D$. Let $M_2$ be the rank-$n$ transversal matroid with slots $Y$: each $d_y$ is adjacent only to $y$; $A,B$ have the private/common incidences; all elements of $E$ are loops. Use their matroid union $K=M_1\vee M_2$, whose independent sets are unions of independent sets in the two factors \cite[Section 2]{b:BK}.

An old basis together with all dummies shows $r(K)=q+n$. Every basis of $K$ partitions into a full $M_1$ basis and a full $M_2$ basis: make a union decomposition disjoint, then its cardinality forces both factor ranks. Old elements belong to the first factor and dummies to the second. If $r$ dummies are omitted, exactly $r$ distinguished elements must be assigned to the second factor. Consequently $r\le2$, with the following complete cases:
\begin{itemize}\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
\item $r=0$: the original basis polynomial $U_0+aU_A+bU_B+abV$;
\item $r=1$, one distinguished element selected: its permitted incidence times $U_0$;
\item $r=1$, both selected: $U_B$ for a private-$A$ head, $U_A$ for a private-$B$ head, and $U$ for a common head;
\item $r=2$: $U_0$, exactly when the two omitted heads match to $A,B$.
\end{itemize}
A basis is counted once regardless of its possible partitions. This explains the Boolean union in the common-head case.

For positive population weights $\omega_y$, substitute $d_y=z/\omega_y$ in the basis polynomial and multiply by $\prod_y\omega_y$. The result is
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:te:eq:union}
 G=z^{n-2}F_M.
\end{equation}
It is Lorentzian. The same assertion for $G$ at zero weights follows by the explicit coefficient limit. Its no-omission term $z^n(U_0+aU_A+bU_B+abV)$ is nonzero. The exponent supports of $G$ and $F_M$ differ by a fixed translation, which preserves symmetric exchange, even when $n<2$ and \eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:union} is read as a Laurent identity. Hence $\supp F_M$ is $M$-convex. We use only support translation here, not a claim that arbitrary monomial division preserves Lorentzianity.

\subsection{Contraction and the five small ranks}\label{mrn:v:te:sec:induction}
For an old nonloop $e$, restriction of each basis family to subsets containing $e$, followed by deletion of $e$, gives exactly its counterpart in $M/e$. This commutes with the Boolean union defining $U$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:te:eq:contract}
 \partial_{w_e}F_M=F_{M/e}.
\end{equation}
For an old loop the derivative is zero. The remaining old elements span the rank-$(q-1)$ contraction.

Induct on $q$, using the already established $M$-convex support. Every order-$q$ derivative involving an old variable follows from \eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:contract} and induction. The only remaining derivatives involve $a,b,z$, of respective maximum degrees $1,1,2$. They vanish for $q\ge5$. We check ranks zero through four explicitly. In boundary arguments, add two common heads of weight $\varepsilon>0$ and let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$; this makes $\mu,R_0,R_1$ positive whenever denominators require them.

\subsubsection{Rank zero}
Here $F_M=z^2+(aR_0+bR_1)z+ab\mu$. Its Hessian in $(a,b,z)$ is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}0&\mu&R_0\\\mu&0&R_1\\R_0&R_1&2\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad \det=2\mu(R_0R_1-\mu),\qquad R_0R_1-\mu=H^2-S\ge0.
\]
When $\mu>0$ the leading $2\times2$ block has one positive and one negative eigenvalue. The nonnegative determinant then excludes a second positive eigenvalue. Limits give the boundary.

\subsubsection{Rank one}
Write $u=\sum_{e\in E\text{ nonloop}}w_e$, $\alpha=U_A\in\{0,1\}$, $\beta=U_B\in\{0,1\}$, and
$W_0=L_1\alpha+L_0\beta+H\max(\alpha,\beta)$. Then $U_0=u$, $U=\max(\alpha,\beta)$, and $V=0$. First work in the single variable $u$: this is the same construction for a rank-one matroid with one old nonloop, so its support is $M$-convex by Section~\ref{mrn:v:te:sec:constructions}. Afterwards substitute the nonnegative sum for $u$.

The $u$ derivative is the rank-zero quadratic. The $a$ derivative has Hessian in $(u,z,b)$
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}0&R_0&\mu\\R_0&2\alpha&W_0\\\mu&W_0&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad \det=2\mu(R_0W_0-\alpha\mu),
\]
where the Boolean identity
\[
 R_0W_0-\alpha\mu
 =\alpha(H^2-S)+(1-\alpha)\beta R_0^2+\alpha\beta R_0L_0\ge0
\]
proves the signature as above. The $b$ case is symmetric. The $z$ derivative has leading $(u,z)$ Hessian block $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&2\\2&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ and Schur complement
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}-2R_0\alpha&-H\alpha\beta\\-H\alpha\beta&-2R_1\beta\end{pmatrix}\preceq0,
\]
since $4R_0R_1\ge H^2$. Sylvester inertia and limits finish rank one.

\subsubsection{Rank two}
The seed $\mathcal S_M$ is now quadratic and $V\in\{0,1\}$. Put $m=(L_1,L_0,H)^T$, so $\phi(m)=\bar\mu$, $e_A^TJm=R_0$ and $e_B^TJm=R_1$. Direct expansion gives all nonzero remaining second derivatives:
\begin{align}
 \partial_z^2F_M&=2\mathcal S_M\bigl(s=(a,b,0)\bigr),\nonumber\\
 \partial_a\partial_bF_M
   &=\mu\mathcal S_M\bigl(s=mz/\mu\bigr)
        -\frac{\bar\mu-\mu}{\mu}z^2V,\label{mrn:v:te:eq:rank2}\\
 \partial_a\partial_zF_M
   &=R_0\mathcal S_M\bigl(s=(2e_Az+mb)/R_0\bigr)
        -\frac{\bar\mu}{R_0}b^2V,\nonumber\\
 \partial_b\partial_zF_M
   &=R_1\mathcal S_M\bigl(s=(2e_Bz+ma)/R_1\bigr)
        -\frac{\bar\mu}{R_1}a^2V.\nonumber
\end{align}
Each seed substitution is nonnegative linear. Also $\bar\mu-\mu=H^2/2-S\ge0$. Thus each subtraction removes a positive semidefinite diagonal matrix from a quadratic Hessian and cannot increase its positive index. The actual derivatives have nonnegative coefficients by \eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:main}. Perturbation removes zero denominators; repeated $a$ or $b$ derivatives vanish.

\subsubsection{Ranks three and four}
At rank three the remaining derivatives are
\[
 \partial_a\partial_z^2F_M=2(U_A+bV),\qquad
 \partial_b\partial_z^2F_M=2(U_B+aV),\qquad
 \partial_a\partial_b\partial_zF_M=W+2zV.
\]
The first is $2\partial_{s_A}\mathcal S_M$ at $s_A=s_U=0,s_B=b$; the second is symmetric. For the third, the nonnegative directional derivative $D_m\mathcal S_M$ equals $W+(m^TJs)V$. If $m\ne0$, choose $r\ge0$ with $m^TJr=2$ and substitute $s=rz$. Such $r$ exists because $Jm$ has a positive coordinate. If $m=0$, use the product $2zV$, with $V$ a rank-one contraction basis polynomial or zero.

At rank four only $\partial_a\partial_b\partial_z^2F_M=2V$ remains. When $A,B$ are independent, $V$ is the rank-two basis polynomial of $M/\{A,B\}$; otherwise it is zero. All these quadratics have the required signature. Higher ranks have no further special-variable derivative. This completes the induction with spanning old ground.

\subsection{Removing the spanning hypothesis}\label{mrn:v:te:sec:padding}
For each distinguished nonloop, add an old parallel copy, $A'$ or $B'$; add nothing for a distinguished loop. Parallel extension is the rank-one instance of \eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:principal}. The extended matroid $\widetilde M$ has the same rank, and its enlarged old ground spans: every distinguished nonloop lies in the closure of its old copy, and loops lie in the closure of the empty set.

Apply the proved theorem to $\widetilde M$ and set the added copy variables to zero. Restriction to the original ground recovers all five full-basis families, including their Boolean union. The resulting polynomial is exactly \eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:main}; nonnegative specialization preserves Lorentzianity. It is nonzero because its $z^2$ coefficient is the basis polynomial of $M$. This proves Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:te:thm:main} without any old-spanning or representability assumption.

\subsection{Bipartite endpoint supports}
Let a finite bipartite graph have disjoint shores $P\sqcup X$ and $Q\sqcup Y$, where $P=\{p_0,p_1\}$, $|Q|=q$, and there is no $X$--$Y$ edge. A feasible endpoint support is a pair $(S,T)$ of equally sized shore subsets such that the induced bipartite graph has a perfect matching. Each pair is counted once. Give right vertices independent nonnegative activities $v_j$; keep all left selected variables $x_i$ separate.
\begin{corollary}\label{mrn:v:te:cor:graph}
The following homogeneous degree-$(q+2)$ polynomial is Lorentzian:
\begin{equation}\label{mrn:v:te:eq:graph}
 \mathcal F_G(\mathbf x,z,\mathbf z_Q)=
 \sum_{(S,T)\text{ feasible}}
   \Bigl(\prod_{j\in T}v_j\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{i\in S}x_i\Bigr)
   z^{2-|T\cap Y|}\prod_{j\in Q\setminus T}z_j.
\end{equation}
Independent nonnegative left activities may be inserted by scaling each $x_i$.
\end{corollary}
\mnote{The statement is source~74's left marginal
(Theorem~\ref{mrn:w:rf:thm:main}) with the private elements of the right cover
part kept as variables $z_j$ instead of $s/v_b$: the basis polynomial of the
transversal matroid of Section~\ref{mrn:w:rf:sec:fulllift} gives it by the same
nonnegative substitution. The proof below derives it from
Theorem~\ref{mrn:v:te:thm:main} instead.}

\begin{proof}
Use the transversal matroid with slots $Q$, old elements $X$ and one private dummy for each slot, and distinguished elements representing $p_0,p_1$. Its rank is $q$, and the old dummies span. Discard isolated $Y$ vertices; classify the others by their neighborhood in $P$, obtaining exactly the three populations. For positive $v_j$ on $Q$, set each dummy variable to $z_j/v_j$, set old $X$ variables to $x_i$, set $a=x_{p_0},b=x_{p_1}$, and multiply by $\prod_{j\in Q}v_j$. The support decomposition of \eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:main} gives \eqref{mrn:v:te:eq:graph}. A selected head set with one common $Y$ vertex uses the Boolean extension union, so multiple matching witnesses cause no overcount. Zero $Q$ activities follow by limits in the explicit endpoint formula. The empty support has coefficient one, excluding zero output.
\end{proof}

For clarity about normalization, scale $x_i=u_it$, set every $z_j=z$, and write the resulting polynomial as
\[
 z^{q+2}\Gamma(t/z)=\sum_k\gamma_k t^kz^{q+2-k}.
\]
